P24 mentions UK residency in the context of FSCS protection but is not a client-specific tax status record. No category A, B, C or E document records the MPAA status as not triggered.
    The report makes a specific client-level assertion — that the MPAA has not been triggered and the client does not have a lower annual allowance. This is a factually verifiable claim that is material to the advice: if the MPAA had been triggered, pension contribution relief and strategy would be affected. No supporting document in the case file corroborates this assertion. The fact find passages (P3, P4, P7, P8, P18) do not record any MPAA trigger event, flexi-access drawdown history, or annual allowance position. The Aviva illustrations (P22, P23) contain only a generic legislative statement about the standard allowance that is not applied to this client specifically. P24 references UK residency for FSCS purposes only. The [I] passages (P1, P2, P5) are the report under audit and cannot corroborate the report's own assertion. There is no contradiction between the report and the evidence — but there is also no corroborating evidence. The Scottish/rest-of-UK band guard is noted but is moot here because no tax band figure is stated at all. The generic legislative statement guard applies to the Aviva illustration text and means the allowance figures there are not client-specific assertions; they cannot serve as corroboration. The claim is therefore unsupported by the case file, which is a Potential Concern where tax treatment is asserted without a recorded tax position, particularly given the recommendation involves pension switching and the MPAA status is relevant to ongoing contribution capacity.

      - Monthly expenditure in current situation section (£2,986) differs from cashflow modelling input (£2,237/month pre-retirement) — a £749/month unexplained discrepancy within the report itself.
      - Savings figure: meeting summary [P13] states ~£17,000 in savings; fact find [P8] states £7,000; report does not state a savings figure explicitly but these two supporting documents conflict.
    Several material discrepancies exist between the report's income and expenditure figures and the supporting evidence.
    
    1. TONY'S CONTRIBUTION: The report states £600/month [I]. Both the fact find [P3] and the meeting notes [P6] record £800/month. This is a direct contradiction between the report and two corroborating evidence documents. It is not explained by gross/net differences or rounding.
    
    2. MORTGAGE PAYMENT IN CASHFLOW: The report's cashflow section uses £750/month for the mortgage. The fact find [P5] records the mortgage expenditure as £600/month. The meeting summary [P13] refers to overpaying by £250/month on what appears to be a lower base payment, which is consistent with £600 base rather than £750 total, though the meeting summary also notes the rate is about to change. These are not on the same basis: the fact find records the current payment, the cashflow may use a projected payment after the rate increase, but the report gives no explanation for this difference.
    
    3. TOTAL ASSETS: The report states £447,000. The fact find [P8] shows a client total of £587,000 including the potential inheritance of £150,000. If the report excludes the inheritance (a reasonable choice given its contingent nature), the remaining fact-find assets are house £430,000 + savings £7,000 = £437,000, still not £447,000. The £10,000 gap is unexplained. The potential inheritance is a contingent asset and its exclusion would be justifiable, but even setting that aside, the residual total does not reconcile.
    
    4. EXPENDITURE INTERNAL INCONSISTENCY: The report states current monthly expenditure of £2,986 in the fact-find narrative but uses £2,237/month in the cashflow model — a £749/month difference with no reconciliation. This is a self-contradiction in the report (flagged in the extraction's internalInconsistencies). One candidate explanation is that the £2,237 figure nets out Tony's £600 contribution and/or the mortgage, but even that does not reconcile arithmetically: £2,986 − £600 (Tony) − £750 (mortgage) = £1,636, not £2,237; or £2,986 − £749 = £2,237 exactly, suggesting the mortgage (£750≈£749 rounding) may have been excluded but Tony's contribution was not netted. No explanation is given in the report.
    
    5. STATE PENSION £230.21/WEEK: This figure appears only in the report and is not corroborated by any evidence document. Absence of corroboration is a finding under the check's stated scope.
    
    6. SAVINGS CONFLICT BETWEEN EVIDENCE DOCUMENTS: The meeting summary [P13] records ~£17,000 in savings while the fact find [P8] records £7,000. This is a conflict within the evidence itself; the report does not explicitly state a savings balance in the financial position section so it cannot be tested directly, but the supporting documents disagree — this is a data quality issue in the case file.
    
    The mortgage and Tony's contribution discrepancies affect the income/expenditure picture used to support the recommendation context. The total assets figure is materially wrong or unexplained. The cashflow modelling uses an expenditure figure £749/month lower than the stated total without explanation. These are material inconsistencies.

The valuation-date guard applies to the cash savings comparison to the extent that the fact find is dated July 2026; however, no document supports a value of £17,000 at any date, so the discrepancy stands rather than being explained by timing.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client expects to receive the Full State Pension of £230.21 per week (gross) from age 67, and this is a full entitlement. State pension age is stated as 67.
    File:     [P1] (ONS.md, category F) references '67 years' and 'State pension' but provides no figure for the weekly or annual amount. [P13] (Fact Find, category B) states 'Once her state pension kicks in at age 67, half of her retirement expenditure will be covered by this leaving the requirement of circa £12,000 per annum' — implying roughly £24,000 per annum / £461 per week if half of retirement expenditure (stated elsewhere as £2,000 net per month = £24,000 per annum), or approximately £12,000 per annum / £230.77 per week for the state pension itself. No HMRC State Pension forecast, government gateway statement or DWP forecast document is present in the pack. No passage corroborates the specific figure of £230.21 per week.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a specific state pension amount of £230.21 per week but no HMRC/DWP forecast or government gateway statement is present in the pack to corroborate this figure.
      - [P13] implies the state pension covers approximately half of £24,000 per annum retirement expenditure, suggesting ~£12,000 per annum / ~£230.77 per week — broadly consistent with £230.21 per week at £52 x £230.21 = £11,970.92 per annum, but this is a back-calculation from a narrative approximation, not a corroborating document.
      - The figure of £230.21 per week is not matched by any value in the retrieved passages; the nearest match is 67 (the state pension age), not the monetary amount.
    The report asserts a full State Pension entitlement of £230.21 per week from age 67. The state pension age of 67 is corroborated by [P1] (ONS.md). However, the specific monetary amount of £230.21 per week is not corroborated by any evidence document in the pack. No HMRC forecast, DWP letter, government gateway statement or equivalent is present. [P13] from the Fact Find (category B) alludes to the state pension covering approximately half of retirement expenditure of ~£2,000 per month net, implying ~£12,000 per annum or ~£230.77 per week — arithmetically close to the report's figure (£230.21 x 52 = £11,970.92) but this is a rough narrative reference, not a corroborating document, and cannot be treated as confirmation of the specific weekly figure. Per the check's own guidance, state pension entitlement is named explicitly and is often asserted without a forecast on file; the absence of a forecast is a Potential Concern. The state pension age is separately confirmed. The guard regarding state pension age versus retirement age does not apply here as the report states age 67 for state pension specifically. The cashflow model also references the state pension for Melanie [P6], further embedding the unverified figure in the advice. Severity is moderate: the amount is not implausible given the narrative in [P13], but the figure cannot be confirmed from the file and it is used in cashflow projections.

The nearest independent figure for the Quilter arrangement is c.£300,000 in fact find notes [P7] and £294,867.94 in [P3], both materially different from c.£260,000, creating an unexplained gap rather than corroboration.   [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report states: (1) Initial advice fee = £9,838.36, described as 3% of the amount invested; total transfer value stated as £67,945.44 (True Potential + Nest). (2) Monthly expenditure in 'Your current situation' = £2,986; monthly expenditure in cashflow modeller inputs = £2,237. (3) True Potential platform charge = 0.40% in the existing arrangements table; current annual recurring charge for True Potential = 0.98% in the switch charges table (includes 0.50% ongoing advice); like-for-like saving quoted as 0.59% (implying existing = 0.98%, new = 0.39%). (4) Nest existing arrangements table merges fund name and risk level as 'Nest Higher Risk Fund 8'; True Potential risk level listed separately as 6. (5) Ongoing service charge = 1% per annum; monetary equivalent stated as circa £3,181.08 for the first year based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'; 1% × £67,945.44 = £679.45; 1% × (£327,945 − £9,838) ≈ £3,181.
    File:     This requirement is assessed entirely from the report against itself. No supporting documents were searched and no external evidence is required or applicable. The derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values confirm: total of all three arrangements = £327,945.44; transfer value of the two arrangements being switched = £67,945.44; 3% × £67,945.44 = £2,038.36 (not £9,838.36); 3% × £327,945.44 ≈ £9,838.36 (matches the stated fee only if the PSO Quilter fund of £260,000 is included in the charge basis, which is never stated). NP1 ongoing charge of 0.39% on £327,946.15 = £1,278.99 (not consistent with £3,181.08 at 1%). 1% × (£327,945 − £9,838) ≈ £3,181 is arithmetically consistent with the stated £3,181.08 but relies on an unstated basis.
    Discrepancies:
      - (1) Initial advice fee arithmetic: 3% × £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, yet the report states £9,838.36. The fee matches 3% of ~£327,945 (all three arrangements including the PSO), but the report never states this combined value as the charge basis — leaving the stated fee irreconcilable with the stated transfer value.
      - (2) Monthly expenditure contradiction: 'Your current situation' states £2,986/month; cashflow modeller inputs use £2,237/month — a £749/month difference with no explanation given.
      - (3) True Potential charge presentation: existing arrangements table shows platform charge of 0.40%; switch charges assessment shows 0.98% as the current annual recurring charge (inclusive of 0.50% ongoing advice); the like-for-like comparison then uses 0.98% as the existing total while presenting 0.39% as the new equivalent — figures used inconsistently across sections without explicit reconciliation.
      - (4) Ongoing service monetary figure: 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45; 1% of £327,945.44 = £3,279.45; the stated £3,181.08 is only arithmetically consistent if 1% is applied to the combined total minus the initial fee (£327,945 − £9,838 ≈ £318,107), but this basis is never stated in the report.
      - (5) Nest risk level / fund name merged cell: the Nest table merges fund name ('Nest Higher Risk Fund') and risk level ('8') into a single cell, creating ambiguity as to whether 8 is a plan-level or fund-level risk rating; True Potential records risk level 6 in a separate dedicated row.
    Five internal inconsistencies are confirmed after applying the guards. (1) INITIAL ADVICE FEE — HIGHEST SEVERITY. The report states the initial fee is 3% of the amount invested and gives £9,838.36. The stated total transfer value is £67,945.44 (True Potential + Nest). 3% × £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, which contradicts £9,838.36 by approximately £7,800. The derived figures confirm that 3% × £327,945.44 (all three arrangements, including the £260,000 Quilter PSO) ≈ £9,838.36, which matches arithmetically — but the report never states that the Quilter PSO is included in the charge basis, and it is excluded from the 'total transfer value' of £67,945.44. The report therefore states a fee and a percentage basis that cannot be reconciled without an assumption the report never makes. This is a material, self-contradicting figure that drives the cost disclosure and the recommendation. No 'as at' date or different basis guard applies: both figures are stated as contemporaneous. (2) MONTHLY EXPENDITURE — HIGH SEVERITY. The report states £2,986/month in the client situation section and £2,237/month in the cashflow modeller inputs — a £749/month (25%) difference. No explanation is given. The cashflow model uses the lower figure, which would materially affect the projected surplus/deficit output. This is a direct self-contradiction on a figure used in the modelling. (3) TRUE POTENTIAL CHARGE PRESENTATION — HIGH SEVERITY. The platform charge of 0.40% in the existing arrangements table and the 0.98% in the switch charges table are not in themselves contradictory — 0.98% = 0.40% platform + 0.58% fund management (+ 0.50% ongoing advice depending on inclusion). However, the like-for-like comparison uses 0.98% as the existing total and 0.39% as the new equivalent. The existing total of 0.98% includes the 0.50% ongoing advice charge; if the new 0.39% also excludes ongoing advice (1% new ongoing excluded), then the comparison is correctly like-for-like. But the report does not state this explicitly, and the 0.40% platform figure in the existing arrangements section appears to under-represent the total charge by 0.58% of fund charges. The inconsistency in how the figures are presented across sections without reconciliation is a disclosure and data consistency issue. (4) ONGOING SERVICE MONETARY FIGURE — HIGH SEVERITY. 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45, which bears no resemblance to £3,181.08. The figure £3,181.08 is arithmetically consistent with 1% × (£327,945 − £9,838) ≈ £3,181, i.e. the combined fund value net of initial fee. However, the basis — that the ongoing charge is applied to all three arrangements including the PSO — is never stated. This is internally inconsistent and leaves the disclosed monetary amount unsupported by any stated calculation basis. (5) NEST RISK LEVEL FORMAT — LOW SEVERITY. The merged cell presentation for Nest ('Nest Higher Risk Fund 8') is a data quality / presentation issue creating ambiguity about whether '8' is a plan-level or fund-level risk rating. It does not contradict another stated figure but is an internal formatting inconsistency. The 'as at' date guard does not apply to any of these: all are contemporaneous figures within the same report. The basis guard applies partially to item (3) — the 0.40% and 0.98% are on different bases (platform only vs. total including fund charges and advice charge) — but the inconsistency in how the like-for-like table is presented is not fully resolved by this guard, because the report still uses 0.98% in one place and 0.40% in another without acknowledging the difference.

      - The stress-test result (funds exhausted at age 81) is asserted in the report but no cashflow modelling output document is in the file to corroborate it.
      - The baseline scenario outcome ('assets sufficient throughout lifetime') is asserted but has no independent modelling output in the file.
      - The cashflow expenditure assumption (£2,237) does not match the fact-find expenditure (£2,986) and no reconciliation or explanation is given — possible partial figures (e.g. net of Tony's £600 contribution and/or mortgage) but no basis is stated.
      - The ongoing service monetary figure of circa £3,181.08 stated as 1% p.a. does not reconcile with 1% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (= £679.45); it is only consistent with 1% of ~£327,945 (all arrangements including PSO) net of initial fee — but that combined basis is never stated.
      - The initial advice fee of £9,838.36 stated as 3% of amount invested does not reconcile with 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (= £2,038.36); it is consistent with 3% of ~£327,945 (all arrangements) but that basis is not stated in the report.
    Two material internal inconsistencies are present that the report does not resolve. First, the cashflow modeller uses £2,237/month expenditure until retirement, but the narrative 'current situation' section of the same report states total monthly expenditure of £2,986. The extraction flags this as an unresolved discrepancy. Possible explanations (e.g. net of Tony's £600 contribution, or excluding the mortgage payment of £750) exist but none is stated. Because the cashflow is used to support the retirement planning recommendation, the input figure drives the output: if expenditure is understated by £749/month the modelled surplus is overstated, and the 'no shortfall' conclusion could be affected. This is a High severity internal contradiction — no external evidence is needed to conclude because both figures appear in the same document. Second, the inheritance timing is internally contradictory: 'next year' in the cashflow assumptions versus 'two years' time' in the chart narrative. The inheritance of £350,000 is a material cashflow input; a one-year difference in its receipt affects the modelled fund trajectory. Third, no cashflow modelling output document appears in the retrieved passages. The cashflow scenarios, stress tests and outcomes (including the age-81 depletion figure) are asserted only within the suitability report itself (category I passages). Without an independent modelling output the assertions cannot be corroborated. The SAP illustrations (P1–P13) use provider-prescribed growth rates (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%) and a retirement age of 87, which differs from the cashflow's 6% growth and age 60 retirement; the guard on provider-prescribed rates explains why those rates differ from 6%, so the rate difference is not a finding. However the absence of any cashflow output document means the modelled assumptions and results have no corroboration at all. The charge arithmetic discrepancies (£9,838.36 initial fee vs 3% × £67,945.44; £3,181.08 ongoing vs 1% × £67,945.44) are both internally inconsistent with the stated transfer value and basis. They are consistent only if the PSO funds (~£260,000) are included in the fee base, but the report never states this. These are findings under CHK-007 primarily but they also represent internal inconsistency relevant to this check. The expenditure discrepancy and inheritance timing conflict are the primary findings here as they directly affect the cashflow inputs and outputs relied on to support the recommendation.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7, G2.8). The report does not state a priority rank for the objectives; the file equally contains no priority ordering — this is internally consistent as an omission but means priority order is unevidenced. The report states the client wants to retire or reduce hours at age 60; the July 2026 meeting summary records Melanie (now 55) contemplating near-term pension access to replace lost income and planning to return to part-time work until retirement, which is broadly consistent but introduces a nearer-term access need not explicitly named as a goal in the report. The meeting summary records an investment horizon of 'five to ten years' (implying ages 55–65 at earliest), whereas the stated retirement target of age 60 implies a five-year horizon from the point of advice; this does not contradict the report directly but the horizon range extends beyond age 60. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report asserts seven objectives: (1) receive PSO funds into a new pension; (2) review and consolidate dormant True Potential and Nest pensions; (3) ensure pension funds perform well to build retirement funds; (4) retire or reduce hours at age 60 with income of £25,000–£30,000 p.a.; (5) preference for tax-efficient, flexible income (FAD) over guaranteed income; (6) estate planning with adult daughters as beneficiaries/intended attorneys; (7) consider commencing regular contributions (adviser-generated, not client-stated). No priority ranking is stated. Objectives 1–6 are marked as client-stated; objective 7 is not.
    File:     Meeting summaries from fact-find meetings (16/01/2026 and 20/02/2026 with James Poka) and later meetings corroborate: the PSO transfer need arising from divorce; desire to consolidate pension pots; interest in flexi-access drawdown and annuities; a retirement income target; estate planning for daughters; and the recent move to self-employment. A July 2026 meeting summary (Morgan Seddon) records Melanie at age 55 contemplating pension access following job departure, discussing drawdown, lump sum, tax efficiency and a return to part-time work — broadly consistent with the flexible income preference. The investment horizon noted in one meeting summary is 'five to ten years'. The fact-find document (21/07/2026) covers retirement planning and savings/investments. Priority order is not evidenced anywhere in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not state a priority rank for the objectives; the file equally contains no priority ordering — this is internally consistent as an omission but means priority order is unevidenced.
      - The report states the client wants to retire or reduce hours at age 60; the July 2026 meeting summary records Melanie (now 55) contemplating near-term pension access to replace lost income and planning to return to part-time work until retirement, which is broadly consistent but introduces a nearer-term access need not explicitly named as a goal in the report.
      - The meeting summary records an investment horizon of 'five to ten years' (implying ages 55–65 at earliest), whereas the stated retirement target of age 60 implies a five-year horizon from the point of advice; this does not contradict the report directly but the horizon range extends beyond age 60.
      - The adviser-generated objective 7 (consider regular contributions) is not sourced to client evidence; the report correctly flags it as not client-stated, which is internally consistent, but there is no independent file evidence it was discussed prior to advice being given in a fact-find context.
      - No file evidence explicitly records the £25,000–£30,000 p.a. income target as a figure stated by the client, though the general retirement income goal is evidenced.
    The core objectives — PSO receipt, pension consolidation, retirement income flexibility, estate planning for daughters, and the general desire to retire around age 60 — are all traceable to meeting summary evidence in categories B and C, satisfying the minimum corroborating categories requirement. The adviser paraphrase guard applies: the meeting summaries use different wording but the substance aligns. The specific income figure of £25,000–£30,000 p.a. is asserted in the report but does not appear verbatim in any evidenced passage; this is a minor evidential gap rather than a contradiction. The investment horizon of 'five to ten years' recorded in the March 2026 meeting summary is broadly consistent with a retirement target of age 60 (the client was approximately 55 at advice), though the upper end extends beyond 60. The July 2026 meeting introduces a materially different short-term context — Melanie has left employment and is considering near-term pension access — which does not contradict the longer-term goals but signals that access requirements may be more imminent than the report's retirement-focused framing suggests. The priority order is absent from both the report and the file; where objectives do not clearly compete (consolidation, PSO receipt, retirement income, estate planning are complementary), the absence of explicit ranking is Low concern under the severity guidance. No vulnerability indicators in the file are identified as bearing materially on the goal-setting or time-horizon elements assessed here. Overall, the main objectives are evidenced, but the specific income figure lacks direct file corroboration and the near-term access context from the July 2026 meeting is not fully reflected in the stated goals section of the report.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts a retirement target age of 60 for the client (Melanie Anness, currently aged 55), giving an investment time horizon of approximately 5 years. The client's intended retirement age is stated as 60 in two places in the canonical model. Time horizons across the objectives are classified as ShortTerm (×2), LongTerm (×4) and MediumTerm (×1). The life expectancy basis uses ONS data for a 55-year-old female to age 87, linked to OBJ4. The switch/projection analysis uses age 87 as the terminal projection age. No explicit targetDate or termYears values were extracted from the report.
    File:     [P9] (Meeting Summary, category C) records that Melanie is 55 at the time of the meeting and is 'considering accessing her pension benefits to support herself financially during a transitional phase, with plans to return to work potentially in January.' It does not state a retirement age of 60 explicitly, but confirms she intends to remain in part-time work 'until retirement, balancing her responsibilities as a grandmother with her financial strategy' [P10]. [P15] (Risk Profile Report, category D) refers to discussions about a 'target and desired time horizon' but the actual retirement age entered is not quoted in the retrieved passage. The switch analysis passages [P19],[P23] project to age 87, consistent with the ONS figure. [P11] (ONS data, category F) corroborates age 87 as the life expectancy for a 55-year-old female. The fund KIIDs [P1]–[P4] and [P5]–[P6] state a minimum recommended holding period of 5 years, consistent with a 5-year horizon to age 60. No passage in categories B, C or F directly records the client stating or agreeing a retirement age of 60; the nearest is [P9] noting she is 55 and in a transitional phase, and [P10] noting she plans part-time work until retirement without specifying an age. The figure 60 is corroborated numerically in [P12] (an [I] passage — the report itself) but not in any independent evidence document.
    Discrepancies:
      - The retirement target age of 60 is stated in the report but is not explicitly confirmed by any category B, C or D evidence document — [P9] and [P10] do not name age 60 as the retirement target.
      - The time horizon mix (ShortTerm ×2, LongTerm ×4, MediumTerm ×1) is not reconciled in the report against a single stated 5-year investment horizon to age 60; the coexistence of LongTerm objectives with a 5-year horizon to retirement creates a potential tension that is not explained.
      - The switch analysis projects to age 87 (terminal age), not to retirement age 60; while the guard notes these are different figures (horizon of the money vs date of goal), the report does not explicitly distinguish them for the reader.
      - No targetDate or termYears were extracted, so the precise investment term used to justify the recommended risk profile cannot be verified from the canonical model alone.
      - The fact find [P17] and meeting summary [P9],[P10] do not record an explicit agreed retirement age of 60, leaving the report's assertion unsupported by primary client-sourced evidence.
    The central question is whether the retirement target age of 60 and the associated investment time horizon are supported by independent evidence. The report asserts target age 60 consistently (canonical model and [P12], which is an [I] passage). However [P12] is the report itself and cannot corroborate its own assertion. The only independent category C document ([P9],[P10]) records Melanie as aged 55, in a transitional phase, intending to return to part-time work, but does not state she has agreed to retire at 60. The risk profile report [P15] refers to a 'desired time horizon' discussed during profiling but the retrieved excerpt does not quote 60 or 5 years. The fund KIIDs [P1]–[P6] carry a minimum holding period of 5 years, which is arithmetically consistent with a 5-year horizon to age 60, but this is a product-level statement rather than a client instruction. The ONS and projection documents corroborate age 87 as the terminal planning age, which the report uses correctly. The guard about distinguishing the horizon of the money from the date of the goal is relevant: projections run to 87 while the retirement goal is 60, and this is an appropriate distinction. However, the mix of objective time horizons — LongTerm appearing four times alongside a 5-year horizon — is unexplained; if some objectives extend beyond retirement, the report does not articulate this, which is a disclosure shortfall rather than a data error. The primary concern is that no category B, C or F passage records the client agreeing to retire at 60. The meeting summary [P9],[P10] is the closest primary evidence and it is silent on the specific age. This leaves the report's retirement age assertion without corroboration in the case file, which under the check's decision logic constitutes a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the objectives are present but the retirement age specifically lacks client-authored evidence, and the LongTerm horizon labels sit unexplained against a 5-year stated horizon. No vulnerability indicator in the file materially alters this assessment: the meeting notes record no adjustment requirements that bear on the time-horizon question specifically.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts three access needs: (1) Emergency – client has £17,000 liquid cash available for immediate access needs during any pension transfer period; client is 'comfortable with the concept' as she has liquid cash for 'instance access funds'. (2) Planned – pension benefits accessible from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028); client intends to retire or reduce hours at age 60; state pension from age 67. (3) Planned/operational – 10–15 working days required for administration, disinvestment and banking on any future access to pension funds. No ad-hoc withdrawal amounts beyond the emergency fund are quantified in the access section, though the recommendation section references flexi-access drawdown flexibility.
    File:     [P5] (Meeting Summary with Jack): confirms approximately £17,000 in savings, noting the client plans to use £2,500 to repay a credit card charge for sofas, implying post-reduction savings of approximately £14,500. [P4] (Meeting Summary – Melanie): references a £20,000 cash savings buffer intended after clearing debts (£7,000 credit card, £13,000 personal loan). Morgan emphasised 3–6 months of expenses as a buffer; Melanie preferred 12 months. [P17] (Fact Find): Retirement Goals notes confirm plan to retire at age 60, draw £25,000–£30,000 annual pension until state pension at age 67, flexible drawdown preference. [P11] (Meeting Summary 2): investment horizon stated as 5–10 years. [P3] (Meeting Summary – Melanie): at age 55 Melanie is considering accessing pension benefits in a transitional phase. [P17] also records state pension retirement age as 67. [P20] (suitability report cashflow chart – [I]): cashflow illustration shows emergency funds of £20,000 earmarked separately. [P16] ([I]): confirms benefit access from age 55 (rising to 57). The Fact Find [P5] records savings of £17,000 with a planned £2,500 reduction; no passage explicitly confirms £17,000 remains intact and unallocated as an emergency reserve at the point of recommendation.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report treats £17,000 as the emergency fund / liquid cash buffer, but [P5] records the client's plan to use £2,500 of those savings to repay a credit card debt for sofas, reducing available liquid cash to approximately £14,500 at the time of the meeting with Jack. No passage confirms the £17,000 figure was reinstated or that the £2,500 was not applied before the recommendation date.
      - [P4] (Meeting Summary – Melanie, a separate later meeting) refers to a £20,000 cash savings buffer being created after clearing £7,000 credit card and £13,000 personal loan debts — a materially different picture from the £17,000 savings referenced in the Jack meeting. The report does not reconcile these two figures or explain which cash position applied at the date of the suitability report.
      - The cashflow chart [P20] labels £20,000 as 'Emergency funds', whereas the report's narrative references £17,000 as the liquid cash. These two figures for the emergency/buffer amount are inconsistent within the report and across the evidence.
      - The report states the client is 'comfortable' with the access arrangement because of liquid cash, but does not quantify the adequacy of that cash relative to expenditure (£2,836–£2,986/month per fact find [P13]). Morgan's own guidance in [P4] was 3–6 months of expenses; £17,000 at ~£2,900/month equates to under 6 months, which is borderline, and the adequacy assessment is not documented.
      - No explicit emergency fund section exists in the report; the extraction note flags that the £17,000 is inferred rather than explicitly earmarked as an emergency fund.
    The core access requirements — planned retirement at 60, state pension at 67, flexi-access drawdown preference, and pension access from 55/57 — are all well-supported by the evidence. [P17] directly corroborates the retirement age and income target; [P3] and [P11] corroborate the transition phase and investment horizon; [P16] and [P23] corroborate the access mechanics. These elements present no concern.
    
    The concern lies with the emergency/liquidity position. Three different figures appear across the evidence: £17,000 (Jack meeting, [P5]); approximately £14,500 (£17,000 minus the planned £2,500 sofa repayment, [P5]); and £20,000 (Melanie meeting cashflow buffer, [P4]; and the cashflow chart [P20]). The report's narrative uses £17,000, the cashflow chart uses £20,000, and neither figure is reconciled to the other. The £20,000 figure in [P4] appears to be a planned buffer to be created after clearing £20,000 of debt, which is itself a different scenario (post-debt-clearance) from the Jack meeting context. The report does not explain which position represents the client's state at the recommendation date.
    
    The adequacy of the buffer is asserted in a single boilerplate-adjacent sentence ('you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds') without quantifying it against expenditure or the adviser's own 3–6 month guidance stated in [P4]. This is a weak justification rather than a demonstrated sufficiency assessment.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the passages that would materially alter this access assessment. The transitional employment gap (no income, monthly deficit of ~£2,036 per [P13]) does heighten the materiality of the liquidity position, because the client has no current earned income and is dependent on savings and pension access. This makes the unresolved discrepancy in the cash figure more significant: the report should have clearly confirmed the liquid reserve available at the date of recommendation and assessed its adequacy explicitly.
    
    The 10–15 working days operational access point is self-referential ([I] only) but is a standard disclosure rather than a client-specific factual claim, so absence of external corroboration is not itself a concern here.
    
    Overall: planned access requirements are well-evidenced; the emergency/liquidity requirement is present but inadequately supported, with an unresolved figure discrepancy between narrative (£17,000) and cashflow (£20,000) and no documented adequacy assessment.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report identifies six foreseeable life changes: (1) change from employed to self-employed status with the same company, losing employee benefits including pension contributions, and Nest plan becoming paid up; (2) expected inheritance of £350,000 from the sale of the late mother's property, currently pending legal proceedings; (3) intended retirement at age 60 with possible part-time work beyond that point; (4) receipt of Full State Pension from age 67 at £230.21 per week gross; (5) inheritance will take the estate above the IHT threshold of £500,000 creating a potential IHT liability; (6) from 6 April 2027, most unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT purposes.
    File:     [P13] (report, category I) confirms the employment-status change, the £350,000 inheritance expectation, retirement at age 60, and the IHT position in its 'Your current situation' and 'Inheritance Tax Summary' sections — but as the report itself these are assertions not independent evidence. [P18] (category C, meeting summary) records that the client's investment horizon is five to ten years and that risk profile was assessed at level 7. [P9] (category C, meeting summary) references the mortgage and stable financial management but does not mention the employment-status change, inheritance or retirement age. [P12] (category C, meeting summary with Jack Davidson) notes objectives were 'Not discussed' and does not evidence any of the foreseeable changes. [P8] (category C) is similarly silent. [P17] (category B, fact find note) states 'She has a future inheritance of £150,000 and this will settle the remainder of her mortgage liability' — a figure materially different from the £350,000 stated in the report. [P17] also references part-time work and pension as a capital pot, broadly consistent with flexible retirement intent, but gives no retirement age. [P4] (category F, ONS) records state pension age as 67 years, consistent with the report. [P10] (category B, fact find) records income and expenditure detail but contains no entry under income changes for the employment-status change or the inheritance. The investment goals and objectives section in [P20] (category B, fact find) is entirely blank ('No details'). [P23]/[P24] (category G, suitability analysis projection) confirm date of birth 15/12/1970 but give no retirement age or foreseeable-change data. No passage in categories B, C or F independently confirms: the employment-status change to self-employed; the £350,000 inheritance figure; the retirement target age of 60; the state pension amount of £230.21 per week; or the IHT threshold breach on receipt of inheritance.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the expected inheritance is £350,000 [P13/I]; the fact find note [P17/B] records a future inheritance of £150,000 — a difference of £200,000.
      - The investment goals and objectives section of the fact find [P20/B] is entirely blank with 'No details', providing no evidenced objectives, time horizons or target dates to corroborate the report's statements.
      - The report records the employment-status change (employed to self-employed) as a foreseeable change; no category B, C or F passage independently records or corroborates this change.
      - The report states retirement target age of 60; no category B, C or F passage independently records or confirms this age (P17 references part-time work and reduced pension reliance but does not state age 60).
      - The meeting summary [P18/C] records an investment horizon of five to ten years, which from age 55 implies an end point of age 60–65 — broadly consistent with retirement at 60 but not explicitly confirming it; no conflict but also no precise corroboration.
      - The state pension amount of £230.21 per week is stated in the report but no state pension forecast document appears in the retrieved passages, despite [P3/C] recording as an action item that the client would send a state pension forecast screenshot.
      - The IHT analysis (inheritance taking estate above £500,000 threshold) is stated in the report but no independent asset schedule or estate valuation in the file corroborates the current estate value or the post-inheritance breach of threshold.
      - The 6 April 2027 pension IHT legislative change is noted in the report; per the guard this is a legislative change affecting everyone and is not a client-specific foreseeable change requiring independent client-level evidence.
    The most material finding is the direct numerical contradiction between the report's stated inheritance of £350,000 and the fact find note at [P17/B] which records £150,000. This is not a formatting difference or a different basis — both figures purport to be the expected inheritance from the sale of the late mother's property. A £200,000 discrepancy in a foreseeable life change that also drives the IHT analysis is material: the report's conclusion that the inheritance will take the estate above the £500,000 IHT threshold depends on which figure is correct. At £150,000 the IHT breach might not occur at all (depending on existing estate value), which would alter the IHT planning narrative. The blank investment goals and objectives section in the fact find [P20/B] means that the report's objectives and time horizons rest on assertion rather than recorded client evidence. The state pension forecast was identified as outstanding in the meeting summary [P3/C] but is absent from the pack, so the £230.21 weekly figure is unverified. The retirement age of 60 and the employment-status change are stated only in report passages (category I) and are not independently corroborated by any category B, C or F document. The legislative IHT change (6 April 2027) is correctly noted as a legislative rather than client-specific change under the guard and does not itself require client-level evidence. The five-to-ten-year investment horizon in [P18/C] is broadly consistent with retirement at 60 from age 55 but does not precisely confirm it. In summary: one direct numerical contradiction (inheritance amount), multiple objectives with no independent evidence, a missing state pension forecast, and a blank fact find objectives section combine to make this a Potential Concern of moderate-to-high severity.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model holds no explicit priority rankings, target dates or target amounts for individual objectives at the paths /objectives[]/priorityRank, /objectives[]/targetDate, or /objectives[]/targetAmount. The report narrative states the client is aged 55, hopes to retire at 60, has a 5-year investment horizon to retirement, and lists objectives including: consolidating pension pots, optimising retirement income via flexi-access drawdown or annuities, paying off the mortgage using the tax-free cash element, and potentially commencing regular contributions. The report also records an expected inheritance of £350,000, two adult daughters as estate planning beneficiaries, and a partner (Tony) contributing £600/month to household bills. The cashflow model uses £2,237/month expenditure until retirement, while the current situation section states total monthly expenditure of £2,986.
    File:     Meeting notes (20/02/2026) confirm the client's objectives include consolidating pension pots for better management and exploring tax-efficient strategies to optimise retirement income, with interest in flexi-access drawdown and annuities. The client intends to retire at 60, considers returning to part-time work until retirement, and has discussed de-risking the portfolio as retirement approaches. The meeting notes also confirm: an expected inheritance (cited as approximately £150,000 in meeting notes vs £350,000 in the suitability report), plans to use pension tax-free cash to pay off the mortgage, a desire for a cash buffer of 12 months of expenses, and the need to manage debts (£7,000 credit card, £13,000 personal loan). The cashflow document uses £2,237/month expenditure until retirement; the current situation section of the report states £2,986/month. No structural conflict between two objectives simultaneously claiming the same capital is explicitly evidenced, though the tax-free cash is referenced for both mortgage repayment and debt clearance, and the inheritance figure differs materially between report and meeting notes.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states expected inheritance of £350,000; the meeting notes (P9) reference approximately £150,000 from the sale of the mother's property — a difference of £200,000 on a figure that feeds directly into cashflow sufficiency assumptions.
      - The current situation section of the suitability report states monthly expenditure of £2,986; the cashflow modelling inputs use £2,237/month — an unexplained difference of £749/month that affects retirement adequacy projections.
      - The meeting notes record objectives as 'not discussed' in the first meeting (16/01/2026, P11) but substantive objectives are evidenced in the second meeting (20/02/2026, P5), so the objectives are partially evidenced but not fully structured with priority rankings or target amounts.
      - No explicit priority ranking between objectives (mortgage payoff, debt clearance, retirement income, estate planning) is present in either the report or the file, though the objectives do not on their face structurally conflict over the same capital provided the tax-free cash and inheritance are both available.
      - The inheritance figure discrepancy (£350,000 vs ~£150,000) is material to the cashflow model's conclusion that 'pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement'.
    The structural conflict test (two objectives claiming the same capital, or a growth horizon outlasting an access date) does not produce a clear conflict: the 5-year horizon to age 60 is consistent with the recommended pension wrapper and the flexi-access drawdown objective. The portfolio's long-term investment objective (5 years or more, per the fund KIID at P22) is aligned with the stated retirement age of 60. No locked-capital product is being recommended against a known near-term access date. However, two material discrepancies are present. First, the inheritance figure in the report (£350,000) differs from the meeting note figure (~£150,000 at P9) by £200,000. This figure is not peripheral: the suitability report's capacity-for-loss and cashflow sufficiency conclusions explicitly rely on the inheritance as a future capital injection. If the correct figure is ~£150,000, the cashflow conclusion may be overstated. Second, the cashflow model uses £2,237/month expenditure while the report's own current situation section states £2,986/month — an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction that has no documented explanation. Both discrepancies affect the reliability of the cashflow-based retirement adequacy assessment that underpins the suitability conclusion. The objectives themselves (consolidation, drawdown, mortgage payoff) are evidenced in the meeting notes and are consistent with the report's narrative. The absence of explicit priority rankings is not a structural concern here because the objectives do not compete for the same capital on the available evidence. No vulnerability factors are identified in the file that would materially alter this assessment beyond what is already noted — the client's post-divorce situation and the pending inheritance legal proceedings are noted and factored into the planning. The inheritance discrepancy and expenditure inconsistency together represent a data inconsistency that weakens the justification for the cashflow sufficiency conclusion.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states five objectives (OBJ1–OBJ5 linked to the pension recommendation REC1, OBJ3/4/7 to REC2, OBJ6 to RECs 3–5). Time horizons are classified ShortTerm (OBJ1, OBJ2), LongTerm (OBJ3, OBJ4, OBJ5, OBJ6) and MediumTerm (OBJ7). The selected retirement age is 60. The recommended product is an Aviva Platform Personal Pension with a transfer-in of £67,945.44 (True Potential and Nest only; Quilter PSO separately received). The report includes cashflow modelling inputs: monthly expenditure of £2,237 until retirement, mortgage of £750/month for 7 years, inheritance of £350,000 next year, and retirement expenditure of £2,000 net per month. No regular contributions are currently committed. The report notes the client has the option to retire or reduce hours at 60. IHT considerations are addressed under OBJ6. Flexi-access drawdown is identified as the intended retirement access method.
    File:     Meeting Summary with Jack confirms retirement target age of 60, income goal of £25,000–£30,000 per annum, preference for drawdown income, no intention to take the 25% tax-free lump sum immediately, and consideration of part-time work post-retirement. The meeting summary also records a significant anticipated inheritance (at least £150,000 from mother's estate valued ~£1.4m, timeline uncertain). Meeting Summary (2) records the investment horizon as 'five to ten years' and risk profile at level 7 (growth). Fact Find records existing arrangements and financial position. Monthly expenditure in the meeting summary is stated as £2,986.23, whereas the cashflow modelling inputs in the report use £2,237/month — a discrepancy of £749/month with no explanation. The inheritance figure in the cashflow model is £350,000, while the meeting note records the client expects 'at least £150,000' with the estate valued at ~£1.4m. The report states the client's retirement income target will be met through drawdown, but no income projection showing sufficiency of fund at 60 is included in the extracted model (projectedRetirementValue absent). The Aviva product KFD confirms access from normal minimum pension age (currently 55, rising to 57 from April 2028), with flexibility for drawdown or annuity — consistent with a retirement age of 60.
    Discrepancies:
      - Monthly expenditure used in cashflow modelling (£2,237) differs from the monthly expenditure stated in the financial position section and meeting notes (£2,986.23) — a gap of £749/month with no explanation in the report.
      - Inheritance figure in cashflow model is £350,000, but the meeting summary records the client expects 'at least £150,000' with the estate valued at ~£1.4m and timing uncertain — the cashflow model uses a figure that does not obviously correspond to the evidenced expectation.
      - The meeting summary records an investment horizon of 'five to ten years', which implies a horizon ending at approximately age 42–47 (client DOB 15/12/1970, so current age ~55); this sits in tension with the stated retirement target of age 60 and the LongTerm classification assigned to OBJ3–OBJ6 in the report. The report does not reconcile these.
      - No projected retirement fund value at age 60 is included in the canonical model (projectedRetirementValue absent), meaning the report does not demonstrate that the recommended solution can deliver the stated income target of £25,000–£30,000 per annum.
      - The transfer into NP1 is stated as £67,945.44 (True Potential + Nest only); the Quilter PSO of ~£260,000 is received separately into the same plan but is not reflected in the transfer figure used as the basis for projections, which may understate the plan's value and distort the retirement sufficiency assessment.
      - The report classifies OBJ1 and OBJ2 as ShortTerm and OBJ7 as MediumTerm, but the precise content of these objectives is not reproduced in the canonical model — it is therefore not possible to confirm from the extracted data alone whether those time-horizon classifications are consistent with what the client said.
    The core retirement objective (retire at 60 on £25,000–£30,000 p.a. via drawdown) is clearly evidenced in the meeting notes and consistently reflected in the report's retirement age of 60 and drawdown mechanism. The Aviva product supports access from 55/57 upwards, so there is no access restriction issue for a target retirement age of 60. The linked-objective structure (OBJ1–OBJ5 to NP1) goes beyond pure boilerplate in that specific objectives are named by ID, and the meeting summary corroborates the substantive content of those objectives.
    
    However, three material concerns arise. First, the cashflow model uses £2,237/month expenditure against a documented figure of £2,986.23/month — a £749/month shortfall that is unexplained and that could materially affect the sufficiency conclusion. Second, the inheritance input of £350,000 in the cashflow model is higher than the 'at least £150,000' evidenced in the file, and the timeline is described as uncertain; using a higher, faster-arriving figure flatters the cashflow picture. Third, and most directly relevant to appropriateness: no projected retirement fund value at age 60 is present. Without this, the report asserts that the recommended solution will deliver the income target but provides no evidence in the file to support that assertion. The critical yield figures run to age 89 (a break-even comparison against existing plans), not a retirement sufficiency projection.
    
    The meeting summary's reference to an investment horizon of 'five to ten years' is potentially consistent with a retirement target of age 60 for a client currently aged ~55, though the phrasing is ambiguous and the report does not acknowledge or resolve it.
    
    No vulnerability markers in the evidence have a bearing on goal alignment or time horizon appropriateness for this check.
    
    On balance, the recommendation is directionally consistent with the client's stated goals, but the absence of a retirement sufficiency projection, combined with the unexplained expenditure discrepancy and the inflated inheritance assumption in the cashflow model, means the report's claim that the recommendation meets the income objective at age 60 is unsupported by the evidence provided.

  [G2.8] Target retirement income stated in the report is consistent with the evidence and with the figures the plan is modelled on
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The client wants to retire at age 60, maintaining an income of between £25,000 and £30,000 per annum. The cashflow modeller uses: employment income £45,000 p.a. until age 60; monthly expenditure until retirement £2,237; monthly expenditure in retirement £2,000 net; one-off sofa spend £2,500; mortgage £750/month for 7 years; inheritance £350,000 next year; pension growth 6% p.a. before charges; state pension from age 67; cashflow runs to age 100; surplus income 0% saved; general expenditure inflation 2%; savings inflation 2%; state pension inflation 2.5%.
    File:     Meeting summary [P11] records: retirement at age 60; target annual pension of £25,000 to £30,000 until state pension at age 67; preference for drawdown; no immediate PCLS. Fact find [P17] records projected withdrawals of £1,900/month (£22,800 p.a.) and notes state pension from age 67 will cover circa half of retirement expenditure, leaving a requirement of circa £12,000 p.a. Meeting summary [P11] records annual salary of £42,000 (not £45,000) and total monthly expenditure of £2,986.23. The cashflow modeller uses £2,237/month pre-retirement expenditure, but the fact-find/meeting summary records £2,986. The retirement income modelled in the cashflow (£2,000 net/month = £24,000 p.a. net) is broadly consistent with the lower bound of the £25,000–£30,000 gross range, but the basis (gross vs net) is not confirmed in the file. The SAP documents use proposed retirement age 87, not 60. The inheritance is described as 'at least £150,000' in the meeting summary, with timeline uncertain, not £350,000 next year. The cashflow assumption of £350,000 next year is not corroborated.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report models employment income at £45,000 p.a.; meeting summary records salary as £42,000 p.a.
      - Report cashflow uses monthly pre-retirement expenditure of £2,237; fact-find and meeting summary record total monthly expenditure of £2,986.23 — an unexplained £749/month shortfall
      - Report assumes inheritance of £350,000 received 'next year'; meeting summary records expected inheritance of 'at least £150,000' with uncertain timeline; the amounts and timing both differ materially
      - Report states retirement income target as £25,000–£30,000 gross p.a.; cashflow models retirement expenditure at £2,000 net/month (£24,000 p.a. net) — basis (gross/net) not reconciled and the modelled figure falls below the stated lower bound on a gross basis unless tax assumptions bring it into range
      - SAP comparison documents use proposed retirement age 87 for projection purposes, whereas the client's stated retirement target is age 60 — these are different and the report does not explain the 87-year projection term in the context of the stated goal
      - Report states inheritance of £350,000; extraction note flags that 'Current Total Assets' chart narrative places receipt in two years' time, conflicting with 'next year' stated in cashflow assumptions — internal inconsistency within the report itself
      - Fact find [P17] refers to projected withdrawals of £1,900/month, which is lower than the £2,000/month used in the cashflow model
      - State pension inflation assumption is 2.5% in the report; no corroborating figure found in the evidence passages
    The target income range of £25,000–£30,000 p.a. is corroborated by the meeting summary [P11], so the headline objective is supported. However, several of the key assumptions underpinning the cashflow model diverge from the evidenced client data in ways that are material to whether the plan can deliver the stated objective. First, the salary used in the cashflow (£45,000) exceeds the figure in the meeting summary (£42,000) by £3,000 p.a. — this inflates the model's pre-retirement income position. Second, the monthly expenditure input to the cashflow (£2,237) is substantially lower than the evidenced figure of £2,986 in the meeting summary and fact find [P11]; no explanation is given for the £749 difference. The extraction metadata flags this as an identified internal inconsistency. A possible explanation is that the cashflow figure is net of Tony's £600 contribution and/or mortgage, but this is never stated in the report, and the mortgage assumption is separately modelled at £750/month — so the two figures do not reconcile on that basis either. Third, the inheritance assumption of £350,000 received 'next year' is not corroborated: the meeting summary records the expected share as 'at least £150,000' with uncertain timing [P11], and the fact find [P10] records a future inheritance of £150,000. The amount in the cashflow is more than double the evidenced figure. This assumption is load-bearing in the cashflow because it determines whether the client's assets survive the gap between age 60 and state pension age 67 at the modelled withdrawal rate. Fourth, the modelled retirement expenditure of £2,000 net/month equates to £24,000 p.a. net. The target income is stated as £25,000–£30,000 p.a. — described in the report without explicit gross/net label (extraction note says 'basis not explicitly described as gross or net'). If the target is gross and the model is net, these are not comparable on like-for-like terms and the model may fall short even at its lower bound. Fifth, the SAP documents [P1, P6, P9] use proposed retirement age 87, not 60. This appears to be the regulatory illustration projection age rather than the client's retirement date, so it does not contradict the retirement objective, but it means the switch analysis projections are not directly presented at the client's planning horizon of age 60, which limits their utility in evidencing the plan will meet the income goal at the point it is needed. The fact find [P17] records projected withdrawals of £1,900/month and the capacity for loss assessment is based on that figure; the cashflow uses £2,000/month. This is a minor difference but adds to the pattern of inconsistent data across documents. On vulnerability: no vulnerability flags materially affect this requirement — the client's financial acumen as an accountant and former currency trader is noted [P10, P12] and there is no indication that any communication or support adjustment would change the analysis. The combined effect of the salary discrepancy, the unexplained expenditure reduction, and the unsupported inheritance assumption means that the cashflow model underpinning the retirement income adequacy conclusion rests on inputs that are not consistently supported by the client evidence on file. This is a data quality and data consistency concern material to the suitability assessment.

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   ATR assessed code: 7; agreed code: 7; label: Growth; scale: 1-10.
    File:     The risk profile report [P1] records that a psychometric ATR questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 and that an agreed risk level was reached through interactive discussion. The agreed risk level section [P8] confirms the process but the specific numeric agreed level is not quoted verbatim in the retrieved passages from [P1]/[P8]. The suitability report itself [P2] states 'Your risk profile was agreed as 7 on a scale of 1 - 10' and describes the client as a 'Growth investor', but that passage is [I] (the report under audit). The risk profile document [P1]/[P8] describes the process and scale (1-10 implied by the questionnaire and projection methodology) but does not state the numeric result in the retrieved text. No passage from categories D, B, G or H other than the [G] FE chart entry for 'Risk 7' independently states the agreed code as 7.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk profile document passages [P1] and [P8] describe the ATR process and state that an agreed level was reached, but the retrieved text does not quote the numeric agreed code of 7 — the only non-[I] corroboration of the figure '7' as an agreed risk rating comes from the benchmark label in [P10] ('Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB'), which is a performance comparison label rather than a risk assessment output.
      - No discrepancy exists between the assessed code and agreed code — both are stated as 7 — so no unexplained override is present.
      - No discrepancy exists in the scale: the report states 1-10 and the risk profile document [P1] references a 1-10 framework consistent with that scale.
    The report asserts ATR assessed 7, agreed 7, label Growth, scale 1-10. The risk profile document [P1] and [P8] are on file and confirm that a psychometric questionnaire was completed (16/01/2026) and that an agreed risk level was reached through an interactive process using projections, but the retrieved passages do not state the numeric result. The label 'Risk 7' appears in the FE chart [P10] as a benchmark comparator ('Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB'), which corroborates that risk level 7 is the agreed basis for the investment strategy comparison, though this is a performance document rather than the risk assessment itself. The only direct verbal statement that the agreed code is 7 on a 1-10 scale comes from [P2], which is an [I] passage and cannot self-corroborate. The risk profile report is on file and is not silent — it confirms the process — but the retrieved text does not quote the agreed numeric output. This is a partial evidential gap: the process document exists but the specific agreed score of 7 is not independently confirmed in the retrieved passages beyond the indirect benchmark label in [P10]. There is no assessed-vs-agreed mismatch and no unexplained override. The scale is consistently 1-10 across all references. No vulnerability flags bearing on the risk rating have been identified in the evidence. The concern is narrow: the agreed code of 7 rests primarily on [I] passages with only indirect corroboration from [P10], and the risk profile document's retrieved text does not explicitly state the number. This is a weak justification / missing evidence concern at low severity, as the process document is on file and the indirect corroboration is present.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   CFL is rated 'Medium'. The basis given is: (1) pension funds plus inheritance will be sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after a market fall; (2) the client has the option to work beyond age 60 or part-time if necessary; (3) cashflow analysis was used to assess retirement sufficiency. CFL is stated to be separately assessed from ATR (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true).
    File:     [P21] (Fact Find, category B) states: 'Based on the above her capacity for loss is medium to high, however i feel this is leaning more to medium as if the withdrawals continue at the projected rate of £1,900 per month this will reduce longevity. Once her state pension kicks in at age 67, half of her retirement expenditure will be covered by this leaving the requirement of circa £12,000 per annum which will improve longevity and her overall capacity for loss.' [P1] (Risk profile report, category D) refers to a 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section but records no content — all narrative fields are blank/truncated. [P5] (Fact Find, category B) notes a future inheritance of £150,000 that will settle the mortgage, and that current pension value is circa £300,000. [P3] (Suitability report, category I) contains the CFL section verbatim — this is the assertion side. No separate evidenced cashflow output document is in the pack beyond the report's own narrative. The Fact Find records total monthly expenditure of £2,836.23 and total net monthly income of £800, giving a disposable income of −£2,036.23 per month. The report states monthly expenditure of £2,986 (with Tony contributing £600). An internal inconsistency is noted between cashflow modeller input of £2,237/month and the financial position figure of £2,986/month.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states CFL is 'Medium'; the Fact Find adviser note ([P21]) describes CFL as 'medium to high, however leaning more to medium' — the report drops the 'medium to high' qualifier and presents only 'medium' without acknowledging the dual characterisation recorded by the adviser.
      - The report asserts an inheritance of £350,000 ([P7]) as a basis for retirement sufficiency; the Fact Find ([P5]) records the inheritance as £150,000 — a material difference in the asset used to justify adequate CFL.
      - The report states monthly expenditure of £2,986 ([P7]); the Fact Find ([P6]) records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £2,836.23 — a difference of approximately £150/month.
      - The report states cashflow analysis was used to assess retirement sufficiency, but the cashflow modeller inputs use £2,237/month expenditure ([P8]) rather than either the Fact Find figure (£2,836.23) or the report's own current situation figure (£2,986), creating an unexplained discrepancy that undermines the reliability of the cashflow analysis underpinning the CFL conclusion.
      - The Risk Profile Report ([P1]) contains a 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section with no recorded content, meaning there is no evidenced questionnaire output to support the CFL determination independently of the suitability report narrative.
      - The report asserts CFL is separately assessed from ATR, but the CFL narrative in [P3] opens by referencing the agreed ATR discussion before moving to CFL, and the justification relies primarily on asset sufficiency rather than a distinct quantified assessment — this is a borderline restatement pattern.
    The central CFL finding is a material discrepancy in the inheritance figure used to justify retirement sufficiency: the report uses £350,000 ([P7]) while the Fact Find ([P5]) records £150,000. The inheritance is explicitly cited as a component of the CFL rationale ('pension funds along with the inheritance will be sufficient to cover expenditure in retirement'), so a £200,000 difference in that figure is directly relevant to the adequacy of the CFL assessment and is not explained anywhere in the pack. This is a data inconsistency that cannot be explained by a false-positive guard. The Fact Find adviser note ([P21]) characterises CFL as 'medium to high, leaning more to medium', whereas the report presents it simply as 'Medium' — the underlying analysis is more nuanced than the report reflects. The expenditure inconsistency (£2,986 in the report vs £2,836.23 in the Fact Find vs £2,237 in the cashflow modeller) further undermines the reliability of the cashflow analysis cited as supporting the CFL conclusion. No cashflow output document is in the evidence pack; the cashflow analysis is referenced in the report but not corroborated by a separate evidence document. The Risk Profile Report's CFL questionnaire section is blank, so there is no evidenced questionnaire output. The report's assertion that CFL is separately assessed from ATR is weakly supported: while the CFL section does go beyond merely restating ATR, it relies on asset sufficiency and an option to work longer rather than a rigorous quantified loss-tolerance assessment, and the questionnaire that was evidently completed ([P1] refers to it) has no recorded outputs. The inheritance discrepancy is the highest-severity finding here as it directly affects the factual basis of the CFL rationale. No vulnerability factors identified in the evidence appear to materially alter this assessment beyond what is already captured.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The suitability report states that tolerance for loss was discussed with the client following agreement of the attitude to risk (risk level 7 on a 1–10 scale), and that the client 'confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses.' No maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated. Capacity for loss is described as 'moderate', supported by reference to cashflow analysis, pension funds plus inheritance being sufficient in retirement even after a market fall, and the option to work beyond age 60. The report does not separately quantify a tolerance for loss figure, nor does it model a specific loss scenario with a named percentage fall.
    File:     The risk profile report (P10, P18) confirms a risk profiling questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 and that risk level 7 was agreed. P18 records sections for 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' and 'Capacity for loss for the goal' but the notes fields in those sections are blank — no additional notes were recorded. P19 (meeting summary) records the risk profile as level 7 and confirms the client expressed comfort with the level of risk. P22 (meeting summary with Jack) records the client expressed 'a readiness to accept moderate capital loss in the short term'. The Defaqto report for the existing Nest holding (P7) rates that fund as 'Very Adventurous' risk level 9, which is higher than the agreed level 7. No document in the pack quantifies a specific maximum tolerance for loss percentage or amount for the recommended strategy. No stress-test or loss scenario modelling of the recommended portfolio is evidenced.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report asserts tolerance for loss was confirmed by the client and is consistent with capacity for loss, but no quantification (percentage or monetary amount) of tolerance for loss appears anywhere in the report or supporting documents.
      - The risk profile report (P18) contains a 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section and a 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section, but both are recorded with no notes — the detail that might corroborate the report's tolerance assertion is absent.
      - The meeting summary (P22) records the client's readiness to accept 'moderate capital loss in the short term', but the suitability report describes capacity for loss as 'moderate' without separately distinguishing tolerance for loss or linking the two explicitly.
      - No stress-test or large-loss scenario for the recommended portfolio is modelled in any document, meaning the assertion that confirmed tolerance is within acceptable bounds cannot be verified against a quantified outcome.
      - The report states tolerance was 'confirmed' verbally, but the only corroborating document (P18) records no notes in the relevant sections, leaving the confirmation unsupported by written evidence.
    The core issue under G3.3 is whether tolerance for loss is quantified and evidenced consistently with the capacity for loss assessment and risk profile. The report asserts verbal confirmation of acceptable tolerance but provides no figure. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not quantify tolerance separately, and absence alone is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction. However, the risk profile report (P18) — the document specifically designed to record this — contains blank notes fields for both the capacity for loss questionnaire and the capacity for loss goal sections. This means the supporting documentation that should corroborate the assertion is present in the file but silent on the point: the sections exist, they were not completed. That is a stronger finding than mere absence, because it shows the framework existed but was not used. The meeting summary at P22 offers partial corroboration ('readiness to accept moderate capital loss in the short term') but this is general and qualitative, not linked to a specific scenario or the recommended portfolio. No cashflow stress test modelling a specific fall percentage for the recommended Aviva portfolio is in evidence (the cashflow model referenced in the report is referenced but its loss-scenario outputs are not in the retrieved passages). The P18 passages marked [I] (the report itself) cannot self-corroborate. The net position is: tolerance for loss was asserted as confirmed but is not quantified, and the document that should hold the record of that confirmation contains no notes. This is a documentation gap — a disclosure shortfall and missing evidence — rather than an active contradiction. The severity is Low per the guidance (tolerance not quantified where no large-loss scenario is modelled), as no large-loss scenario modelling is evidenced that would elevate this. The vulnerability overlay: P17 notes the client is recently divorced, self-employed and expecting an inheritance; P14 records no specific vulnerability characteristics that would materially alter this risk/tolerance assessment. No adjustment to severity is required on vulnerability grounds.
