  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model records seven objective-level time horizons: ShortTerm, ShortTerm, LongTerm, LongTerm, LongTerm, LongTerm, MediumTerm. No single consolidated investment term in years is extracted from the risk-assessment section (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears is absent). The suitability report narrative (passage P9) does not state an explicit term in years for the risk profile.
    File:     Meeting summary [P14] records: 'Your investment horizon is five to ten years' and 'Your risk profile assessed as level seven, indicating a preference for growth-oriented investments.' The Defaqto risk profile report [P2] describes the process by which the agreed risk level was reached using 'desired time horizon' as an input, and records the agreed risk level but does not state a numeric term in the extracted passages. The Aviva KFD [P10] states investments of this type 'should be viewed as medium to long term (typically more than five years).' No passage states a specific term in years as the basis for the agreed risk level 7, nor does any passage link a particular term to the capacity-for-loss or strategy decision.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records both ShortTerm and LongTerm objectives for the same client with no explanation of how a short-term objective is reconciled with a risk level 7 (growth) profile that the meeting summary describes as having a five-to-ten-year horizon.
      - The meeting summary [P14] gives a specific investment horizon of five to ten years, but the canonical model's objective list includes ShortTerm horizons — no passage or report narrative explains how short-term objectives are handled within a long-horizon, growth-oriented risk profile.
      - The risk-assessment section of the canonical model contains no investment term in years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears absent), so the term that was used to support risk level 7 is not directly stated in the report.
      - The Defaqto risk profile passages [P1, P2] show that the capacity-for-loss section notes are blank/empty, meaning no rationale for capacity for loss linked to time horizon is recorded in the risk profile document.
    The meeting summary [P14] records an investment horizon of five to ten years and a risk level 7, which is internally consistent — a medium-to-long horizon supports a growth profile at level 7. The Aviva KFD [P10] corroborates a medium-to-long term framing. However, the canonical model also records ShortTerm objectives among the client's goals. The report does not explain how short-term objectives are accommodated within a risk level 7, five-to-ten-year strategy. The guard 'money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon than the retirement date itself — but the report must say so' is relevant: a short-term objective could represent an interim cash need rather than a contradiction, but the report must state this. It does not. The risk-assessment section of the report is silent on the numeric term used to set the risk level, and the capacity-for-loss section of the Defaqto risk profile document [P1] is blank, so no rationale linking time horizon to capacity for loss is evidenced. The absence of /riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears is confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction's own metadata — this is a report silence, not an extraction failure, and is therefore a disclosure shortfall. The primary concern is that short-term objectives sit alongside a growth-risk, long-horizon profile with no bridging explanation, which constitutes a data inconsistency and a disclosure shortfall. Severity is moderate: the five-to-ten-year horizon from the meeting note broadly supports risk level 7 for the main pension strategy, but the unexplained short-term objectives and absent capacity-for-loss rationale leave the term-to-profile link only partially evidenced.

      - The report assigns risk rating 7 to the HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc individually; no factsheet or KIID in the pack assigns a firm-scale rating of 7 to that fund individually — the FE scatter chart [P14] assigns risk 7 only to the blended portfolio, not to either constituent fund alone.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Agreed ATR: risk 7 on a scale of 1–10 (Growth investor). Capacity for loss: Medium (moderate), supported by cashflow analysis and the view that pension funds plus inheritance will cover retirement expenditure even after a market fall, with the option to work beyond age 60. Target risk rating for the recommended strategy: 7. Recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk 7) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (50%, risk 7). Capacity for loss is described as moderate and is substantiated partly by reference to attitude to risk and partly by cashflow modelling. No risk-profile override or upward amendment is described.
    File:     [P1] and [P6] confirm an ATR questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 using a psychometric tool and that an agreed risk level was reached following interactive discussion of natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return, but the passages do not state the numerical agreed risk level. [P6] notes that no additional notes were recorded against any of the sub-sections (Attitude to Risk, Investor Experience, Capacity for Loss, Investment Target, Agreed Risk Level). [P8] (Defaqto report for the Nest holding) rates the NEST Higher Risk Fund at risk level 9 (Very Adventurous) on its own scale. [P4] (report — [I]) shows the two recommended funds each carry a risk rating of 7. [P14] (FE scatter chart) labels the proposed blended portfolio 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB', confirming the strategy carries risk level 7. The KIID passages [P13]/[P23]/[P24] for Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity show a SRRI of 5 out of 7 on the EU/UK SRRI scale. The HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic KIID in [P15] shows a fund-level risk rating of 6 out of 7 on the SRRI scale. No passage independently corroborates the agreed ATR as 7 on the adviser's 1–10 scale; the risk profile report [P6] confirms the process took place and an agreement was reached but records no number. CFL is described in [P2] ([I]) as moderate, with the rationale referring to pension funds, inheritance and flexibility of retirement date; the capacity-for-loss questionnaire section in [P6] records no notes. No cross-scale mapping between the adviser's 1–10 scale and fund SRRI (1–7) scales is provided anywhere in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The agreed ATR of 7 (on the adviser's 1–10 scale) is stated in the report but is not independently confirmed by any non-[I] passage; the risk profile report [P6] records no numerical agreed level and notes that no additional notes were recorded in any sub-section.
      - The CFL section of the risk profile report [P6] records no notes, yet the suitability report asserts a moderate CFL with a qualitative rationale; no independent CFL questionnaire scoring or output is evidenced in the pack.
      - The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity KIID carries SRRI 5/7; the HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic KIID carries SRRI 6/7. These are on a different scale (1–7) from the adviser's agreed rating (1–10). No mapping is provided; the report does not reconcile these ratings to the agreed ATR of 7/10.
      - The Defaqto report for the Nest existing holding [P8] assigns it risk level 9 (Very Adventurous) on that tool's scale. The suitability report records the Nest fund risk level as 8, also on an unspecified provider scale. Neither scale is mapped to the adviser's 1–10 scale, leaving comparability unresolved.
      - The CFL rationale in the report refers partly to the client's attitude to risk as a supporting factor, which risks conflating ATR and CFL rather than treating them as independent assessments.
    The core ATR/strategy alignment can be partially assessed: the report asserts ATR 7/10 and the recommended funds each carry a risk rating of 7 (confirmed by [P4] [I] and [P14] [G]). On the adviser's own scale, strategy equals agreed rating — no upward breach is apparent. However, three concerns arise.
    
    First, the agreed ATR of 7 is stated only in [I] passages; [P6] (the risk profile report, a [D] document) confirms the process was completed and an agreement reached but records no number, and explicitly notes no additional notes were recorded in any sub-section including the agreed-risk-level section. The claim that ATR is 7 therefore has no independent evidential support — it is asserted by the report and unconfirmed by the document that should corroborate it.
    
    Second, the capacity-for-loss assessment is substantively weak. [P6] records nothing in the CFL sub-section. The report's CFL rationale ([P2], [I]) mentions that pension funds plus inheritance will cover retirement expenditure and that the client could work beyond 60 — these are plausible CFL factors — but also states the client 'confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance based on your capacity to suffer any losses', which partially restates ATR rather than independently quantifying loss tolerance. The cashflow modelling is referenced but no stress-tested loss figure or quantified tolerance is stated. This meets the Moderate severity descriptor (CFL asserted partly as a restatement of ATR).
    
    Third, the fund KIIDs use the SRRI scale (1–7): Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is SRRI 5 and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic is SRRI 6. These cannot be compared to the adviser's scale of 1–10 without a mapping, which is absent from the pack. The guard requires reporting incomparability rather than a breach, but the report implicitly claims the funds align with the agreed rating of 7/10 without resolving this. That claim is unsupported.
    
    The FE scatter chart [P14] labels the blended portfolio 'Risk 7', which is on the adviser's scale and is consistent with ATR 7 — this is the strongest corroborating evidence for strategy/ATR alignment. On the adviser's own scale, no upward breach is demonstrated. The issues are: (a) the agreed ATR number lacks independent documentary confirmation; (b) CFL is inadequately evidenced and partly conflated with ATR; and (c) the SRRI ratings of the recommended funds are not reconciled to the adviser's scale.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers or characteristics are recorded in the accessible passages of the case file that would materially alter this assessment.
    
    Overall: material clarification and additional evidence are required — the agreed ATR is unconfirmed by the primary ATR document, CFL lacks independent scoring, and fund-level risk ratings on a different scale are not mapped to the agreed rating.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   Client Melanie Anness has an assessed and agreed ATR of 7 on a 1-10 scale, labelled 'Growth'. No override was applied — questionnaire result and agreed code are both 7. The profile description states she does not see herself as cautious, views risk as opportunity, looks for higher returns and understands the need to take risk to meet goals.
    File:     P3/P10 (Risk profile report 16/03/2026, category D): the Defaqto Engage risk profile report for Melanie Anness records her natural risk level from the ATR questionnaire completed 16/01/2026 but the body text of the passages retrieved is truncated and does not explicitly state the numeric score. P11 (nest Defaqto Existing Holdings, category D): the Nest existing-holdings Defaqto report rates the portfolio as risk level 9 'Very Adventurous' on the same 1-10 scale, with a 10-year volatility of 17.68. P12 (Meeting Summary, category C): records 'Your risk profile assessed as level seven, indicating a preference for growth-oriented investments' and confirms the client expressed comfort with the level of risk. P1 (Risk profile report, category D): the body of the risk profile report document is present but the score is in the truncated/backfilled portion; the section headings confirm an ATR questionnaire, capacity-for-loss questionnaire and agreed risk level section exist but the actual numeric result is not visible in the retrieved text.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the agreed and assessed ATR is 7 (label: Growth). The Defaqto Existing Holdings report for the Nest plan (P11) records risk level 9 'Very Adventurous' on the same 1-10 scale. These are two different numeric scores on the same scale appearing in the same case file, though one relates to an existing holding and one to the agreed profile.
      - The report's description for level 7 ('Growth') states the client 'understand that you need to take some risk to meet your investment goals', while the level-9 'Very Adventurous' description in P11 states 'you need to take considerable risk to meet your investment goals' — the label and description differ materially.
      - The numeric score 7 stated in the report is not directly corroborated by the visible text of the risk profiler report (P1/P3/P10) because the score appears in the truncated portion of that document; the only independent corroboration is the meeting summary (P12, category C), not the primary profiling document (category D).
      - The risk profile report (P1/P3) was dated 16/03/2026 but the ATR questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 — a two-month gap — with no explanation of whether any interim discussion or re-confirmation occurred.
    The report asserts a single ATR of 7/10 (Growth) for Melanie Anness, with no override. The meeting summary (P12, category C) independently corroborates level 7. The primary risk profiling document (P1/P3/P10, category D) is present in the file but the numeric result is in the truncated portion and cannot be read from the retrieved passages — so the minimum corroborating category D evidence for the score of 7 is effectively absent from what can be verified, though the document's structure (ATR questionnaire section, agreed risk level section) is confirmed. The most notable finding is in P11 (category D, Defaqto Existing Holdings for Nest): the existing Nest holding is rated level 9 'Very Adventurous' on the same 1-10 scale. The scale-guard requires establishing whether these are on the same scale before treating a numeric difference as a conflict. Both use the Defaqto 1-10 scale (confirmed by the identical descriptive language pattern and the fact that P11 explicitly states 'Very Adventurous' at level 9 while the report's level 7 is 'Growth' — both Defaqto labels). These are therefore directly comparable scores on the same scale. However, P11 is an assessment of the existing Nest fund's risk characteristics, not the client's ATR — so the level-9 score is the fund/portfolio risk rating, not the client's agreed profile. The scale-guard about profiler vs fund-house scales is relevant: the existing fund's Defaqto risk rating of 9 is a portfolio-level assessment, while 7 is the client's ATR. Under the 'what to assess' rules, existing fund suitability is excluded, but conflicting risk labels across the file are in scope. The presence of a level-9 fund in the existing portfolio while the client's agreed ATR is 7 does appear across the file without comment in the suitability report, and could represent a label conflict. The suitability report does not discuss why the existing Nest holding is at risk level 9 while the client's agreed profile is 7. This is a mild concern rather than a high one because: (a) the level-9 rating is for an existing holding being switched away, not the recommendation; (b) the recommended strategy is rated at 7; (c) the client confirmed comfort with the risk level. There is no vulnerability indicator in the evidence that would affect this assessment materially. The missing primary-document numeric confirmation is the more substantive gap: the category D risk profiler report's actual score is not readable in the retrieved evidence, leaving the score of 7 corroborated only by a category C meeting summary rather than the primary D-category profiling document. This represents missing evidence of the agreed risk level from the principal assessment document.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client's risk profile is rated 7 on a 1–10 scale (label: Growth), agreed following a risk profiling questionnaire. Capacity for loss is assessed as moderate, supported by cashflow analysis, asset sufficiency (pension funds plus inheritance sufficient even after a market fall) and the option to work beyond age 60. The recommended strategy is a 50/50 blend of HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 7, OCF 0.19%, passive) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (risk rating 7, OCF 0.20%, passive), both rated 7 on the same 1–10 scale as the agreed profile. The strategy target risk rating is 7 on a 1–10 scale. The report states the funds reflect the client's risk profile individually and combined, that portfolio drift will not alter the risk being taken, and that they are part of a CIP regularly reviewed by a quarterly investment committee. The management style is described as Blend (active/passive mix), though both funds are individually described as passive. The benchmark is UT Mixed Investment 40–85% Shares Retail TR in GB. An investment term is stated in the cashflow/projection section but is absent from the extracted model path /riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears. The report describes a 'moderate' CFL, and the rationale distinguishes it from the ATR (separately assessed). No quantified tolerance for loss figure or specific loss scenario is modelled in the CFL section.
    File:     [P2] FE scatter chart confirms the recommended blended portfolio (Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB) achieved 59.29% performance and 8.63 volatility over the period to 24 March 2026, benchmarked against UT Mixed Investment 40–85% Shares Retail TR in GB (37.41%, 7.48 volatility). [P10]–[P13] SAP documents confirm the portfolio holds HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio Accumulation C at 50% and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity Fund A Acc at 50%, with a 5-year annualised best-case of 10.99% and worst-case of 5.51%, and a worst 1-year return of -8.76% (Dec 21–Dec 22). Standard deviation over 5 years is 8.62. [P15] HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio fact sheet states the fund aims to provide growth in line with its risk profile in the long term (five years or more) and that its risk profile is rated 4 where 1 is lower and 5 is higher (HSBC's internal 1–5 scale). [P1] KIID for HSBC confirms ongoing charge of 0.19%. No independent fund-level document confirms that either fund carries a rating of 7 on the adviser's 1–10 scale; the HSBC fund sheet states a risk rating of 4 on HSBC's own 1–5 scale. No passage confirms a risk profiling questionnaire result of 7 or its methodology in a category D, B, G or H document. No passage from a category D or B document (risk profiling tool output) is present in the evidence pack. The SAP documents confirm a long-term investment objective for the funds (5 years or more). No explicit investment term (in years) for the client is evidenced in a supporting document in the pack.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report assigns both funds a risk rating of 7 on the adviser's 1–10 scale; [P15] states the HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio's risk profile is rated 4 on HSBC's own 1–5 scale. These are different scales but the report presents '7' as if it is the fund's own rating without clarifying it is the adviser's scale mapping, not HSBC's published SRRI/risk indicator.
      - The report describes the management style as 'Blend (active/passive)', but both constituent funds are individually described as passive (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity are both passive index-based funds). No active fund is identified in the holdings to justify the 'active/passive blend' label.
      - No category D or B document (risk profiling questionnaire output) is present in the evidence pack to corroborate the agreed ATR of 7 on the 1–10 scale. The report asserts the rating was determined by questionnaire, but there is no independent evidence of the questionnaire result.
      - Capacity for loss is assessed as 'moderate' but no quantified loss scenario is modelled: the rationale is expressed in qualitative terms (sufficiency of assets, option to work longer, cashflow analysis referenced) without a figure or percentage drop being tested. The CFL conclusion is not demonstrably independent of the ATR — both are described at a level consistent with risk 7 without a separate quantitative stress test being evidenced.
      - No investment term in years is extracted from the report (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears absent) and no supporting document in the pack records the client's agreed investment horizon, making it impossible to confirm the portfolio's long-term (5-year minimum) horizon matches the client's stated term.
    The core alignment check — does a risk-7 portfolio match a risk-7 client on the same scale — is internally consistent within the report: the agreed ATR is 7/10 and both funds are stated as 7/10. [P2] corroborates the blended portfolio's composition and its relative outperformance versus the benchmark. [P10]–[P13] confirm the 50/50 split and provide worst-case drawdown data (-8.76% over 1 year), which is broadly consistent with a growth-oriented risk 7 profile. However, three concerns arise. First, the report's fund-level risk rating of '7' is the adviser's own scale mapping, not the funds' published ratings. [P15] shows HSBC rates the Dynamic Portfolio at 4 on its own 1–5 scale. The guard correctly notes that SRRI and risk-and-reward indicators run on their own scales and cannot be used as value matches — so this is not a contradiction — but the report presents '7' as the fund rating without clarifying the basis, which is a disclosure shortfall. Second, no independent category D/B document corroborates the questionnaire result. The report asserts a questionnaire-derived rating but the questionnaire output is absent from the pack. This is an unsupported assertion, not an extraction failure (the extraction metadata does not flag this path as missing due to extraction error). Third, the management style label 'Blend' is not supported by the holdings: both HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity are passive funds. The report's rationale states 'a blend of active and passive investments' but no active component is identified. This is a weak or inaccurate justification. The CFL is separately assessed and references cashflow analysis, but is expressed purely qualitatively. No stress-tested loss scenario is documented. This falls short of a fully evidenced CFL but, given the cashflow tool outputs are present in the pack ([P6]–[P9]) and the CFL rationale references them, this is moderate rather than high severity. No vulnerability overlay concerns are identified that would affect this requirement — no vulnerability flags are recorded in the accessible evidence that would alter the risk or CFL conclusions. The investment term cannot be confirmed from the pack, but the fund factsheets and SAP documents all state a minimum 5-year horizon for the recommended funds, and the cashflow projections to retirement age are consistent with a long-term horizon, so this gap is low severity. The most material concerns are the absent questionnaire output and the inaccurate active/passive blend characterisation.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   ATR assessed as 7/10 (Growth) via psychometric questionnaire; agreed profile is also 7/10 with no override. Capacity for loss assessed as moderate, based on: (1) pension funds plus inheritance sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after a market fall; (2) option to work beyond 60 or part-time if necessary; (3) cashflow analysis conducted. Tolerance for loss discussed and verbally confirmed by client as acceptable. Investment term stated (meeting summary) as 5–10 years. Strategy: 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc (risk 7) / 50% Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity Fund A Acc (risk 7), target risk 7/10, on Aviva platform; both funds and combined portfolio are said to reflect the agreed risk profile. The report states the funds individually and combined are rated 7, matching the agreed ATR. No active-passive blend rationale inconsistency — both funds are described as passive in the canonical model despite the report citing an 'active/passive blend' rationale.
    File:     Risk profile report (D, P1/P2/P19) confirms a psychometric ATR questionnaire was completed on 16/01/2026 and an agreed risk level was reached following interactive discussion of projections. The agreed risk level is documented but the specific numeric result (7) is not visible in the retrieved text of P1/P2 — the number is confirmed externally by meeting summary P3/P4, which records 'risk profile assessed as level seven on a scale from [1–10]' and client confirmation of comfort with that level. FE scatter chart (P9) labels the proposed portfolio 'Risk 7 HSBC Gl Dyn/Vanguard LS 80 TR in GB', directly corroborating the risk-7 classification of the combined strategy. Fund performance data (P10–P16) confirms the two funds recommended. Defaqto data for the Nest existing holding (P18/P22) rates it risk 9 ('Very Adventurous') with a 17.68 10-year volatility, and separately shows an amended holding rated 8 ('Growth') with 13.21 volatility (P22). The suitability report's internal risk label 'Growth' and description for risk 7 exactly match the Defaqto label and description for risk level 8 in P22. Capacity for loss: no independent CFL assessment document is in the retrieved evidence; the rationale in the report is self-contained. Cashflow analysis is referenced in the report and a July 2026 meeting (P7/P8) discusses cashflow planning, but a dedicated pre-recommendation cashflow model output is not among the retrieved passages. Inheritance of ~£150,000 is corroborated (P8). Option to work part-time corroborated (P8). Investment term: meeting summary (P4) records '5 to 10 years'. The report's fund rationale includes 'active/passive blend', but both recommended funds are passive (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic is passive; Vanguard LifeStrategy is passive); no active fund is present.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report describes the client's risk profile as 'Growth' with the description matching risk level 7 on its stated 1–10 scale; however, the Defaqto report for the amended proposed holding (P22) uses the label 'Growth' and the identical description for risk level 8, not 7. This raises a question whether the report's risk-7 label 'Growth' is consistent with the external tool's taxonomy, or whether the agreed level is 8 mis-stated as 7.
      - The report's fund rationale states 'a blend of active and passive investments', but both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio C Acc and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity) are passive index/multi-asset passive funds. No active fund is present in the 50/50 portfolio.
      - The capacity for loss is assessed as 'moderate' with reference to cashflow analysis, but no standalone cashflow model output (pre-recommendation) is present in the retrieved evidence to corroborate this. The July 2026 meeting note (P7/P8) post-dates the recommendation and concerns a different situation (job loss, pension access).
      - Tolerance for loss is confirmed only verbally ('you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance'); no quantified maximum acceptable loss figure or stress-test scenario is documented.
      - The Defaqto report for the Nest existing holding (P18) assigns it risk level 9 ('Very Adventurous'), yet the suitability report refers to the client's agreed risk as 7 ('Growth'). While existing holdings are out of scope, the file contains two different Defaqto ratings (8 and 9) for Nest-related holdings (P18 vs P22), creating potential ambiguity about which risk tool/scale the adviser used for the proposed portfolio risk labelling.
    The core ATR and agreed risk rating (7/10) is corroborated by the meeting summary (P3/P4) and the FE scatter chart (P9), which labels the proposed portfolio 'Risk 7'. No override was applied and no incompatible scale comparison arises. The strategy risk rating (7) equals the agreed ATR (7), so the highest-severity trigger (strategy exceeds agreed profile) does not apply.
    
    However, two issues warrant a Potential Concern:
    
    1. Active/passive blend mismatch: The report explicitly states the strategy provides 'a blend of active and passive investments'. Both recommended funds are passive (HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio is a passive multi-asset fund; Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity is a passive fund). This is an internally inconsistent statement in the rationale that is not explained anywhere in the file. It is not boilerplate — it is a client-specific rationale claim that is factually incorrect given the fund composition.
    
    2. 'Growth' label at risk 7 vs Defaqto's 'Growth' at risk 8: The risk profile tool excerpt in P22 (Defaqto, amended existing holdings) uses the label 'Growth' and the verbatim description 'As a Growth investor you do not see yourself as a cautious person and are inclined to view risk as a source of opportunity rather than as a threat... you typically look for higher returns rather than safer investments' for risk level 8. The suitability report uses this same label and near-identical description for risk level 7. This creates ambiguity: either the adviser's CIP/tool maps 'Growth' to 7 on a different scale from Defaqto, or there is a labelling inconsistency. The risk profile report (P1/P2) does not display the numeric result in the retrieved text, so independent confirmation that the questionnaire produced 7 (rather than 8) cannot be made from the document text alone. The meeting summary confirms 'level seven' verbally (P3/P4), which provides some corroboration, but the scale on which this was agreed is not explicitly stated in P1/P2 as retrieved. The FE scatter chart (P9) labels the portfolio 'Risk 7', which supports the 7/10 assertion. This discrepancy is therefore of moderate rather than high concern — the evidence on balance supports 7 as the agreed rating, but the 'Growth' label is shared between risk levels 7 and 8 on different tools, creating a disclosure ambiguity.
    
    3. Capacity for loss: The assessment is separately stated from ATR (which is positive), references three rationale elements (asset sufficiency, alternative provision, cashflow analysis), and cites an inheritance and option to work part-time, both corroborated in the file (P8). The cashflow analysis is referenced in the report but no pre-recommendation cashflow output is among the retrieved passages. This means the cashflow-supported CFL rationale is partially unsupported by the evidence pack. The July 2026 meeting (P7/P8) discusses cashflow in a different context. This is a missing evidence concern rather than a contradiction.
    
    4. Tolerance for loss: Confirmed verbally with no quantified loss scenario. Per the severity guidance, this is a Low concern where no large-loss scenario is modelled.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability flags are recorded in the canonical model or the retrieved passages that would materially affect this assessment.
    
    The active/passive blend misstatement is the clearest finding: it is a factually incorrect rationale claim that is neither boilerplate nor explained by any guard.

  [G3.11] Funds inside a retained arrangement align with the agreed risk rating
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   All three existing arrangements (Quilter PSO, True Potential pension, Nest pension) are being fully transferred (adviceAction: FullTransfer for all three). There are therefore no retained arrangements. The client's agreed risk rating is 7. The True Potential plan had a risk level of 6 on an unspecified provider scale and held a mixed portfolio (including 'Balanced' and 'Aggressive' labelled funds). The Nest plan held the 'Nest Higher Risk Fund' with a risk level of 8 on an unspecified provider scale. The report states explicitly that 'The current portfolio is not in line with the agreed risk level' as a rationale for transferring the True Potential plan. No equivalent statement is made about Nest or Quilter fund alignment.
    File:     [P1] confirms the agreed risk level process and identifies the client's agreed risk level, but does not state a numeric result in the retrieved excerpt. The report itself (category [I]) states the agreed risk code is 7. No passage in categories D, B, G or H provides a fund-level risk rating for any of the three existing arrangements. The Nest Defaqto file [P5] provides performance data for the NEST Higher Risk Fund but does not state a risk rating number. The True Potential fund factsheets [P6] and [P8] do not state a numeric risk rating for the individual funds held. No scale mapping between provider risk ratings (e.g. True Potential's 1–10, Nest's rating) and the adviser's agreed 1–10 scale is present in any passage. No evidence in the file corroborates or contradicts the True Potential plan-level rating of 6 or the Nest fund rating of 8 from an independent source.
    Discrepancies:
      - All three arrangements are recorded as FullTransfer, meaning no arrangement is retained — yet the requirement asks about funds inside retained arrangements. This makes the primary alignment question (retained fund vs agreed profile) technically not applicable.
      - Despite all being transferred, the report asserts the True Potential portfolio 'is not in line with the agreed risk level' (risk 6 vs agreed 7), but no independent evidence corroborates the risk rating of 6 on any named scale.
      - The Nest fund is stated to have a risk rating of 8 (above the agreed 7), but no independent evidence corroborates this rating or identifies the scale it sits on.
      - No scale mapping is provided to confirm whether the True Potential provider scale (1–10 assumed) and the Nest provider scale are comparable to the adviser's agreed 1–10 scale.
      - The report's rationale for misalignment is given only for True Potential ('not in line with agreed risk level') and not articulated for Nest (which at rating 8 would exceed the agreed level of 7) or Quilter.
      - No passage in D, B, G or H provides fund-level risk evidence for any existing arrangement.
    Because all three existing arrangements carry adviceAction of FullTransfer, none is technically retained. Under the strict trigger for G3.11 (funds inside a retained arrangement), the requirement is not triggered by any of the three plans. However, the check instruction states that a plan transferred partly because it was misaligned still warrants review of whether the misalignment claim is evidenced — the report explicitly uses risk misalignment as a transfer rationale for True Potential, so that assertion is in scope.
    
    For True Potential: the report states a plan-level risk rating of 6 on an 'assumed' 1–10 scale and asserts this is not in line with the agreed level of 7. No passage in the evidence categories D, B, G or H corroborates the rating of 6. The fund factsheets retrieved ([P6] True Potential TrinityBridge Balanced, [P8] True Potential Allianz Balanced) do not state numeric risk ratings for those funds. The assertion therefore rests solely on the report itself — an [I] category source — which cannot corroborate itself.
    
    For Nest: the report states the fund has a risk level of 8, embedded in the combined cell 'Nest Higher Risk Fund 8'. A rating of 8 would exceed the agreed level of 7, which under the check's decision logic is the highest severity issue. However, the rating is taken from a merged table cell in the report, no independent passage corroborates it, and no scale mapping confirms comparability. The Nest Defaqto passage [P5] provides only performance data and does not state a risk level number.
    
    For Quilter: no fund-level risk rating is stated and no alignment claim is made, so no finding arises beyond noting the gap.
    
    The guards confirm that where scales are incomparable and no mapping is given, the finding is incomparability/missing evidence rather than a confirmed breach. The report does assert that the True Potential portfolio is misaligned ('not in line with the agreed risk level') — that assertion is therefore testable on its own terms, but it is supported only by the report itself. The Nest fund at rating 8 (above agreed 7) is also an unsupported assertion: the claim that the fund carries a rating of 8 is made only in the report and is not corroborated by any independent document.
    
    No vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially affect this specific assessment.
    
    The outcome is a Potential Concern: the report makes risk-alignment assertions (including misalignment for True Potential and an implicit above-profile rating for Nest) that are unsupported by any evidence outside the report itself, and the scale comparability is never established.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report asserts the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate' as a graded assessment, but no document in the case file contains a formal graded knowledge/experience assessment (e.g. none/limited/moderate/advanced) against specific product types to corroborate this grade. The risk profile report (P2) contains an 'Investor Experience' section but records no notes within it, leaving the file silent on any formal assessment of investment experience captured at that stage. The report's characterisation of knowledge as 'Moderate' is supported only by the client's own background description (accounting and currency trading) and self-reported pension checking behaviour; no independent or assessed grading appears in the supporting documents. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report states the client's knowledge and experience level is 'Moderate'. It notes that her background in accounting and currency trading has equipped her with a solid understanding of financial matters, that she has invested in pensions without taking advice within the last 5 years, and that she regularly checks the value of her funds. The report describes her as having 'a fair degree of knowledge and understanding'.
    File:     The fact find meeting summary (P6) records the client as 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters' and notes she 'does not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio'. The risk profile report (P1, P2, P11) records a risk level of 7, described as 'Growth' investor, with the risk profile section for 'Investor Experience' present but with no recorded notes (P2). The meeting summary (P5) records risk profile assessed as level 7. The suitability report itself (P24, category [I]) repeats the knowledge and experience narrative. No fact find document in the evidence contains a formal graded assessment of knowledge level (e.g. none/limited/moderate/advanced) against specific product types or investment categories. The Defaqto risk descriptor at level 9 (P3) notes suitability for someone with 'substantial experience of investments' who 'finds financial matters easy to understand', while the recommended level 8 descriptor (P14) notes suitability for someone with 'experience of investments' who 'find financial matters fairly easy to understand'. The fact find (P12) is present but contains no knowledge/experience grading fields visible in the retrieved content.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts the client's knowledge level is 'Moderate' as a graded assessment, but no document in the case file contains a formal graded knowledge/experience assessment (e.g. none/limited/moderate/advanced) against specific product types to corroborate this grade.
      - The risk profile report (P2) contains an 'Investor Experience' section but records no notes within it, leaving the file silent on any formal assessment of investment experience captured at that stage.
      - The report's characterisation of knowledge as 'Moderate' is supported only by the client's own background description (accounting and currency trading) and self-reported pension checking behaviour; no independent or assessed grading appears in the supporting documents.
      - The report states the client has 'invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years' — this is a self-reported assertion appearing only in the suitability report ([I] passages) with no corroborating fact find record visible in the retrieved evidence.
    The report asserts a 'Moderate' knowledge level for the client. The supporting narrative points to the client's professional background in accounting and currency trading and self-reported pension engagement. These are consistent with the meeting summary (P6), which records a solid financial background. However, 'Moderate' is a graded assessment, not merely a description of background; the regulatory requirement is that the knowledge level be assessed and recorded, not merely inferred from occupation. The risk profile report's 'Investor Experience' section (P2) is blank — no notes were recorded. The fact find (P12) content retrieved does not show a completed knowledge/experience grading field. The suitability report's own knowledge narrative (P24) is an [I] passage and cannot corroborate itself. The client's accounting and currency trading background is corroborated by the meeting summary (P6, category C), which is evidence. This supports the characterisation of financial literacy, but falls short of a formal assessed grading of investment product knowledge. The recommended product (a personal pension on the Aviva platform invested in diversified multi-asset and index funds at risk level 7-8) is not an especially exotic or highly complex product for someone with this professional background, and the risk profile descriptor at level 8 (P14) does refer to a client who has 'experience of investments' and finds financial matters 'fairly easy to understand', which is broadly consistent with the client's background. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics in the evidence materially affect this assessment. On balance, the knowledge level stated is plausible given the evidenced background, but the absence of a formal graded assessment in the fact find or risk profile document means the 'Moderate' grade is asserted by the report without an independent assessed grading on file to corroborate it. This is a missing evidence concern, albeit of lower severity given the consistent professional background narrative across multiple meeting summaries.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified
    Report:   The suitability report recommends an Aviva Platform Personal Pension (classified by the extractor as 'Complex') with complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown capability, pension consolidation involving multiple transfers including a Pension Sharing Order, fund selection within a platform wrapper, and a critical yield and charges comparison. The client's knowledge and experience is described as: background in accounting and currency trading equipping them with a solid understanding of financial matters; having invested in pensions without advice in the last 5 years; regularly checking fund values; and having a fair degree of knowledge and understanding. Risk profile is agreed at 7 (Growth). The recommended investment is 50% Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and 50% HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic. The report does not explicitly classify the product as 'complex' or 'non-complex'; the complexity classification is inferred by the extractor.
    File:     The Aviva Pension KFD and T&C documents confirm the product is a personal pension platform offering flexi-access drawdown, fund selection (including insured funds, structured products, investment pathways, model portfolios), capped drawdown conversion, and various death benefit options. The Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic KIIDs are on file confirming these are multi-asset funds with ongoing charges of 0.19% each and risk ratings of 4 out of 7 (SRRI). The Aviva Pension KFD confirms flexi-access drawdown, single and phased drawdown, and the full range of income options. The fact-find passages [P15] confirm accounting and currency trading background, self-directed pension investment experience without advice in the last 5 years, and regular fund monitoring. No fact-find document separate from the suitability report is evidenced in the retrieved passages — all knowledge and experience passages derive from the suitability report itself (category [I]).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not explicitly classify the product as complex or non-complex — the 'Complex' classification is the extractor's inference, not a stated conclusion in the report itself.
      - All knowledge and experience assertions (accounting background, currency trading, 5-year self-directed pension experience, regular fund monitoring) are drawn solely from [I] passages (the suitability report); no separate fact-find or client questionnaire document is present in the retrieved evidence to independently corroborate these assertions.
      - The recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic) are both rated 4/7 on the SRRI scale per the KIIDs — a standard risk rating for multi-asset funds — which is consistent with a mainstream personal pension, not an unusually complex product, but the report's complexity drivers reference flexi-access drawdown and a PSO transfer as elevating complexity without any client-specific explanation of how these features were explained to or understood by the client.
      - No record of client questions, specific explanations of the drawdown or PSO features to the client, or confirmation of comprehension of those unusual features appears in any non-[I] passage.
    The guard notes that a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not complex and product volume or value alone should not escalate findings. The two recommended funds (Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity and HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic) are standard multi-asset funds with SRRI 4/7 ratings, confirmed by KIIDs on file [P23, P18]. The Aviva Platform Personal Pension is a mainstream personal pension wrapper with flexi-access drawdown, confirmed by product literature on file [P1, P7, P24]. On this basis the underlying investments are not inherently complex, and the product is a standard pension platform. However, the specific transaction involves a PSO transfer and consolidation from multiple schemes, and flexi-access drawdown is available within the product. The report identifies these as complexity drivers, which is reasonable. The critical gap is that all knowledge and experience evidence — the accounting background, currency trading experience, self-directed pension investment without advice, and regular fund monitoring — is sourced exclusively from the suitability report ([P15], [P16], both [I] category). No separate fact-find, client questionnaire, or other supporting document in a non-[I] category independently corroborates these assertions. The report cannot corroborate itself. This means the knowledge and experience description, while plausible, is unsupported by independent file evidence. Furthermore, no passage from any non-[I] document records that the client was given a specific explanation of the drawdown options or PSO implications, or that comprehension was confirmed. Given the product involves flexi-access drawdown (an unusual feature requiring specific understanding per the severity guidance), the absence of any corroborating record of explanation or comprehension is a moderate concern. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially affect this assessment. The finding is therefore: knowledge and experience asserted in the report but not corroborated by independent file evidence; no record of client explanation or comprehension of the drawdown and PSO features in any non-report document.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The client (Melanie Anness) is recorded as having a 'Moderate' level of knowledge and experience. The recommended product is classified as 'Complex'. The report states she has a background in accounting and currency trading giving her a solid understanding of financial matters, has invested in pensions without advice in the last 5 years, regularly checks fund values, and has a fair degree of knowledge and understanding. Risk profile agreed at 7/10 (Growth investor).
    File:     Category C meeting summaries (fact find, 16/01/2026 and 20/02/2026) describe Melanie as a 'seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, which provides you with a solid understanding of financial matters' and note she has experience as a currency trader with 'a keen awareness of market dynamics', though she does not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio. The meeting summary of 18/03/2026 confirms risk profile at level 7. The Defaqto report for the existing Nest holding rates the portfolio as 'Very Adventurous' (risk 9) and the existing Nest fund as 'Higher Risk'. The fact find (category B) does not contain a graded knowledge/experience field with a specific level recorded. No category C passage recording client questions asked about, or explanations given of, the complex product's features was retrieved — Q4.4.2 returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex' and the client's knowledge as 'Moderate', but no category C record of the client demonstrating comprehension of the complex product's features (e.g. flexi-access drawdown, pension consolidation mechanics, investment pathway rejection) is present in the evidence.
      - The report states knowledge level as 'Moderate' but the fact find (category B) does not contain a graded knowledge/experience field corroborating this specific grading — the level is stated in the report but not independently graded in the file.
      - The meeting summary notes the client does not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio, which sits in mild tension with the 'Moderate' knowledge classification for a complex pension product, though her accounting and currency trading background partially offsets this.
      - The risk profile description used in the report ('Growth investor') carries preconditions referencing investment experience and ease of understanding financial matters; the file confirms the background but does not independently verify these preconditions through a knowledge assessment separate from the risk questionnaire.
    The report records the client's knowledge level as 'Moderate' and the recommended product as 'Complex'. Under the decision logic, a complex product recommended to a client with moderate knowledge is not automatically the highest-severity finding; the concern is whether the moderate level is genuinely supported and whether comprehension is evidenced. On the positive side, the category C meeting summaries corroborate the accounting and currency trading background and confirm a risk profile of 7/10, which is consistent with Growth investor characteristics. The existing Nest holding in the 'Higher Risk' / 'Very Adventurous' fund also evidences prior engagement with higher-risk products. These are real pieces of corroborating evidence. However, two gaps remain. First, the 'Moderate' grading itself: the fact find (category B) does not contain an independently graded knowledge/experience field — the grade appears only in the suitability report itself (category I passages P1), which cannot corroborate its own assertion. The meeting summaries describe the background qualitatively but do not assign a grade. This means the specific label 'Moderate' is asserted by the report and unsupported by an independent document. Second, and more materially given the product is classified Complex: the search Q4.4.2 returned nothing. There is no category C record of the adviser explaining the complex features of the recommended product — flexi-access drawdown mechanics, pension consolidation from three sources including a Pension Sharing Order, investment pathway consideration and rejection — and no record of the client asking questions or confirming understanding. The guard 'You confirmed you understand' appearing in the report (P1: 'you confirmed this is within an acceptable tolerance') is an adviser assertion, not evidence of comprehension. The vulnerability overlay is relevant here: the client is recently divorced and navigating a Pension Sharing Order for the first time, a life event that can create financial stress and complexity even for financially literate individuals. The report does not record any specific support needs or reasonable adjustments, but the absence of a comprehension record is more significant against this backdrop. The product complexity combined with the absence of an independent knowledge grade and the absence of any comprehension record produces a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the knowledge level is stated but not independently graded, and there is no corroboration of comprehension where the product warrants it.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client's background in accounting and currency trading has equipped her with a solid understanding of financial matters, and that she feels she has a fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning. No further evidence of comprehension — such as questions asked, explanations sought, or specific understanding of product features — is stated in the report beyond these two assertions.
    File:     The meeting summaries (category C) confirm the client is a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, providing a solid understanding of financial matters, and that she does not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio. The meetings show she engaged with options including flexi-access drawdown and annuities, and her risk profile was assessed at level 7. The risk profile report (category D) records that investor experience, attitude to risk and capacity for loss were discussed but records no actual notes in those sections. There is no recorded evidence in any category C document of specific questions the client asked about product features, nor any record of her demonstrating comprehension of the specific risks and features of the recommended Aviva pension with a multi-asset fund portfolio. Search Q4.5.1 returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts a 'fair degree of knowledge and understanding in relation to pension planning' but this is a self-assessment by the client with no corroborating comprehension test, questions asked, or adviser-recorded explanation in the file.
      - The risk profile report (P16) has blank/empty fields for Investor Experience notes — no substantive content is recorded there despite the section existing.
      - No record of the client asking questions about, or demonstrating comprehension of, the specific features or risks of the recommended product (Aviva pension with Vanguard LifeStrategy and HSBC funds) is present anywhere in the case file.
      - The report does not record any specific explanations given to the client about the complexity of the recommended solution, nor any client responses evidencing understanding.
    The recommended solution involves a pension consolidation into an Aviva platform with multi-asset fund investments (Vanguard LifeStrategy, HSBC funds) including flexi-access drawdown options — a product of moderate-to-high complexity involving investment risk, platform mechanics, drawdown sequencing risk, and tax considerations. The requirement under G4.5 is for evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of such a product, demonstrated through questions asked and comprehension shown. The report relies solely on (a) the client's professional background in accounting and currency trading, and (b) her self-assessed 'fair degree' of pension knowledge. The category C meeting summaries in the file corroborate the professional background claim (P1), but this corroboration establishes general financial literacy, not specific comprehension of the recommended product's features and risks. The risk profile report (P16) has empty note fields for Investor Experience — the section exists but was not populated, meaning the file is silent rather than confirmatory on this point. No category C document records the client asking specific questions about the recommended product, no adviser note records what was explained and understood, and search Q4.5.1 returned nothing. The guard states that absence of recorded questions is not proof of non-understanding, but directs that it be reported as an evidence gap weighted by product complexity. Given the moderate-to-high complexity of the recommended product, this gap is material. The background in accounting and currency trading is relevant context but is not a substitute for recorded comprehension of pension-specific product features including drawdown risk, platform charges interaction, and investment fund characteristics. The self-assessment of 'fair degree' of pension knowledge is an [I] passage assertion with no independent corroboration beyond the general financial background. Accordingly, the claim that the client's knowledge and experience are sufficient to evaluate the product's risks and features is unsupported by independent evidence in the file.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report states that the client (Melanie Anness) has invested in pensions without taking any advice within the last 5 years and regularly checks the value of her funds. Three existing pension arrangements are recorded: (1) a Quilter arrangement (plan type 'Other', current value £260,000); (2) a True Potential Personal Pension holding six named funds (Goldman Sachs Balanced, Growth Aligned Aggressive, Schroders Balanced, SEI Aggressive, Trinity Bridge Balanced, UBS Aggressive) with a combined value of approximately £46,672.80; and (3) a Nest OccupationalDC pension holding the Nest Higher Risk Fund (risk rating 8) with a current value of £21,272.64. The report implies from these holdings that the client has meaningful pension experience and investment awareness.
    File:     The fact find [P16] records under 'Money Purchase Pension Schemes': 'Do you have any existing money purchase schemes? No' — all fields blank. However, the same fact find [P16] also records under 'Personal Pensions': 'Do you have any existing Personal Pension arrangements? Yes', with an Aviva pension (policy AV2966413-001) listed under Melanie Anness. The Aviva pension illustration [P22] confirms three uncrystallised transfers into the recommended Aviva pension: £46,672.80 (True Potential), £21,272.64 (Nest), and £260,000.00 (Quilter), totalling £327,945.44 — consistent with the suitability report's existing arrangements. Fund factsheets for True Potential Goldman Sachs Balanced [P13], True Potential UBS Aggressive [P5][P6][P14][P15], True Potential Growth Aligned Aggressive [P10], and True Potential Schroders Balanced [P4] are on file, as is a Nest Higher Risk Fund document [P8][P9][P11][P12], confirming those holdings exist. The Defaqto documents [P17][P18][P19] relate to the proposed Nest/new portfolio and do not evidence the client's own prior knowledge. There is no fact find section recording the client's knowledge and experience grade, no investment questionnaire, and no record of client questions or comprehension checks in the retrieved passages. The fact find note [P1] references Nest steps and regular taxable income but does not grade knowledge or experience. The Certified PSA [P7] evidences the Pension Sharing Order (Quilter) but says nothing about the client's investment knowledge.
    Discrepancies:
      - The fact find [P16] states 'Do you have any existing money purchase schemes? No' with all fields blank, yet the suitability report records a Nest OccupationalDC pension as an existing arrangement — this is a direct inconsistency between the fact find and the suitability report on whether a money purchase/occupational DC scheme exists.
      - The suitability report asserts the client has invested in pensions without advice and regularly checks fund values, but no fact find knowledge/experience grading or investment questionnaire is present in the file to corroborate this characterisation of the client's engagement and understanding.
      - The file contains no record of a knowledge and experience assessment (grade, score or classification) for the client, meaning the report's implied knowledge level is stated but not graded in any supporting document.
    The primary holdings-based corroboration is partially supported: the Aviva illustration [P22] confirms all three transfer values (True Potential £46,672.80, Nest £21,272.64, Quilter £260,000.00), and fund factsheets for the True Potential and Nest holdings are on file, establishing that the client did hold those products. This confirms pension ownership and, to a degree, experience of holding pension and investment products. However, three concerns arise. First, the fact find [P16] records 'Do you have any existing money purchase schemes? No' — an explicit negative that directly contradicts the suitability report's record of a Nest OccupationalDC arrangement. While the Nest plan may have been auto-enrolled (in which case the guard applies: auto-enrolment evidences ownership, not investment experience), the flat negative in the fact find is a direct inconsistency with the report. Second, the report asserts that the client invested in pensions 'without taking any advice' and 'regularly checks the value of your funds' — this characterises active, informed engagement. No fact find knowledge/experience grade, investment questionnaire result, or client comprehension note is on file to corroborate this characterisation. The only [D] category passages ([P17][P18][P19]) are Defaqto analyses of the proposed portfolio, not a record of the client's own knowledge level. Third, the Quilter arrangement arrived via a Pension Sharing Order [P7], meaning it was an inherited arrangement rather than one the client actively set up or managed — per the guard, an arrangement set up by a previous adviser or received through a court order says little about the client's own experience. Taken together: holdings do partially corroborate experience of pension ownership, but the fact find contradiction on the Nest scheme and the absence of any knowledge/experience grading mean the report's assertion of active, unaided pension engagement is unsupported beyond the report itself. The severity is moderate: the knowledge level is stated in the report but not graded in the file, and one specific factual inconsistency (Nest as money purchase) exists between the fact find and the report.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The recommended product is classified as Complex, with complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown capability, pension consolidation involving multiple transfers including a Pension Sharing Order, fund selection within a platform wrapper, and critical yield and charges comparison. The suitability report provides rationales covering: lower costs (Quilter: 0.19% vs 0.27% saving £208 p.a.; True Potential platform: 0.21% vs 0.40% saving £88.68; fund charges: 0.20% vs 0.58% saving £177.35); client preference (no ties to Quilter); ongoing monitoring and active management; risk alignment (Nest fund at risk 8 vs agreed risk 7); wider fund range vs Nest's 6 funds; more flexible retirement options including flexi-access drawdown and tax-efficient withdrawal. The client is described as a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading, with solid understanding of financial matters, no current stocks and shares portfolio but awareness of market dynamics, and a risk profile of level 7.
    File:     Meeting summaries (P2, P3) corroborate that the client has a background in accounting and currency trading, providing solid understanding of financial matters, though she does not currently maintain a stocks and shares portfolio. P3 confirms the client is interested in understanding flexi-access drawdown and annuities options. P1 and P4 confirm a risk profile of level 7 and that she confirmed comfort with that level of risk. The risk profile report (P21) records the risk level agreed following interactive discussion considering attitude to risk, capacity for loss and required return, but the investor experience section contains no substantive notes recorded. The Nest Higher Risk fund factsheet (P17) confirms the Nest Higher Risk Fund exists and is in a growth phase. KFIDs and T&Cs (P6, P10, P11, P13, P14, P15) are present in the file relating to the recommended Aviva pension and Vanguard funds. No passage from the case file independently corroborates the specific charge comparison figures (0.19% vs 0.27% for Quilter; 0.21% vs 0.40% for True Potential platform; 0.20% vs 0.58% for fund charges) from a source document. The risk profiling tool's investor experience section (P21) records no notes, meaning the file holds a record that is silent on the client's prior investment and pension experience in the structured assessment.
    Discrepancies:
      - The risk profile report's Investor Experience section (P21) records no notes, yet the suitability report asserts a detailed knowledge and experience profile (accounting background, currency trading, awareness of market dynamics). The structured assessment is silent where the report is specific.
      - The specific charge savings figures cited in the rationale (£208 p.a. saving on Quilter, £88.68 saving on True Potential platform, £177.35 saving on fund charges) are not corroborated by any independent source document in the file — they appear only in the report itself.
      - No category C passage (meeting record or file note) independently records that the complexity of the recommended product — specifically flexi-access drawdown — was explained to and understood by the client, beyond the client's own expressed interest in understanding the option.
    The recommended product is correctly classified as complex, engaging multiple complexity drivers including flexi-access drawdown and pension consolidation with a Pension Sharing Order. The rationale given in the report is substantive and client-specific across multiple topics. The client's background in accounting and currency trading, evidenced in the meeting summaries (P2), provides a credible basis for the report's assertion of financial acumen. P3 confirms her expressed interest in understanding flexi-access drawdown and annuities, which aligns with the complexity of the recommendation. However, the risk profile tool's investor experience section (P21) is explicitly silent — no notes were recorded — which means the structured file record does not corroborate the detailed knowledge and experience narrative in the report. This is a gap rather than a contradiction, but it means the claim rests solely on the meeting summary category C documents. The charge comparison figures underlying the cost rationale are asserted in the report but are not independently supported by a source document such as a charges schedule or provider confirmation in the file. The meeting summaries confirm the client's comfort with risk level 7 and her interest in the options discussed, but there is no specific file note recording that the features of flexi-access drawdown — its complexity, tax implications and risks — were explained to the client in a way that demonstrates comprehension, beyond the client's own expressed interest. The absence of substantive investor experience notes in the risk profiling tool (P21) moderately weakens the corroboration of the knowledge and experience narrative. The overall rationale is present and multi-faceted, but the supporting evidence for the client's demonstrated comprehension of product complexity is partially absent from the structured record. This is a moderate concern: no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features, and the investor experience section of the risk profile is blank.

CHK-005 — Financial Resilience, Affordability and Liquidity
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G5.1, G5.2, G5.3, G5.4, G5.5, G5.7, G5.8). Cashflow model uses monthly expenditure of £2,237 (P11) but the Fact Find records total monthly expenditure of £2,986 (P17) — a £749/month difference with no explanation provided. The initial fee of £9,838.36 is stated as 3% of 'the amount invested' but 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The fee only reconciles against a combined fund total of ~£327,945 (including the Quilter PSO) which the report never explicitly names as the fee basis. The ongoing charge monetary figure of £3,181.08 is not corroborated by any passage outside the suitability report itself; 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45 (does not match); 1% of ~£318,107 (£327,945 minus £9,838 initial fee) ≈ £3,181 (consistent only if the PSO funds are included and the initial fee is deducted), but this basis is not stated.

  [G5.1] Affordability of initial and ongoing commitments
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts: (1) an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, stated in monetary terms as £9,838.36 'with current transfer values'; (2) an ongoing annual service charge of 1% p.a., monetised as circa £3,181.08 for the first year 'based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'; (3) affordability is asserted on the basis that pension funds plus inheritance will be sufficient to cover retirement expenditure even after a market fall, with the option to work beyond age 60; (4) cashflow analysis is stated to confirm assets are sufficient to make up any income shortfall throughout the client's lifetime; (5) cashflow inputs use monthly expenditure of £2,237 until retirement; (6) the client's current monthly expenditure is stated elsewhere in the report as £2,986.
    File:     The Fact Find (P14, P17, P21) records: the client is currently living off savings reduced to £7,000 (likely to fall to circa £4,000 by the time pension income is set up); she prefers a 12-month emergency reserve of circa £24,000; her current savings of £7,000 far below that preferred level; she plans a tax-free cash withdrawal of £40,000 (£20,000 to replenish emergency fund and cover legal fees; £20,000 to pay off personal loan and credit card liabilities); she has stopped working and has no employment income, living off savings; a partner contribution of £800/month towards bills; a future inheritance of £150,000 expected to clear the mortgage. The Fact Find expenditure table (P17) itemises monthly outgoings totalling approximately £2,986/month (council tax £221, electricity £120, water £42, telephone £20, food £300, PCP £440, fuel £200, car tax £1,000 annually, plus further items). The cashflow model inputs (P11) use £2,237/month expenditure until retirement — a £749/month discrepancy with the Fact Find figure. No income and expenditure reconciliation or explanation for this gap is found in the evidence. No monthly surplus calculation is presented. The emergency fund position is recorded as materially below the client's own stated preference, with depletion acknowledged as causing discomfort. The report's basis for the £9,838.36 initial fee (3% of 'amount invested') is internally inconsistent: 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36; the figure only reconciles if the PSO Quilter funds (~£260,000) are included, bringing the total to ~£327,945 and 3% thereof to ~£9,838, but the report never explicitly states this combined basis.
    Discrepancies:
      - Cashflow model uses monthly expenditure of £2,237 (P11) but the Fact Find records total monthly expenditure of £2,986 (P17) — a £749/month difference with no explanation provided.
      - The initial fee of £9,838.36 is stated as 3% of 'the amount invested' but 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The fee only reconciles against a combined fund total of ~£327,945 (including the Quilter PSO) which the report never explicitly names as the fee basis.
      - The ongoing charge monetary figure of £3,181.08 is not corroborated by any passage outside the suitability report itself; 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45 (does not match); 1% of ~£318,107 (£327,945 minus £9,838 initial fee) ≈ £3,181 (consistent only if the PSO funds are included and the initial fee is deducted), but this basis is not stated.
      - The client's emergency fund stands at £7,000 (likely to fall to ~£4,000), materially below her own stated preference of ~£24,000 (12 months' expenditure). The report does not address this gap explicitly or explain how liquidity needs are met pending the tax-free cash drawdown.
      - No monthly surplus or income-minus-expenditure calculation is presented in the report or the evidence pack to demonstrate ongoing affordability on a cash-flow basis prior to retirement.
      - The client has no employment income at the point of recommendation (living off savings); the report does not quantify available monthly income against expenditure to show the ongoing recommendation is affordable in the interim period.
    There are two distinct but related concerns here.
    
    1. Expenditure inconsistency: The cashflow model uses £2,237/month expenditure until retirement (P11), but the Fact Find records £2,986/month (P17). This £749/month discrepancy is flagged as an internal inconsistency in the extraction metadata and is not explained anywhere in the evidence pack. The severity guidance classifies cashflow inputs differing from the fact find figures as Moderate. This is a Data inconsistency finding.
    
    2. Emergency fund and liquidity: The Fact Find (P14) records that the client is currently living off savings of £7,000 (expected to fall to ~£4,000), that she prefers a 12-month reserve of ~£24,000, and that she is 'uncomfortable' with the current position. The plan to replenish the emergency fund via a £20,000 tax-free cash lump sum is noted in the Fact Find but is not addressed in the suitability report's affordability commentary. The report asserts affordability on the basis of pension funds plus inheritance sufficiency in retirement, but does not quantify or address the immediate pre-retirement liquidity gap. The severity guidance identifies 'emergency fund unquantified or depleted' as a Potential Concern, and 'emergency reserves consumed by the recommendation' as High. Here the reserves are already depleted relative to the client's own stated need before the recommendation is implemented — the recommendation does not itself consume them, but it does not address the gap either. This is a Data quality / Missing evidence concern.
    
    3. Fee basis opacity: The £9,838.36 initial fee is described as 3% of 'the amount invested' but this is only arithmetically consistent if the Quilter PSO (~£260,000) is included in the base. The report never states this. 3% of the transfer-only figure of £67,945.44 yields £2,038.36. The fee basis is therefore undisclosed, creating a Disclosure shortfall. The guards note that fees taken from the fund do not need to be tested against monthly surplus — so this is not an affordability failing in the income-vs-expenditure sense, but it is a material disclosure gap about what the client is being charged and on what base.
    
    4. The vulnerability overlay is relevant: the client has stopped working, is living off depleting savings causing acknowledged discomfort, and has ongoing legal proceedings (inheritance dispute requiring barrister fees). These vulnerability indicators heighten the importance of accurate cashflow inputs and transparent emergency fund planning. The report's affordability assertion does not engage with these circumstances in any quantified way.
    
    The fees are taken from the fund (guard applies) so they are not tested against monthly surplus. However, the combined effect of: (a) the £749/month cashflow input discrepancy, (b) the unaddressed emergency fund deficit, and (c) the absent monthly surplus calculation in a case where the client has no employment income, constitutes a material set of affordability concerns that are not resolved by the evidence.
