  [G5.2] Financial situation plus cashflow supports the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report states: total assets £447,000; total liabilities £70,000; net worth £377,000; total monthly expenditure £2,986 (Tony contributes £600/month toward household bills). Cashflow modeller inputs: income £45,000 p.a. until age 60; expenditure £2,237/month until retirement; £2,000/month in retirement; mortgage £750/month for 7 years; one-off £2,500 sofa; inheritance £350,000 'next year'; full state pension from age 67; 6% pension growth; run to age 100; 0% surplus saved. Baseline scenario: no shortfall to age 100. High-inflation stress test: funds exhausted at age 81. Market-crash stress test: no shortfall. ONS life expectancy for 55-year-old female: age 87.
    File:     Fact Find [P7]: assets include main residence £430,000, cash savings £7,000, potential inheritance £150,000 (estimated 2–3 years, maybe sooner) — total listed as £587,000. Liabilities [P7/P17]: mortgage at £600/month, interest rate 5.25%, term to age 65; two further liabilities with payments of £175 and £308/month. Expenditure [P5/P18]: itemised expenditure in the fact find includes mortgage £600/month, council tax £221, electricity £120, water £42, telephone £20, food £300, car/travelling (PCP £440, other £50, fuel £200, tax £1,000 p.a.), housekeeping, TV/satellite £61, TV licence £15, sports/recreation £41.40, holidays £3,000 p.a., entertainment £150, life assurance £30 — monthly total derivable from these items. Partner income: fact find [P18] shows 'Partner's Contribution' of £800/month (updated 21/07/2026), not £600/month as stated in the report. Fact find total assets stated as £587,000, not £447,000. Inheritance: fact find [P7] describes it as 'Potential Inheritance of £150,000… estimated to be in 2–3 years time, maybe sooner'; the report models £350,000 received 'next year'. Profile notes [P14]: July 2026 update notes Melanie has stopped working through self-employment. ONS [P23]: life expectancy for 55-year-old female is 87; 1-in-4 chance of reaching 95. Mortgage in cashflow: report inputs £750/month for 7 years; fact find states mortgage is £600/month to age 65. Report also states Tony contributes £600/month but fact find records £800/month partner contribution.
    Discrepancies:
      - Report states monthly expenditure for cashflow purposes is £2,237/month; the report itself separately states total monthly expenditure is £2,986 — a £749/month gap with no explanation.
      - Report states mortgage payment as £750/month for the next 7 years in the cashflow model; the fact find [P17] records the mortgage payment as £600/month to age 65 — both the amount and the term differ.
      - Report states Tony contributes £600/month toward household bills [P13]; the fact find [P18] records 'Partner's Contribution' as £800/month (as at 21/07/2026).
      - Report models total assets of £447,000; the fact find [P7] states Melanie's total assets as £587,000 — a £140,000 difference. The report's figure excludes the potential inheritance of £150,000 which the fact find includes (offset partly by differing values of other items).
      - Report models inheritance of £350,000 received 'next year'; the fact find [P7] describes it as a 'Potential Inheritance of £150,000… estimated to be in 2–3 years time, maybe sooner' — both the amount (£350,000 vs £150,000) and timing ('next year' vs 2–3 years) differ materially.
      - Cashflow assumption chart narrative (per extraction note) states the inheritance was assumed received in two years' time, conflicting with the main cashflow section which says 'next year'.
      - Profile notes [P14] record a July 2026 update that Melanie has stopped working through self-employment, yet the cashflow model assumes £45,000 p.a. employment income until age 60 — the income assumption may no longer reflect her current position.
      - Cashflow model does not separately show mortgage of £600/month alongside the £2,237 expenditure figure; the report lists both mortgage (£750/month for 7 years) and expenditure (£2,237) as separate cashflow line items, but the relationship between the £2,237 figure and the fact-find total of £2,986 (which already includes the £600 mortgage) is unexplained.
      - No cashflow modelling output document is evidenced in the file; the cashflow conclusions are supported only by the suitability report itself (all cashflow passages are [I]).
    Multiple material discrepancies exist between the cashflow model inputs used in the report and the figures recorded in the supporting fact find.
    
    1. EXPENDITURE DISCREPANCY (£749/month): The report states total monthly expenditure as £2,986 in the 'Your current situation' section but uses £2,237/month in the cashflow model. No reconciliation is provided. One candidate reading is that £2,237 = £2,986 − £600 (mortgage) − £149 (unknown), or that it nets off Tony's contribution; another is that it is simply an error. The extraction flags this as an unresolved ambiguity. Either way, the cashflow model uses a materially lower expenditure figure than the fact find without explanation, which means the cashflow's surplus income and hence the no-shortfall conclusion may be overstated.
    
    2. MORTGAGE INPUT: The cashflow model uses £750/month for 7 years. The fact find [P17] records the mortgage as £600/month to age 65. At age 55 with a term to age 65, that is 10 years, not 7. The report also separately lists Tony's contribution as £600/month in the narrative but £800/month in the fact find [P18]. These discrepancies mean the cashflow double-counts or mis-specifies the mortgage outgoing and mis-states the partner's contribution to household costs.
    
    3. INHERITANCE — AMOUNT AND TIMING: The cashflow models £350,000 received 'next year'. The fact find records 'Potential Inheritance of £150,000… estimated to be in 2–3 years time, maybe sooner'. Both the amount (more than double) and the timing differ. The inheritance is a key driver of the baseline no-shortfall conclusion (it represents a substantial cash injection); using the wrong amount and an earlier receipt date would materially flatter the cashflow. The extraction note also flags an internal inconsistency that the chart narrative places receipt two years away, not one.
    
    4. INCOME ASSUMPTION — POSSIBLE STALE DATA: The July 2026 fact find update [P14] records that Melanie has stopped working. The cashflow assumes £45,000 p.a. until age 60. If she is no longer earning, this assumption overstates income and the cashflow conclusions are built on a figure that the file itself suggests is no longer current. This is the most significant individual concern from a vulnerability/financial resilience perspective, though the fact find note is brief and the position may have changed again.
    
    5. ASSET TOTALS: The report's total assets of £447,000 and the fact find's £587,000 are on different bases: the fact find includes the £150,000 potential inheritance as a current asset whereas the report's balance sheet does not (the report treats it as a future cashflow item). The £430,000 main residence and £7,000 savings are consistent across both. This difference is largely explainable by the inheritance treatment, but the inheritance amount itself differs (£150,000 in fact find; £350,000 modelled), which remains a discrepancy.
    
    6. NO CASHFLOW MODELLING OUTPUT ON FILE: All cashflow passages are sourced from [I] (the suitability report itself). There is no independent cashflow tool output, schedule or annex in the evidence pack. The cashflow conclusions — including the baseline no-shortfall result and the stress tests — are therefore unsubstantiated by any document other than the report's own narrative. The report cannot corroborate itself.
    
    7. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: The profile note [P14] records that Melanie has type 2 diabetes and that as at July 2026 she has stopped working, which reduces her financial resilience. The cashflow's income assumption (£45,000 until 60) does not reflect this. Where a client has potential health-related vulnerability and a recent material change in income, the accuracy of cashflow inputs is heightened in importance from a suitability perspective.
    
    The guards do not resolve these issues. The mortgage amount difference (£750 vs £600) is not a growth-assumption difference. The inheritance amount difference (£350,000 vs £150,000) is not a chart-parsing ambiguity. The expenditure discrepancy is an identified internal inconsistency in the report itself. The income staling is a file-level finding, not a rounding difference. Multiple inputs that drive the affordability conclusion are inconsistent with the fact find, and no independent cashflow output is on file to validate them.

  [G5.3] Emergency fund and liquidity needs, foreseeable and unforeseeable
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts the client has £17,000 in liquid cash which is sufficient for emergency/immediate access needs during the pension transfer period. It states the client is 'comfortable with the concept as you have liquid cash should you require instance access funds.' No explicit required amount is stated. The report notes that pension funds will take 10–15 working days to access. The emergency fund amount is inferred (not explicitly earmarked) as the £17,000 cash holding. No quantified emergency fund requirement is stated in the report.
    File:     The fact find (P17) corroborates the £17,000 cash holding. However, P15 (Fact Find notes) relates to a different client scenario (Melanie Anness) where cash reserves are described as reduced to £7,000 and the client prefers 12 months of expenditure (circa £24,000) as an emergency reserve, with the £7,000 likely to fall to circa £4,000 by the time pension income is set up. This passage is from the same fact find document and indicates a significantly different liquidity position from another client or scenario recorded in the same file. The fact find (P17) shows total monthly expenditure of £2,836.23 and total net monthly income of only £800, giving a monthly deficit of -£2,036.23. The cashflow modeller inputs in P1 use £2,237/month expenditure until retirement, which differs from the £2,836.23 in P17 (and the £2,986 stated in the report's 'current situation' section per the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction). There is no separate emergency fund assessment or quantified requirement in the evidence. The £17,000 cash is the only liquid asset identified. With a monthly deficit of over £2,000, the £17,000 cash reserve would be depleted in approximately 8 months if used to cover living costs, yet this is not addressed. P15 also references competing claims on cash: a £40,000 TFC withdrawal to replenish emergency funds and cover legal fees, and £20,000 to repay personal loan and credit card liabilities — but this appears to relate to Melanie Anness rather than the primary client (Tony), creating ambiguity about which client this case concerns and whether the cash position is correctly characterised.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts £17,000 cash is sufficient for emergency/liquidity needs with no quantified requirement; P15 references a client preference for 12 months expenditure (circa £24,000) as emergency reserve and describes cash as reduced to £7,000 (falling to ~£4,000), which materially contradicts a £17,000 sufficient-cash assertion if these relate to the same client scenario.
      - Monthly expenditure in cashflow modeller inputs (P1: £2,237/month) differs from fact find total monthly expenditure (P17: £2,836.23/month) and the report's own 'current situation' figure of £2,986/month — a discrepancy of up to £749/month with no explanation.
      - The fact find (P17) shows a monthly deficit of -£2,036.23 (income £800, expenditure £2,836.23), meaning the £17,000 cash reserve would be consumed in approximately 8 months at current run-rate; the report does not address this depletion risk.
      - The report does not quantify a required emergency fund amount, making the sufficiency assertion unverifiable.
      - P15 references competing claims on the same cash pool (TFC withdrawal to replenish reserves, legal fees, loan/CC repayment) which, if applicable to this client, would materially affect the liquidity position — but the client identity in P15 is ambiguous relative to the primary client in this suitability report.
    The report asserts £17,000 cash is sufficient for emergency and immediate access needs. The figure itself is corroborated in the fact find (P17 confirms £17,000 cash). However, the sufficiency assertion is unsupported and potentially contradicted by several pieces of evidence. First, the fact find (P17) records a monthly deficit of -£2,036.23, meaning the £17,000 would be exhausted in roughly 8 months if used to fund living costs — a material depletion risk the report does not address. Second, P15 (within the same fact find document) records a client with cash reduced to £7,000 (falling to ~£4,000) and a stated preference for £24,000 emergency reserve; if this relates to the same client or scenario, it directly contradicts the £17,000 sufficient-cash assertion. The passage identifies the client as 'Melanie Anness' which matches the fact find client name in P22, creating significant ambiguity about whether the suitability report is for Tony or Melanie, or whether P15 represents an updated position not reflected in the report. Third, there is a material and unexplained £749/month discrepancy between cashflow inputs (£2,237) and fact find expenditure (£2,836.23), with a further difference against the report's own stated figure of £2,986. This undermines the reliability of any affordability and cashflow assessment built on those inputs. The guard on unquantified sufficiency applies: the absence of a required amount means the sufficiency claim cannot be verified against a target — but the claim is still unsupported by the file. The guard on competing claims on cash is directly engaged: P15 records the same cash pool as earmarked for TFC replenishment of reserves, legal fees, and liability repayment. The vulnerability overlay is relevant: P15 describes a client who is 'living off savings' with a declining cash reserve, feeling 'uncomfortable' — if this is the same client, the emergency fund position may be more precarious than the report acknowledges, and any vulnerability associated with financial stress is not addressed in the report's liquidity discussion.

  [G5.4] Initial and ongoing commitments are unaffordable based on income, expenditure and cashflow
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts affordability on the basis of: (1) an initial advice fee of 3% = £9,838.36; (2) ongoing annual advice/service charge of 1% = circa £3,181.08 for the first year; (3) capacity for loss described as moderate with pension funds and inheritance sufficient to cover retirement expenditure; and (4) cashflow analysis confirming assets are sufficient to make up income shortfall throughout the client's lifetime. Monthly expenditure is stated at £2,986 in the current situation section but £2,237 in the cashflow modeller inputs. No monthly surplus/deficit figure is explicitly stated. The report notes the client can work beyond age 60 or continue part-time if necessary.
    File:     The fact find (P1, P21) records detailed monthly expenditure items totalling approximately £2,986/month when annualised items are converted, including £600/month mortgage, £440 PCP, £200 fuel, £300 food, £221 council tax, £120 electricity, £150 entertainment and others. The fact find records a partner contribution of £800/month as income (P1) but the client's own income is not quantifiable from the passages provided — P17 shows the income fields are blank in the fact find. The meeting summary (P18) states the client's prior annual income was approximately £45,000 (~£2,900 net/month) but she had recently left employment and was in a transitional phase. P19 records credit card debt of £7,000 and personal loan of £13,000, and a desired cash buffer of 12 months of expenses. P24 records projected withdrawals of £1,900/month from the pension. The cashflow modeller input uses £2,237/month expenditure (internal inconsistency noted in extraction). No net income figure, no monthly surplus calculation, and no emergency fund quantum appear in the evidence passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's cashflow modeller uses £2,237/month expenditure but the report's own current situation section states £2,986/month — a £749/month difference with no explanation provided.
      - The initial fee of £9,838.36 is stated as 3% of the amount invested, but 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The figure is only reconcilable if the PSO funds (~£260,000) are included, giving 3% × ~£327,945 ≈ £9,838, but this combined basis is never stated in the report.
      - The ongoing annual commitment of £3,181.08 is described as 1% but 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45, not £3,181.08. The figure is broadly consistent with 1% of ~£327,945 net of the initial fee (~£318,107 × 1% = £3,181), but again the combined basis is not explicitly stated.
      - Net monthly income for the client is not evidenced in the passages — fact find income fields are blank (P17) and the client had recently left employment (P18), meaning the income base for affordability cannot be verified.
      - No monthly surplus/deficit figure appears in the report or the evidence, so ongoing commitment affordability against evidenced surplus cannot be tested.
      - Emergency fund quantum is not stated — the meeting summary notes the client wanted a 12-month buffer (P19) but the report does not quantify or confirm that this has been established.
      - The £9,838.36 initial fee figure is not corroborated by any supporting document — it does not match the evidenced transfer value basis.
      - The £3,181.08 ongoing fee figure is not corroborated by any supporting document.
    NOT ASSESSED — the comparison needs a value that was not available: Client's current net monthly income (fact find income fields blank; client recently left employment — no verified income figure in the evidence), Monthly surplus/deficit figure (absent from both report and evidence), Basis for cashflow expenditure input of £2,237/month versus fact find figure of £2,986/month — no explanation provided, Emergency fund current balance or confirmation it has been established, Confirmation of whether the client has returned to work and at what income level at time of recommendation
    The comparison between ongoing commitment and evidenced monthly surplus cannot be performed because no verified net monthly income figure is present in the evidence — the fact find income fields are blank (P17) and the client had recently left employment at the time of the meeting (P18). Without an income figure, no surplus can be computed and the affordability assertion cannot be tested against evidence. This is a missing evidence finding, not a calculation error by the pipeline.
    
    Beyond the missing income figure, there are two material internal inconsistencies that are themselves findings. First, the expenditure figure used in the cashflow modeller (£2,237/month) differs from the figure stated in the report's own current situation section (£2,986/month) by £749/month. No explanation is given. The guard that employment income due to cease should be excluded from surplus does not explain an expenditure discrepancy. The meeting summary (P18) notes a £600/month mortgage and P21 confirms this; one candidate reading is that the cashflow input strips out the mortgage (which will be settled by inheritance) and/or Tony's contribution, but this is not stated and is speculation — the discrepancy stands as a finding.
    
    Second, the initial fee of £9,838.36 is arithmetically inconsistent with '3% of the amount invested' unless the PSO funds are included in the base, but the report never states this combined basis. The figure check confirms £9,838.36 is unmatched against any passage value. Similarly, £3,181.08 at 1% implies a fund value of ~£318,107, which is consistent with the total of all arrangements net of the initial fee, but the basis is unstated. These are disclosed under CHK-007 primarily but they also bear on affordability: the client is committed to a fee of £9,838.36 upfront, and the basis of that commitment is opaque.
    
    The emergency fund is not quantified. The meeting summary records that the client wanted a 12-month buffer (P19) and that Morgan emphasised 3-6 months, but the report does not confirm whether a sufficient liquid reserve has been established or what its value is. This is an unquantified emergency fund assertion.
    
    On vulnerability: the meeting summary (P18) records that the client is 55, recently left employment, in a financial transitional phase, managing credit card and personal loan debts (£7,000 and £13,000), and anticipating a complex inheritance. These circumstances heighten the importance of robust affordability evidence. The fact that income is unverified and the cashflow input is unexplained is more concerning given this context.
    
    Affordability is asserted but the cashflow inputs contradict the fact find figures, the income base is unverified, the emergency fund is unquantified, and the basis for the initial fee quantum is unstated. This is a Potential Concern of moderate-to-high severity.

  [G5.5] Emergency funds not taken into account, or depleted or unavailable when required
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report asserts that liquidity needs are funded from 'Cash savings (£17,000)' and 'Aviva pension, state pension from age 67'. It notes the client has liquid cash should instant access be required, and that pension fund access takes 10–15 working days. The report states monthly expenditure is £2,986 (Tony contributes £600), and uses £2,237/month in the cashflow modeller. The cashflow shows an emergency fund line item of £20,000 in the graphical output. No quantified emergency fund shortfall or sufficiency analysis is stated in the extracted model.
    File:     P5 (suitability report [I]) confirms cash savings of £17,000 and monthly expenditure of £2,986, with Tony contributing £600. P2 ([I]) states 'you have liquid cash should you require instant access funds' in the context of the 10–15 working day pension access timeline. P3 ([I]) shows the cashflow chart includes an 'Emergency funds' line at £20,000. The fact find [P10] does not separately evidence the £17,000 cash savings or provide a savings/emergency fund figure. No fact find or file note passage independently corroborates the £17,000 cash savings figure or its sufficiency. No passage in the evidence addresses whether £17,000 constitutes an adequate emergency fund relative to the client's expenditure or commitments. The cashflow modeller inputs in P4 ([I]) use £2,237/month expenditure rather than the £2,986 stated in the current situation section, a difference of £749/month that is unexplained. No vulnerability-related factors appear to affect emergency fund sufficiency materially from the retrieved passages.
    Discrepancies:
      - The cashflow modeller uses £2,237/month expenditure but the current situation section states £2,986/month — a £749/month unexplained difference that affects the adequacy of the cashflow and affordability assessment.
      - The emergency fund is referenced as £17,000 in cash savings, but the cashflow chart labels an 'Emergency funds' figure of £20,000 — these figures are not reconciled in the report.
      - The £17,000 cash savings / emergency fund assertion is supported only by report passages ([I]); no independent fact find, file note or other evidence corroborates it.
      - The report asserts the emergency fund is sufficient ('you have liquid cash') but does not quantify it as sufficient relative to expenditure (roughly 5.7 months at £2,986, or 7.6 months at £2,237) — no sufficiency analysis is provided.
      - No emergency fund shortfall assessment is present in the extracted model or any retrieved passage.
    The central issue is twofold. First, the cashflow modeller inputs use £2,237/month expenditure against the £2,986/month stated in the financial position section. This is a documented internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction metadata. If the correct expenditure figure is £2,986, the cashflow projections understate outgoings by £749/month, which could materially affect the adequacy conclusion. This falls within the Moderate severity band per the guidance (cashflow inputs differ from fact find figures). Second, the emergency fund is asserted as adequate — 'you have liquid cash should you require instant access' — but is never quantified as sufficient against expenditure. The £17,000 cash figure appears only in [I] passages; no independent document corroborates it. The cashflow chart references £20,000 as an 'Emergency funds' line without reconciling this to the £17,000 cash savings. At £2,986/month expenditure this represents approximately 5.7 months of expenditure, and at £2,237/month approximately 7.6 months, but the report performs no such analysis. The guard regarding pension transfers not depleting an emergency fund applies here — the transfer itself does not consume the £17,000 — but the concern is the absence of corroboration and the failure to address sufficiency explicitly. No new capital is being committed from cash savings, so the emergency fund is not being directly depleted by the recommendation. However, the unexplained expenditure discrepancy in the cashflow modeller means the affordability and cashflow outputs may be unreliable, and the emergency fund is asserted as sufficient without evidenced quantification. These together constitute a Potential Concern at Moderate severity. No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that materially alter this assessment.

  [G5.8] Planned future expenditure and foreseeable financial needs
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report identifies six foreseeable changes/needs: (1) change from employed to self-employed, loss of employee benefits including pension contributions, Nest plan becomes paid up; (2) expected inheritance of £350,000 from sale of late mother's property, pending legal proceedings; (3) intended retirement at age 60 with possible part-time work; (4) receipt of full State Pension from age 67 at £230.21 gross per week; (5) inheritance will take estate above IHT threshold of £500,000 creating potential IHT liability; (6) from 6 April 2027, unused DC pensions and death benefits included in taxable estate for IHT. For liquidity: client has £17,000 cash savings for immediate access; pension access from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028); 10–15 working days for administration on pension access.
    File:     The meeting summary (P2) records the client has approximately £17,000 in savings but plans to use £2,500 to pay off sofas charged to a credit card, leaving an effective cash buffer of approximately £14,500. P2 also records the inheritance as a quarter share of a property valued at approximately £1.4 million, with the client estimating they might 'eventually receive at least £150,000' — not the £350,000 stated in the suitability report — and the timeline is uncertain due to ongoing legal matters. P4 records the client rarely has surplus funds at month end despite income slightly exceeding outgoings, and confirms the £17,000 savings buffer and the plan to use £2,500 for sofas. P6 confirms estate planning activity and awareness of the potential inheritance from the late mother's property valued at approximately £1.4 million. P3 confirms the client's interest in drawdown flexibility and retirement planning. P5 confirms state pension forecast was to be provided by the client. No state pension forecast document is present in the evidence pack. P8 references a different client (Melanie) and is not directly relevant to the foreseeable needs of this client. No cashflow model output or affordability assessment is in evidence that reconciles the inheritance figure or the retirement income gap.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the expected inheritance is £350,000 (from sale of late mother's property). The meeting summary (P2) records the client estimated receiving 'at least £150,000' as their quarter share of a property valued at approximately £1.4 million, with an uncertain timeline — materially lower than the £350,000 figure in the report.
      - The suitability report treats the £17,000 as the full cash reserve available for emergency access. The meeting summary (P2/P4) records the client planned to use £2,500 of those savings to pay off credit card debt for sofas, reducing the effective liquid reserve to approximately £14,500. The report does not reflect this planned reduction.
      - The state pension is stated at £230.21 gross per week from age 67, but no state pension forecast document is present in the evidence pack to corroborate this figure, despite P5 noting the client was to send a screenshot of their state pension forecast.
      - The cashflow model inputs use £2,237 per month expenditure (noted in the extraction as an internal inconsistency) versus £2,986 per month stated in the financial position section — a £749/month gap with no explanation. This affects the reliability of any affordability or foreseeable-needs modelling.
      - The report records the inheritance as pending legal proceedings. The meeting summary (P2) is more specific: the mother's ex-partner still resides in the property, the proceedings are ongoing, and the timeline is uncertain — contextual detail not reflected in the report's characterisation.
    The most material discrepancy is the inheritance figure. The suitability report states £350,000; the contemporaneous meeting summary records the client's own estimate as 'at least £150,000' from a quarter share of a £1.4 million property. A quarter of £1.4 million is £350,000 gross, but the client qualified this as 'at least £150,000' given the complexity and legal uncertainty — suggesting the report has adopted the gross mathematical share rather than the client's own cautious estimate, without explaining the basis. This is a potentially material data inconsistency in a foreseeable financial need. The cash reserve position is also slightly overstated in the report: £17,000 is cited as the liquid emergency buffer, but the client confirmed in the meeting that £2,500 of this would be used to clear credit card debt, making the actual available reserve approximately £14,500. While this does not eliminate the emergency fund, the report should reflect the net position. The state pension figure of £230.21 per week is unverified — P5 records the client was asked to provide their state pension forecast but no such document appears in the pack, so the figure is asserted without evidential support. The expenditure discrepancy (£2,237 vs £2,986/month) was flagged as an internal inconsistency in the extraction; this directly undermines the reliability of any cashflow or foreseeable-needs modelling the report relies on. On the vulnerability overlay: the client has recently divorced, changed employment status, is dealing with a deceased mother's estate involving legal proceedings, and is navigating a pension sharing order. These are cumulative life events that could affect financial resilience. The report notes the employment change and inheritance but does not address the combined pressure these events place on liquidity or the uncertainty of the inheritance timeline — which is a foreseeable financial need that is inadequately quantified. The guard on aspirational versus committed needs is noted: the inheritance is aspirational/uncertain, which makes the report's use of £350,000 as a stated figure — without caveating it as uncertain — a disclosure shortfall rather than a confirmed fact. The guard on undated plans does not rescue the inheritance inconsistency because the issue is not the absence of a date but the mismatch in the quantum. Overall, the requirement is not fully met: there are material inconsistencies between the report's characterisation of the inheritance and the client's own estimate, the cash reserve is overstated, the state pension is unverified, and the expenditure inputs used for foreseeable needs modelling are internally inconsistent.

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report provides rationale for each of the five recommendation groups (REC1–REC5). For the Aviva wrapper/provider: Personal Pension most suitable for retirement objectives, Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM, access to 6,900+ funds. Cashflow analysis shows pension funds plus inheritance sufficient to cover retirement expenditure. Additional rationale covers nomination form, Will update and Power of Attorney.     File:     Meeting summary [P1][P2] confirms risk profile agreed at level 7, investment horizon 5–10 years, and that the purpose of the follow-up meeting was to present pension recommendations.     Discrepancies:
      - The report states the Nest fund is 'risk 8' against agreed risk 7, but the switch analysis documents [P3][P4] are for Nest only and do not separately confirm the fund risk level; the risk profile report [P11] notes sections are blank with no corroborating detail recorded.

      - The report claims cashflow analysis shows sufficiency even after a market fall; the cashflow inputs use £2,237/month expenditure whereas the financial position section states £2,986/month — a £749/month discrepancy noted in the extraction as an internal inconsistency — and no explanation is given in the rationale.

      - The gap analysis (/needsAnalysis/gapAnalysis) is absent, noted by the extractor as NotPresentInDocument, meaning the link between the recommended strategy and the retirement income objective (£25,000–£30,000 p.a.) is not evidenced by a quantified gap analysis.
      - The SAP Nest analysis [P4] shows a Reduction in Yield of 1.5–1.6% (all charges) and effect on fund of -31.35% if moved to Aviva; the report's rationale presents the transfer positively without addressing this material adverse critical yield outcome in the client-specific rationale.
      - Three rationale bullets are flagged isClientSpecific=false by the extraction (active monitoring, Aviva rating, fund range) and read as generic provider/product statements.
    The report contains individualised rationale for the main recommendations and links them to named client objectives and personal circumstances (employment change, PSO, risk misalignment, retirement age target). Several bullets name the client's actual figures and plan-specific facts, satisfying the client-specific test on their face. However, a number of material weaknesses exist. First, the charge comparisons underpinning the Quilter and True Potential transfer rationale (0.27% Quilter platform, 0.40%/0.58% True Potential charges) are unsupported by any independent document in the pack: no research, provider comparison or fact-find extract corroborates them. The report cannot corroborate itself. Second, no alternatives to Aviva are considered or rejected anywhere in the report or file — the decision logic flags this as a High concern where research shows options were compared, and the SAP documents confirm research was conducted. Third, the SAP Nest analysis [P4] (all charges scenario) shows a critical yield requiring 4.17% at the 2.94% growth assumption and a -31.35% fund effect at switch; the client-specific rationale for the Nest transfer does not address this adverse outcome, which is a material omission in a switch rationale. Fourth, the cashflow rationale claims sufficiency in retirement but the cashflow inputs use £2,237/month rather than the stated £2,986/month expenditure — an unexplained £749/month difference that undermines the reliability of the cashflow conclusion used as a rationale bullet. Fifth, the gap analysis is absent, so the link between the recommended strategy and the quantified retirement income objective (£25,000–£30,000 p.a. at age 60) is not formally evidenced. The guard on client-specific language is satisfied for the majority of bullets; however, the absence of corroborating evidence for the charge figures, the absence of alternatives, the unaddressed adverse critical yield and the cashflow discrepancy each independently constitute Potential Concerns. The vulnerability overlay: the file records no specific vulnerability that materially affects this assessment. Severity is assessed as High given that: no alternatives are presented despite the SAP documents evidencing a comparison was conducted, the adverse Nest critical yield is unaddressed in the rationale, and key charge figures are unsupported by evidence.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

    Report:   The report recommends the Aviva Platform Personal Pension (wrapper: personal pension) for client C1, funded by transfer of £67,945.44 from True Potential and Nest (Quilter PSO separately). Alternatives considered were: leave existing untouched, leave and switch funds, switch into another existing arrangement, switch into employer scheme, or set up new plan — with Option 5 (new Aviva plan) selected.     The report provides a structured alternatives analysis (five options, Option 5 selected) and fund-level research is evidenced by the SAP documents [P2]-[P5] and [P13], which show performance data for HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity — the two recommended funds.   [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report presents five numbered options (leave untouched; in-situ fund switch; consolidate into existing; switch to employer scheme; set up new plan) and recommends Option 5 — a new Aviva pension — for each existing arrangement. For the Quilter PSO: lower platform cost (0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 p.a.), no client preference to remain with Quilter, and ability for adviser to monitor actively. For Nest: higher charges, limited fund range, no servicing adviser permitted on Nest, risk profile not aligned. For True Potential: higher charges, limited fund range, risk profile not aligned. A stakeholder pension is rejected as less cost-effective and lacking flexible access/fund range. Investment pathways are rejected as too generic, not reflecting individual withdrawal requirements, risk profile or review service. Options 3 and 4 are listed but carry no individual rejection reasons in the canonical model. The gap analysis is absent from the document. Monthly income is absent from the document. The report also contains an internal inconsistency: monthly expenditure stated as £2,986 in the financial position section but £2,237 used in cashflow modelling inputs, with no explanation given.
    File:     The case file contains: (a) three SelectAPension switch analysis documents (all charges ex existing OAC; IAC only ex existing OAC; no charges ex existing OAC) plus one including existing OAC, which compare projected fund values and charges between the existing plans (True Potential and Nest) and Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice); (b) an FE fund research report covering risk 7 funds; (c) an FE scatter chart comparing the proposed blended portfolio against Nest Higher Risk and True Potential on 5-year performance and volatility. The SAP analyses show Aviva outperforming existing plans on a no-charges and all-charges-ex-existing-OAC basis (e.g. £151,000 vs £134,613 at 2.94% growth in the no-charges scenario). The scatter chart shows the proposed Risk 7 blend (59.29% cumulative, 8.63% volatility) slightly outperforming Nest Higher Risk (59.28%, 8.50%) and materially outperforming True Potential (52.25%, 8.45%) over 5 years to March 2026. No research document in the file addresses Options 3 or 4 (consolidate into existing arrangement or switch to employer scheme). No document in the file provides a gap analysis linking client income/expenditure to retirement income needs. The SAP analyses cover only True Potential and Nest; the Quilter PSO is not included in any switch charges comparison document in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - Options 3 (consolidate into existing) and 4 (switch to employer scheme) are listed in the report as considered alternatives but carry no rejection reasons in the report or the research file — the check requires reasons, not just a list.
      - The Quilter PSO is included in the recommendation but absent from all switch charges analysis documents (SAP); the report's cost saving claim of £208 p.a. (0.19% vs 0.27%) for the Quilter transfer is not corroborated by any research document in the file.
      - The gap analysis (/needsAnalysis/gapAnalysis) is absent from the document — no link between the client's income/expenditure and her retirement income needs is demonstrated in the report or evidenced in the file.
      - Monthly expenditure stated as £2,986 in the financial position section but £2,237 used as cashflow modelling input — a £749/month discrepancy with no explanation in the report or supporting file.
      - The stakeholder pension rejection reason ('more cost effective') is generic and not linked to a specific cost comparison for this client; no document in the file demonstrates a cost comparison against a stakeholder pension.
      - OPT2 (in-situ fund switch) rejection reasons reference fund range and risk profile alignment but do not address charges quantitatively for the in-situ option, whereas the SAP documents only compare transfer scenarios.
    The report presents a structured alternatives analysis covering five options and two product-type alternatives (stakeholder pension and investment pathways). For the Nest and True Potential switches, the SAP research documents in the file corroborate the existence of a charges and projection comparison, and the FE scatter chart supports the performance and risk claims. These elements provide adequate evidential backing for those two recommendations. However, three material gaps remain. First, Options 3 and 4 are listed without any rejection reason in either the report or the research file. The check method requires individual reasons, not merely listing; a guard explicitly states that options listed without individual rejection reasons are a partial answer. Second, the Quilter PSO recommendation — which involves the largest single fund (£260,000) — is not covered by any switch analysis document in the file. The report asserts a specific saving of £208 p.a. based on a charge differential of 0.08%, but no SAP or equivalent document in the file corroborates this figure or any projection comparison for the Quilter transfer. This is the most material gap: the largest component of the recommended transaction has no research corroboration. Third, the gap analysis is absent, so the report does not demonstrate that the recommended accumulation strategy is calibrated to a quantified retirement income need — an absence flagged by the extraction's own expected-but-absent list and relevant to whether the overall strategy rationale is client-specific. The cashflow expenditure discrepancy (£2,986 vs £2,237) compounds this concern because it means the cashflow modelling inputs may not accurately reflect the client's financial position; no explanation is given in either the report or the file. The stakeholder pension rejection is generic (no cost comparison document exists in the file for that alternative). The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would affect the suitability assessment here (the vulnerability detail path is absent per the extraction). The most serious issue is the absence of any research document for the Quilter PSO transfer, given the size of that fund relative to the others. Options 3 and 4 lacking reasons is a secondary but clear gap. The cashflow discrepancy and absent gap analysis are supporting concerns. Taken together, the file does not fully support the recommendation rationale as presented.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Three existing arrangements are identified: (1) Pension Sharing Order (Quilter, £260,000) — recommended outcome: FullTransfer, rationale: lower platform cost (0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 p.a.), client expressed no preference to stay with Quilter, and ongoing monitoring capability. (2) True Potential Personal Pension (£46,672.80) — recommended outcome: FullTransfer, rationale: lower platform charges (Aviva 0.21% vs TP 0.40%, saving £88.68), lower fund charges (0.20% vs 0.58%, saving £177.35), risk misalignment of current portfolio, and ongoing monitoring. (3) Nest Occupational Pension (£21,272.64) — recommended outcome: FullTransfer, rationale: current fund not in line with agreed risk level, limited fund range (6 funds), lower cost structure, and ongoing monitoring. All rationales are flagged isClientSpecific: true and linked to named objectives (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3). The report also contains a cashflow modelling section with £2,237/month expenditure input, though the financial position section states £2,986/month. No gap analysis is present in the report. The Aviva platform charge used in the rationale for True Potential transfer is stated as 0.21% but the charges section states 0.19%.
    File:     Switch analysis documents (G-category) confirm the True Potential transfer value of £46,672.80 and Nest transfer value. Three Selectapension switch analyses exist: (a) 'All charges ex existing OAC' — Aviva projected fund lower than existing schemes (£106,000 vs £134,612 at 2.94%), with critical yield of 3.7% (+0.76%) and reduction in fund of -20.75%; (b) 'IAC only ex existing OAC' — Aviva projects higher fund (£146,000 vs £134,612 at 2.94%), with critical yield of 2.67% (-0.27%) and positive fund effect of +8.97%; (c) 'No charges ex existing OAC' — Aviva projected fund £151,000 vs existing £134,612 at 2.94%. Fund factsheets confirm True Potential existing funds include Schroders Balanced (risk: Balanced, OCF 1.19%), SEI Aggressive (OCF 0.96%), and UBS Aggressive. The Aviva recommended fund switch analysis uses HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio Accumulation C (ongoing 0.21%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity (0.22%). Meeting summary confirms risk profile assessed at level 7 (growth-oriented). Fact find notes reference Nest tax-free cash withdrawal and regular income requirements. No corroborating document confirms the Quilter PSO charge of 0.27% independently. The Quilter PSO is absent from the Selectapension switch charges analysis (only True Potential and Nest are assessed).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report rationale for the Aviva platform charge when comparing to True Potential states 0.21%, but the charges section and derived figures show the negotiated Aviva platform charge as 0.19% — two different figures appear for the Aviva platform charge within the report itself.
      - The Quilter PSO (£260,000) is absent from the Selectapension switch charges analysis; only True Potential and Nest are assessed. No like-for-like charges comparison document exists in the file for the largest single arrangement being transferred.
      - The 'All charges including existing OAC' scenario shows Aviva projected fund (£73,200) lower than existing schemes (£134,612) at 2.94% growth, which is materially worse; the report does not acknowledge or explain this scenario.
      - The cashflow modelling input uses £2,237/month but the financial position states £2,986/month — a £749/month discrepancy with no explanation in the report; this internal inconsistency is relevant to whether the suitability rationale is grounded in accurate client circumstances.
      - No gap analysis is present in the report (/needsAnalysis/gapAnalysis absent per extraction metadata), meaning the rationale does not demonstrate how the recommended strategy closes an identified retirement income gap.
      - The True Potential existing fund factsheets show OCF of 1.19% (Schroders Balanced) and 0.96% (SEI Aggressive), yet the switch analysis uses a fund charge of 0.58% for the existing True Potential arrangement — the basis for the 0.58% figure is not reconciled against the fund factsheets in the file.
    All three existing arrangements carry a stated recommended outcome (FullTransfer) and multiple rationale points linked to named objectives. The rationale is personalised — it references specific charge percentages, saving amounts, risk misalignment, and the client's expressed preference. The structure of the recommendation is adequate at a surface level.
    
    However, several material concerns arise on closer examination:
    
    1. QUILTER PSO — ABSENT FROM SWITCH ANALYSIS: The largest arrangement (£260,000, approximately 79% of total funds) has no Selectapension switch charges analysis in the file. The report asserts a saving of £208 p.a. based on 0.27% vs 0.19% platform charges, but no independent document corroborates the Quilter platform charge of 0.27%. This is the most material gap: the rationale for the largest transfer rests solely on the report's own assertion of lower costs, unverified by any evidence in the file.
    
    2. AVIVA PLATFORM CHARGE INCONSISTENCY: The report states 0.21% when comparing to True Potential but 0.19% as the negotiated rate elsewhere. These are internal inconsistencies within the report itself (both from [I] passages). The 0.19% figure is supported by derived arithmetic. The 0.21% figure in the True Potential rationale is unexplained — it may reflect a different tiering or a rounding, but the report does not explain it.
    
    3. SWITCH ANALYSIS — 'ALL CHARGES INCLUDING OAC' SCENARIO: The scenario including all charges shows Aviva materially underperforming the existing arrangement. The report does not acknowledge this scenario or explain why the relevant comparison excludes ongoing advice charges. While it is legitimate to compare on a like-for-like basis (ex-OAC), the report should acknowledge the scenario where charges including OAC are considered, as the client will bear them.
    
    4. EXPENDITURE DISCREPANCY: The cashflow model uses £2,237/month vs £2,986/month in the financial position section. This is an internal inconsistency that undermines confidence in the suitability rationale being grounded in accurate client data.
    
    5. EXISTING FUND CHARGES: The True Potential existing fund factsheets show OCFs of 0.96%–1.19%, yet the switch analysis benchmarks the existing fund charge at 0.58%. If the actual fund charges are higher, the switch analysis understates the cost saving — paradoxically making the rationale more favourable than documented — but the discrepancy is unexplained and reduces the transparency of the analysis.
    
    6. NO GAP ANALYSIS: The extraction confirms /needsAnalysis/gapAnalysis is absent. Without a gap analysis, the report cannot demonstrate that the recommended strategy is sufficient to meet the client's retirement income objective.
    
    VULNERABILITY: The fact find notes reference specific income needs (£1,900 regular taxable income) and tax-free cash requirements post-Nest consolidation. The report rationale does not address whether these income needs are met by the recommended strategy, nor whether the consolidation into Aviva preserves the flexibility to draw income in the required pattern. This is a potential gap in client-specific rationale for a client with specific income needs.
    
    CONCLUSION: Rationale exists for all three arrangements but the Quilter PSO rationale is unsupported by any corroborating evidence in the file, the internal charge inconsistency is unexplained, and the absence of a gap analysis means the report cannot demonstrate suitability against the client's retirement income objective. These are material concerns warranting a Potential Concern finding at Moderate severity, elevated toward High by the absence of any switch analysis for the largest transfer.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome
  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified
    Report:   Five recommendations are made: (1) Set up a new Aviva Platform pension, transfer True Potential and Nest pensions into it, and receive the Quilter PSO funds — status 'Recommended', adviser will implement, intended outcome is consolidation onto a single platform with lower charges, wider fund range, flexible retirement options and active adviser oversight to build retirement funds by age 60 with income of £25,000–£30,000 p.a. (2) Consider commencing regular contributions to the new Aviva pension — status 'Suggested', implementation responsibility 'NotStated', intended outcome is building retirement funds as a self-employed person. (3) Complete the nomination of beneficiaries form — status 'Recommended', implementation responsibility 'NotStated', intended outcome is ensuring daughters and other chosen beneficiaries are nominated. (4) Update the existing Will — status 'Suggested', implementation responsibility 'Client', intended outcome is ensuring the Will reflects current wishes post-divorce. (5) Set up and register a Power of Attorney — status 'Suggested', implementation responsibility 'Client', intended outcome is ensuring a nominated person can act on the client's behalf if incapacitated. Implementation details state 10–15 working days timescale, cancellation rights, and out-of-market risk disclosure.
    File:     The case file passages (meeting summaries and supporting documents) confirm the client is Melanie Anness, recently divorced, managing pension transfer matters including a Pension Sharing Order from Quilter. Meeting summaries confirm the adviser (Morgan Seddon/James Poka) discussed pension strategy, fund transfers, and agreed to document the advice report. The risk profile report confirms a risk level was agreed interactively. No passage in the file directly confirms or denies specific implementation responsibility statements for recommendations 2–5, or whether the regular contributions recommendation is clearly distinguished as a suggestion rather than a current recommendation. No passage contradicts the report's stated implementation responsibilities.
    Discrepancies:
      - Recommendation 2 (regular contributions) is described in the action field as 'IncreaseContribution' but the adviceStatus is 'Suggested' rather than 'Recommended', and the responsibleParty is 'NotStated' — the report uses the language 'I recommend that you consider commencing regular contributions', which conflates 'recommend' and 'consider', making it unclear whether this is a recommendation or a suggestion. The guard notes that 'you may wish to consider' is not a recommendation; the wording here is similar.
      - Recommendation 3 (nomination of beneficiaries form) is classified as 'Recommended' but the responsibleParty is 'NotStated' — while the next-steps text says 'I recommend that you complete the nomination of beneficiaries form that will be provided to you after the transfer process is begun', it does not explicitly state who will implement or arrange this action (the client completes the form, but this is not stated in the implementation field).
      - The ambiguity noted in the extraction regarding regular contributions (whether they are part of the current recommendation or deferred to a future review) means the intended outcome and timing of recommendation 2 are not clearly stated in a way that distinguishes it from a future review item.
      - The expenditure discrepancy (£2,986 in current situation vs £2,237 in cashflow inputs) noted in the internal inconsistencies is relevant to whether the suitability rationale is based on consistent client-specific financial data, though it does not directly affect implementation statements.
    The primary recommendation (REC1 — Aviva pension consolidation) is clearly stated as recommended, the adviser will implement it, and the intended outcome is fully articulated and client-specific (retirement at 60, income of £25,000–£30,000 p.a., consolidation rationale). This recommendation meets the requirement. For REC3 (nomination of beneficiaries), the report states 'I recommend' and links it to OBJ6, but the responsibleParty is NotStated. The text implies the client completes the form (it will be 'provided to you'), so the responsible party is implicitly the client, but this is not explicitly stated in the implementation field. This is a minor gap. The more material concern is REC2 (regular contributions). The report uses the wording 'I recommend that you consider commencing regular contributions', which the guard identifies as ambiguous — 'recommend that you consider' is not a firm recommendation. The adviceStatus is 'Suggested' and the responsibleParty is 'NotStated'. The report does not state when this will happen, who will implement it, or what the specific contribution amount will be. The ambiguity noted in the extraction acknowledges it is unclear whether this is a current recommendation or a deferred future review item. The solution table shows 'Regular Contributions: Portfolio Nil', reinforcing that no contributions are being set up now. This creates a situation where a change (commencing contributions) is described without clearly stating whether it is recommended, when it will be acted upon, or by whom. REC4 and REC5 (Will update and Power of Attorney) are clearly labelled as suggested, the client is the responsible party, and the intended outcomes are stated. These meet the requirement adequately given they are non-product suggestions. The cashflow expenditure discrepancy (£2,986 vs £2,237) is an internal inconsistency in the factual foundation but does not directly undermine the implementation statements for this check. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the file passages that would materially alter this assessment. Overall, the primary concern is the ambiguous status of REC2 — it reads as a suggestion that is not clearly distinguished as deferred, with no implementation details, which is a process risk for a client in transition post-divorce who needs clarity on next steps.

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following about implementation: (1) The new pension transfer/consolidation will take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes. (2) The nomination of beneficiaries form will be provided 'after the transfer process is begun' and the client is recommended to complete it — responsibility assigned to Client, timing 'after transfer process is begun'. (3) The client is recommended to consider commencing regular contributions to the new pension, to be discussed in greater detail — responsibility NotStated, timing not specified. (4) Out-of-market risk is disclosed. (5) Cancellation rights of 30 days are noted. The primary new plan recommendation (NewPlan/transfer) has the adviser identified as responsible party. For the non-product actions (NominationForm, RegularContributions), the responsible parties are Client and NotStated respectively, and precise timing is absent for the contributions recommendation.
    File:     The meeting summary [P1] records that Morgan Seddon (the adviser) will 'Document the advice report and process disinvestment and payment within four to six weeks', which differs from the 10-15 working day timescale stated in the suitability report. No other passage in the file corroborates the 10-15 working days timescale or the specific action/timing/responsibility breakdown in the report. The Aviva T&C [P2, P6] and KFD [P7, P8] are generic provider documents and say nothing about when or by whom this client's transfer will be executed. The risk profile report [P10] and cashflow documents [P11, P12] do not address implementation timescales or responsibilities.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report states the transfer will take 10-15 working days; the meeting summary [P1] states Morgan Seddon will 'process disinvestment and payment within four to six weeks' — these are materially different timescales (10-15 working days ≈ 2-3 weeks vs 4-6 weeks).
      - The responsible party for the regular contributions recommendation is recorded as NotStated in the canonical model — no party is named in the report to action this recommendation.
      - The timing for the regular contributions recommendation is not specified ('which we can discuss in greater detail' indicates deferral but no timeframe is given).
      - The nomination of beneficiaries form step assigns responsibility to the Client but only says 'after the transfer process is begun' — no specific date or deadline is given for when it must be completed, though this is a minor process point.
    The main finding is the conflict between the suitability report's stated timescale of 10-15 working days and the meeting summary's record of 'four to six weeks'. These are not on different bases — both purport to describe how long the implementation will take. 10-15 working days is approximately 2-3 calendar weeks; four to six weeks is materially longer. This is a data inconsistency between two documents, and the meeting summary predates the report, raising a concern that the report has updated the timescale without explanation or that the meeting note reflects a different scope of work. The guard 'a timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale' applies to the 10-15 working days figure in the report, which is framed as a provider/administration standard. The meeting note figure of 4-6 weeks appears to be the adviser's own stated commitment. The discrepancy is therefore between the adviser's meeting-note commitment and the report's provider-standard figure — they may refer to different things (total end-to-end vs. provider processing), but the report does not explain this distinction, so it reads as contradictory. The regular contributions recommendation (REC2) is materially incomplete as an action point: it names no responsible party and no timescale, and is framed as a future consideration ('which we can discuss in greater detail'). Where a recommendation presents a possible outcome without specifying what action will be taken, when, and by whom, this falls short of the requirement. However, because regular contributions have not yet been quantified and are explicitly deferred to a future discussion, the suitability gap is moderate rather than high — the report signals the deferral rather than simply omitting it. No vulnerability factors identified in the file (the canonical model records a vulnerability assessment detail as NotPresentInDocument) materially affect this specific implementation-clarity requirement beyond the general need for clear communication. The nomination of beneficiaries point is adequately handled — client responsibility is clear and timing ('after transfer process is begun') is sufficient for a non-time-critical administrative step.

  [G6.9] Ancillary recommendations — beneficiary nomination, Power of Attorney, will review — are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report recommends: (1) the client complete a nomination of beneficiaries form to be provided after the transfer process begins (linked to OBJ6, responsible party: Adviser for the new plan recommendation overall); (2) the client consider commencing regular contributions to the new pension (linked to OBJ7, responsible party: NotStated). The canonical model records that the client has a will (hasWill: true), that it is NOT up to date (isWillUpToDate: false), and that no Power of Attorney has been granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false). The report does not recommend a will review or Power of Attorney despite recording that the will is not up to date and no PoA exists.
    File:     The file contains a Certified Final Order [P4] which notes that divorce affects inheritance under a will (section 18A of the Wills Act 1837) and that provisions appointing the former spouse as executor/trustee or conferring powers of appointment shall take effect as if the former spouse had died on dissolution. No passage in the file records the client's will status, confirms it is or is not up to date, or records that a Power of Attorney has or has not been granted. The meeting summary [P5] notes the client is post-divorce and managing financial affairs but does not mention estate planning, will review or PoA. No file document corroborates or contradicts the report's assertion that the will exists but is not up to date, or that no PoA is in place.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records the will is not up to date (isWillUpToDate: false) but makes no recommendation to review the will, despite the Certified Final Order [P4] explicitly noting that divorce affects inheritance under an existing will.
      - The report records no Power of Attorney is in place (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false) but makes no recommendation to consider establishing one.
      - The responsible party for the regular contributions recommendation is 'NotStated', leaving no clear owner for that ancillary recommendation.
      - No file document corroborates the report's assertion that a will exists but is out of date, or that no PoA has been granted — these assertions are unsupported by the evidence.
    The requirement is that ancillary recommendations are stated with an owner and are consistent with what the file records. Three distinct issues arise. First, the report itself records that the client's will is not up to date (isWillUpToDate: false). The Certified Final Order at [P4] independently flags that divorce revokes provisions in favour of a former spouse under s.18A Wills Act 1837 — making a will review particularly pertinent here. Yet the report contains no recommendation to review the will and names no owner for such action. This is a missing component: a known gap identified in the report's own data is not addressed. Second, the report records no Power of Attorney exists (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false) but again makes no recommendation and names no owner. Third, the nomination of beneficiaries recommendation is present and client-specific, with the adviser as the responsible party (form to be provided after transfer begins). However, the regular contributions recommendation has no responsible party stated (NotStated), which is a minor process gap. The guards confirm that recommending a PoA is different from the client already having one, and that an ancillary recommendation the adviser will not implement is not a defect provided the report says who will — but here no recommendation is made at all, so the guard does not explain away the gap. The vulnerability overlay: the client is post-divorce, managing complex financial arrangements alone, and the Certified Final Order specifically flags will implications of dissolution. These factors increase the materiality of the missing will review and PoA recommendation. The will and PoA assertions in the report are unsupported by any file document, which is a secondary gap but the primary concern is the absence of any recommendation despite the report's own recorded status.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). 1. The report states the initial advice fee is 3% giving a monetary amount of £9,838.36, but 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (True Potential + Nest) = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The basis for £9,838.36 (implying a fund total of ~£327,945 including the Quilter PSO of £260,000) is never explicitly stated in the report, and no passage in the file reconciles this figure. 2. The report states the ongoing adviser charge is circa £3,181.08 for the first year, described as based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'. 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45 (does not match). 1% of ~£327,945 minus £9,838 = ~£3,181 matches only if the Quilter PSO funds are included and the initial fee is deducted, but the report does not state this basis explicitly. 3. The SAP illustration [P6] shows Aviva AMC as 0.25% pa with a 0.08% decrease giving a net effective platform charge of 0.17%, whereas the report states 0.19% as the negotiated platform rate. These differ by 0.02pp; both are stated as Aviva's platform charge. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following recommended annual recurring charges on the Aviva Platform Personal Pension: Platform 0.19% (£623.10), Investment Management 0.20% (£655.89), Total 0.39% (£1,278.99). Transaction costs are excluded with a stated rationale (variable, accounted for in net performance figures). An initial advice fee of 3% is stated with a monetary figure of £9,838.36. An ongoing adviser charge of 1% pa is stated (circa £3,181.08 for the first year). A like-for-like pension switch charges comparison is provided for True Potential (existing 0.98%, new 0.39%, saving 0.59%) and Nest (existing 0.30%, new 0.39%, additional cost 0.09%). The report states the personal illustration contains further information on the cumulative effect of charges. The report asserts disclosureCompleteness flags productOrPlatformDisclosed, investmentOrFundDisclosed, transactionCostsDisclosed, initialAdviceFeeDisclosed, ongoingAdviceFeeDisclosed all as true, with dfmOrDiscretionaryFeeDisclosed false, and aggregatedTotalDisclosed and cumulativeEffectDisclosed both true. The Quilter PSO is excluded from the switch charges comparison table without explicit stated reason.
    File:     The Aviva illustration [P18, P19] confirms the Aviva platform charge at 0.19% and fund OCF/TER charges of 0.19% (HSBC) and 0.20% (Vanguard), weighted average 0.20%, consistent with the report's investment management charge of 0.20%. The illustration [P18] shows all-charges reduction in yield of 2.2% (growth after inflation from 2.8% to 0.6%), and product/investment charges reducing growth from 2.8% to 2.4% (0.4% reduction). The SAP comparison tools [P5, P6] show Aviva RIY of 1.5% across growth scenarios and projected values at age 87. The True Potential existing OCF is shown in the weighted average charges document [P16] as £272.72 on £46,672.80. True Potential fund factsheet [P2, P7] shows OCF of 0.63% for the UBS Aggressive fund. The SAP illustration [P6] shows Aviva AMC 0.25% pa with a 0.08% decrease applied (net 0.17%), fund costs of HSBC 0.21% and Vanguard 0.2207%, weighted average 0.2154%. The Aviva KFD [P13, P17] confirms charges are shown in the illustration and references adviser charges, DIM charges, and that fund charges are variable. No passage evidences the initial advice fee monetary amount of £9,838.36 as 3% of any explicitly stated combined transfer total. No passage states the basis for the ongoing adviser charge monetary figure of £3,181.08.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report states the initial advice fee is 3% giving a monetary amount of £9,838.36, but 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (True Potential + Nest) = £2,038.36, not £9,838.36. The basis for £9,838.36 (implying a fund total of ~£327,945 including the Quilter PSO of £260,000) is never explicitly stated in the report, and no passage in the file reconciles this figure.
      - 2. The report states the ongoing adviser charge is circa £3,181.08 for the first year, described as based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee'. 1% of £67,945.44 = £679.45 (does not match). 1% of ~£327,945 minus £9,838 = ~£3,181 matches only if the Quilter PSO funds are included and the initial fee is deducted, but the report does not state this basis explicitly.
      - 3. The SAP illustration [P6] shows Aviva AMC as 0.25% pa with a 0.08% decrease giving a net effective platform charge of 0.17%, whereas the report states 0.19% as the negotiated platform rate. These differ by 0.02pp; both are stated as Aviva's platform charge.
      - 4. The SAP illustration [P6] shows fund weighted average ongoing cost of 0.2154%, whereas the Aviva illustration [P19] shows weighted average 0.20%, and the report states 0.20%. The SAP and illustration differ by 0.0154pp.
      - 5. The report's annual recurring charges table includes only the ongoing product/platform/fund layers (0.39% total) and does not show the ongoing adviser charge (1%) in the same table, meaning no single row shows the all-in ongoing cost to the client.
      - 6. The Quilter PSO (£260,000) is excluded from the pension switch charges comparison table without an explicit stated reason in the report (exclusion is inferred, not stated).
      - 7. The True Potential existing arrangement platform charge is stated as 0.40% in the existing arrangements table, but the switch charges table uses 0.98% as the current annual recurring charge (inclusive of ongoing adviser charge of 0.50%). The like-for-like comparison subtracts the ongoing advice charge to produce a net comparable figure, but the basis for the 0.98% figure including the ongoing advice charge is used inconsistently across sections.
      - 8. The report claims the ongoing service fee monetary equivalent is 'circa £3,181.08' but the stated basis ('current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee') is not reconcilable with the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 alone — it is only reconcilable if the PSO funds are included, yet this is never stated.
    The report discloses all required cost layers individually: platform (0.19%), investment management/fund (0.20%), initial adviser charge (3%), ongoing adviser charge (1%), and excludes transaction costs with a stated rationale that qualifies as a disclosure rather than a silent omission under the guard. The Aviva illustration [P18, P19] corroborates the platform charge at 0.19% and fund charges at a weighted average of 0.20%, and shows the cumulative effect of charges (all-charges RIY of 2.2% reduction in growth rate), satisfying the cumulativeEffectDisclosed assertion. The SAP tools [P5, P6] provide a comparative RIY of 1.5% for the Aviva solution.
    
    However, three material concerns remain after applying the guards:
    
    First, the initial advice fee monetary figure of £9,838.36 is arithmetically irreconcilable with the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (3% × £67,945.44 = £2,038.36). It is only reconcilable if the Quilter PSO of £260,000 is included in the calculation base (3% × ~£327,945 ≈ £9,838), producing a combined total of approximately £327,945 — which matches the derived figures. However, the report never states this combined total as the basis for the fee. This is an internal inconsistency flagged by the extraction and confirmed by the derived figures: the report states 3% and £9,838.36 without stating the fund total to which 3% is applied. No passage in the file resolves this. This is a material disclosure shortfall: a client reading the report cannot verify the initial fee arithmetic from the information given.
    
    Second, the ongoing adviser charge monetary figure of £3,181.08 is similarly only reconcilable against the full combined fund (£327,945 minus £9,838 initial fee ≈ £318,107 × 1% ≈ £3,181). The report's stated basis — 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee' — does not make explicit that the Quilter PSO £260,000 is included. Again, no passage confirms the basis.
    
    Third, the annual recurring charges table (0.39%) does not include the ongoing adviser charge of 1%, so there is no single disclosed all-in ongoing charge figure. The report discloses them separately but never aggregates them. While the illustration [P18] captures all charges cumulatively, the report's own charges table is incomplete as a standalone disclosure.
    
    The SAP platform charge discrepancy (0.17% vs 0.19%) is a minor difference likely attributable to rounding or the way the 0.08% discount from 0.25% is applied; the Aviva illustration [P19] directly confirms 0.19%, so this is low severity.
    
    The Quilter PSO exclusion from the switch charges table is a genuine omission of explanation: the PSO is the largest asset being consolidated (£260,000 of £327,945 total) yet it does not appear in the like-for-like switch charges comparison. This affects the completeness of the comparison presented to the client.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: No specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially heighten the impact of these charge disclosure issues beyond the standard assessment.
    
    On balance: the initial fee arithmetic is internally inconsistent and unreconciled in the report; the ongoing fee basis is unstated; no all-in ongoing percentage is disclosed in the charges table; and the largest asset is excluded from the switch charges comparison without stated reason. These collectively constitute material disclosure shortfalls requiring clarification.
