  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) a pension switch does not guarantee a greater fund at retirement and new provider funds may not outperform existing ones; (2) cancellation risk — the previous provider is not obliged to take the plan back; (3) cancellation loss risk — if investment falls before cancellation notice is received, the fall will be deducted; (4) minimum access age rising from 55 to 57 from 6 April 2028; (5) IHT legislative change — from 6 April 2027 most unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate; (6) capital at risk — unit prices can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital, past performance is no guarantee; (7) inflation stress test — at 6% continued inflation assets could be depleted at age 81; (8) projection uncertainty — small variances in assumptions can compound to a marked difference; (9) long-term care costs not considered in charts; (10) liquidity risk — 10–15 working days to access funds; (11) return risk — no guarantee the new investment will outperform the existing arrangement. No projected retirement value (NP1) is stated in the report (noted as absent by extraction). The report does not address counterparty risk, credit risk, currency/exchange-rate risk, derivatives risk, or emerging-markets risk identified in the product KIIDs and fund literature.
    File:     The Aviva Pension KFD (P3, P11, P12) discloses income drawdown risks (means-tested benefits impact, fund depletion from high income withdrawals, less money for dependants/annuity), property liquidity risk, overseas investment exchange-rate risk, price fluctuation risk between instruction and transaction, fixed-term product access risk, and that the current favourable tax treatment may change. The Vanguard LifeStrategy KIID (P1, P6) identifies counterparty risk, liquidity risk, credit risk, inflation risk, derivatives risk, currency exchange-rate risk, and emerging-markets risk. The HSBC fact sheet (P7, P10) identifies emerging-markets risk, derivatives/leverage risk, and liquidity risk. The True Potential fund literature (P15, P16, P17, P18, P20) discloses credit risk, exchange-rate risk, liquidity risk, and capital-at-risk warnings. The Nest fund sheet (P19) is present but does not contain a detailed risk section in the retrieved passage. The FE research report (P14) is a correlation/allocation table with no additional risk narrative. No projected retirement value for NP1 appears in any file passage.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not mention counterparty risk identified in the Vanguard KIID (P1).
      - The report does not mention credit risk identified in the Vanguard KIID (P1) and True Potential literature (P20).
      - The report does not mention currency/exchange-rate risk identified in the Vanguard KIID (P1, P6), HSBC fact sheet (P10), and True Potential literature (P20).
      - The report does not mention derivatives risk identified in the Vanguard KIID (P1, P6) and HSBC fact sheet (P10).
      - The report does not mention emerging-markets risk identified in the Vanguard KIID (P6) and HSBC fact sheet (P10).
      - The report does not mention the income drawdown risks (means-tested benefits, fund depletion from withdrawals, reduced legacy/annuity provision) disclosed in the Aviva KFD (P3).
      - The report does not mention the tax-treatment-may-change risk disclosed in the Aviva KFD (P11).
      - The report does not mention the risk that charges may be deducted from income or capital depending on how funds levy them, as disclosed in the Aviva KFD (P11).
      - No projected retirement value for the recommended plan (NP1) is stated in the report, which was flagged as absent by the extraction; no passage in the file provides one either.
      - The report states an inflation stress-test loss scenario (assets depleted at age 81 under 6% inflation) but provides no equivalent downside scenario based on poor investment returns, despite the recommended funds showing negative annual returns in some years (e.g. HSBC rolling year to 31/01/23: -1.22% per P7).
    The report covers the core capital-at-risk warning, past performance caveat, switch-performance risk, liquidity (access timing), cancellation risks, IHT legislative change, minimum access age change, a specific inflation stress scenario, and projection uncertainty. These are genuine and substantive disclosures. However, the product literature on file identifies several material risks that are entirely absent from the report. The Vanguard LifeStrategy KIID (P1, P6) and HSBC fact sheet (P10) both highlight counterparty risk, credit risk, currency/exchange-rate risk, derivatives risk, and emerging-markets risk. The True Potential fund literature (P20) separately flags credit risk, exchange-rate risk and liquidity risk. The recommended portfolio invests in multi-asset funds with significant non-GBP exposure and uses derivatives (noted in the Vanguard KIID), making currency and derivatives risks product-specific and material rather than generic. Their absence from the report is a disclosure shortfall against the product literature on file. The guard against delegating risk disclosure to product literature applies: simply providing KIIDs does not satisfy the obligation on the suitability report to explain these risks. On the drawdown side, the Aviva KFD (P3) discloses specific income drawdown risks — impact on means-tested benefits, fund depletion from high income, and reduced legacy/annuity provision — none of which appear in the report. The case involves a pension consolidation with a drawdown-capable platform; these are directly relevant. The tax-treatment risk in the Aviva KFD (P11) is also absent, though the IHT legislative change is noted (a separate but related point). The extraction records that no projected retirement value (NP1) was present in the report; no passage in the file provides one. The absence of a projected value means there is no positive scenario against which the inflation depletion scenario can be contextualised, weakening the loss illustration. No vulnerability detail is on file (noted as absent by extraction at /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail), so no vulnerability overlay adjustment is possible here. The identified gaps — currency/exchange-rate risk, derivatives risk, counterparty risk, credit risk, emerging-markets risk, and income drawdown risks — are product-specific omissions from the report that are evidenced in the product literature on file. The discrepancies about general charges-from-income and tax-treatment-may-change are less material in context but add to the overall pattern. Severity is Moderate: the core capital-at-risk and switch risks are stated, but several fund-specific and drawdown-specific risks present in the file are absent from the report.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report states the following tax consequences: (1) pension contributions attract tax relief (£80 net becomes £100 gross); (2) flexi-access drawdown allows up to 25% tax-free lump sum (subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275 for those without LTA protection), with the remainder subject to income tax at marginal rate; (3) death before age 75 — benefits paid tax-free to nominated beneficiaries; death at or after age 75 — benefits taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate; (4) pensions are currently outside the estate for IHT but from 6 April 2027 most unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (5) Annual Allowance is £60,000 per annum (2025/26), excess contributions attract a charge of up to 45%; the client has not triggered the MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance; (6) tax-law caveat that levels and basis of relief are subject to change. The report also states the client's current assets are below her £500,000 allowance with no current IHT liability, but the forthcoming inheritance will take her over the limit. No projected retirement value is stated. No client-specific income tax band is established. The initial advice fee is stated as £9,838.36 (3% of amount invested), which is irreconcilable with 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (= £2,038.36) unless the PSO funds (~£260,000) are included, but this basis is never stated.
    File:     The Aviva KFD [P5] confirms: up to 25% can normally be taken as a tax-free lump sum; income payments and lump sum payments are subject to income tax depending on personal circumstances; HMRC lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance apply; tax rules may change. The Aviva T&C [P2] confirms tax relief on contributions up to HMRC limits. The Aviva pension illustration [P7] states annuity income figures are shown before deduction of tax. The switch analysis documents [P3, P4] note that a tax charge may be levied on any death benefit amount above the Lifetime Limit Pension Commencement Lump Sum allowance, and that the Aviva plan will provide 97% of the existing death benefit immediately post-switch. The SAP [P14] notes that income from drawdown is not guaranteed and will depend on future investment returns and levels of withdrawals. The report itself [P19] explains the IHT position in detail including the 2027 legislative change, NRB, RNRB and that on receipt of the inheritance the client will exceed her allowance. No passage in the file establishes the client's marginal income tax rate. No projected retirement value is present in any passage (confirmed absent by extraction metadata). No passage corroborates the £9,838.36 initial fee basis.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report does not explain the income tax consequence of drawdown withdrawals by reference to the client's own tax rate or band — the client's marginal rate is never established in the report or the file, so the tax consequence on withdrawals is stated generically rather than client-specifically.
      - No projected retirement value is stated in the report (confirmed absent by extraction metadata path /solution/NP1/projectedRetirementValue), meaning the income tax consequence of future crystallisation events cannot be contextualised for the client.
      - The switch analysis [P3, P4] notes the Aviva plan will provide 97% of the existing death benefit immediately post-switch (£65,907 vs £67,945), representing a reduction. The report does not disclose this death-benefit shortfall or its tax implications to the client.
      - The report states the initial advice fee as £9,838.36 at 3%, which is irreconcilable with 3% of the stated transfer value of £67,945.44 (= £2,038.36). The basis for the charge is never stated in the report, so the tax-deductible or non-deductible nature of the fee and its impact on the invested amount are not transparently disclosed.
      - The SAP documents [P3, P14] reference a Lifetime Limit / A-Day allowance figure of £1.0731m (2021/22 basis, noting CPI indexation from April 2026), whereas the report quotes the Lump Sum Allowance as £268,275. These are different allowances (lifetime allowance legacy vs. post-2024 lump sum allowance regime) but the report does not reconcile or clarify the distinction for the client, which could cause confusion about what limits apply.
      - The report states the IHT consequence in generic legislative terms for the 2027 change without quantifying the likely IHT liability after receipt of the inheritance, even though the report itself acknowledges the inheritance will take the client over the threshold.
    The report provides a broad and reasonably comprehensive tax disclosure section covering the main heads: contribution relief, TFLS, drawdown income tax, death benefits, annual allowance, MPAA, IHT and the 2027 legislative change, and a tax-law caveat. These are corroborated by the Aviva KFD [P5], T&C [P2] and illustration [P7]. Against the 'generic legislative explanation is not the same as stating the consequence for this client' guard: the drawdown income tax point is stated purely generically — the report never establishes the client's marginal income tax rate. The IHT section personalises the position (assets currently below £500,000 threshold, inheritance will take her over) but does not quantify the projected liability, which would have been possible given the facts in the report. These are at most moderate concerns: the generic income tax point is standard industry disclosure and the rate guard means it is unverifiable rather than wrong, but the absence of any personalisation of the drawdown tax charge for a client specifically planning to access drawdown falls short of the 'client-specific' standard. More materially, the switch analysis documents [P3, P4] show the proposed Aviva plan will provide only 97% of the existing death benefit (£65,907 vs £67,945) immediately post-switch. The report does not mention this reduction anywhere, nor its tax implications. This is a material disadvantage identified in the supporting evidence and absent from the report. The A-Day/lifetime allowance figure of £1.0731m in the SAP documents refers to the pre-2024 lifetime allowance regime; the report correctly cites the current £268,275 Lump Sum Allowance. These are different allowances and the discrepancy reflects legacy document wording in the SAP rather than an error in the report — this difference is explained by the fact the two passages address different regulatory concepts. The irreconcilable initial fee figure (£9,838.36 vs 3% of stated transfer value) is primarily a CHK-007 matter, but to the extent it affects tax transparency (the fee is deducted before investment, reducing the tax-relieved amount), the report's failure to state the basis for the charge is a disclosure gap here too. On vulnerability: no specific vulnerability is flagged in the file that would require enhanced tax explanation, and CHK-010 covers that assessment. Overall: the death-benefit reduction disclosed in the switch analysis but absent from the report is the principal concern under this requirement.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The report contains two loss scenarios under 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests': (1) a high inflation stress test at 6% showing assets depleted at age 81; (2) a market crash stress test equivalent to the 2007/8 crash showing assets would still last the client's lifetime.
    File:     The supporting evidence contains cashflow modelling outputs referenced in the canonical model and the Aviva illustration (P5) showing projected fund values at low, middle and high growth rates. The Aviva KFD (P1, P2, P3) contains income drawdown risk warnings including that high income withdrawal could deplete the pension fund, and that investment returns are not guaranteed. The switch analysis documents (P19, P20, P21, P24) contain projection tables and notes that figures are not guaranteed. None of the non-report passages in the evidence pack reproduce or corroborate the specific stress-test scenarios (6% inflation depletion at age 81; 2007/8 crash equivalent) stated in the report. The stress test content appears only in the suitability report itself (passages marked [I]).
    Discrepancies:
      - The two specific loss scenarios (6% inflation stress test showing depletion at age 81; 2007/8 crash stress test) are stated only in [I] passages — no independent supporting document in the pack corroborates or reproduces these cashflow stress-test outputs.
      - No projected retirement value for NP1 is present in the canonical model (flagged as absent in extraction metadata), meaning the baseline projection against which stress tests would be compared is not independently evidenced.
    The check method is PresenceOnly: the question is whether at least one loss scenario is stated in the report and whether the file is consistent with that. The report does state two loss scenarios — a 6% inflation stress test (assets depleted at age 81) and a 2007/8 market crash equivalent (assets survive lifetime) — both located in the 'Alternative Scenarios & Stress Tests' section of the suitability report, which a client reading the report would encounter. The guard confirms that a stress test in a cashflow model counts as a loss scenario even if not labelled as such in the risk section, so the placement is adequate. However, both scenarios are drawn exclusively from [I] passages (the suitability report itself). No cashflow model output document, no third-party stress-test result, and no other independent file passage reproduces or corroborates these specific scenarios. The report cannot corroborate itself. The stress-test content therefore exists as a report assertion without evidential support from the file. The absence of the projected retirement baseline value (flagged in extraction metadata) further means neither the starting point nor the stressed endpoint can be verified against file evidence. That said, the check's decision logic for 'No Issue' requires evidence complete and consistent — and the evidence side is silent, not contradictory. The scenarios are present and visible in the report, meeting the presence threshold, but they are unsupported by any independent document in the pack. Under the check's own logic ('Both absent: Potential Concern'), and given that the file provides no independent corroboration of the stress scenarios, the appropriate outcome is Potential Concern at Low severity: the scenarios are stated and visible but rest solely on the report's own assertions with no supporting file evidence. The vulnerability overlay does not materially alter this finding; no vulnerability detail is on file for this check's purposes.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report contains a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' in the main body (not relegated to an appendix). It states: the basis of advice section flags potential disadvantages and risks and cost/taxation implications. The capacity for loss section explains that investment values can fall, that the client's moderate capacity for loss is based on pension funds plus inheritance being sufficient even after a market fall, and that she could work beyond 60 if necessary. The fund description section states unit prices and fund values can fall as well as rise, there is no guarantee of return of original capital especially in early years or if income is taken, past performance is no guarantee of future returns, and a switch does not guarantee a greater fund at retirement. The 'Other Considerations of Switching' section discloses: market timing risk on disinvestment/reinvestment (could be disadvantaged or advantaged); a 10–15 working day access delay; the explicit statement 'There is no guarantee the return on the new investment will be greater than that of your existing arrangement'; and a delegation of all other risks to product literature ('All other risks associated with this investment are detailed in the product literature provided to you. You should read this document carefully.'). The report includes a pension switch charges assessment and critical yield / reduction in yield figures. No projected retirement value for the new plan (NP1) is present. Death benefit figures appear in the supporting illustrations (SAP documents) but the suitability report itself does not appear to discuss the death benefit reduction.
    File:     The SAP illustration (all-charges, ex-existing OAC) at [P9] shows that on the day of switch the Aviva plan provides 97% of the existing arrangements' death benefit (£65,907 vs £67,945 combined), and notes: 'If the death benefit available after switch is lower than that provided by the existing arrangements, consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall.' [P9] also notes a potential tax charge on lump sum death benefits above the Lifetime Limit PCLS allowance. The SAP illustrations at [P10] and [P16] show critical yield tables and reduction-in-yield figures (0.3%–0.4% for Nest; 0.4%–1.6% for True Potential depending on charges scenario), with the effect-of-switching figure varying from −2.68% to +20.85% depending on whether the existing OAC is excluded. [P11] and [P16] show the True Potential projected fund tables including critical yield. [P13] shows a lump-sum-at-retirement comparison: existing schemes £33,600 vs Aviva £26,600 at age 87, a material reduction. [P3] (report) confirms the basis-of-advice statement that disadvantages and risks will be highlighted. [P6] (report) contains the 'Other Considerations of Switching' content. [P5] (report) contains the capacity for loss and attitude to risk narrative. [P7] (report) contains the standard risk warnings about fund values falling.
    Discrepancies:
      - The SAP illustration [P9] discloses a death benefit reduction from £67,945 to £65,907 (97% of existing) on day of switch and flags that consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall — this material disadvantage does not appear to be disclosed in the suitability report's own body text.
      - The SAP illustration [P13] shows the estimated PCLS at retirement (age 87) is £26,600 from Aviva vs £33,600 from existing schemes — a reduction of approximately £7,000 (21%) — which is a material projected disadvantage not disclosed in the suitability report body.
      - The report delegates 'all other risks' to product literature rather than addressing them in the report itself, which may leave material product-specific risks (including those evidenced in the illustrations) undisclosed within the report.
      - No projected retirement value for NP1 is present in the report (confirmed absent by extraction metadata), so no loss scenario in monetary terms is illustrated within the report body.
      - The report does not discuss the tax consequences of death benefits above the Lifetime Limit / PCLS allowance, which the illustration [P9] flags as potentially applicable.
      - The report does not address any specific tax consequence of the pension switch or the pension sharing order receipt beyond the generic basis-of-advice statement that taxation implications will be covered.
    The report has a dedicated 'Other Considerations of Switching' section in the main body and is not relegated to an appendix, satisfying the prominence structure requirement. Standard investment risk warnings (values can fall, no guarantee of capital return, no guarantee of outperformance) are present. The capacity for loss narrative provides a qualitative loss scenario. However, two material disadvantages evidenced in the supporting illustrations are absent from the report body: (1) the death benefit reduction on day of switch (£65,907 vs £67,945, i.e. 3% lower), flagged in [P9] with an explicit note that consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall — this is a concrete, quantified disadvantage the client is giving up and it is absent from the report; (2) the projected PCLS at retirement being lower under Aviva (£26,600 vs £33,600 from existing schemes per [P13]), which is a material reduction in projected retirement benefit. The guard about bases being different applies to critical yield comparisons (different charge assumptions across the SAP variants), but it does not explain away the absence of the death benefit and PCLS reduction disclosures from the report body — those are specific, quantified outputs the illustration itself flags as requiring consideration, and they appear only in the supporting documents, not in the report. The 'all other risks in product literature' delegation in [P6] is a further concern: product-specific risks identified in the illustrations are not brought into the report. The extraction metadata confirms no projected retirement value for NP1 appears in the report, meaning the client receives no monetary illustration of what the recommended plan might deliver. Taken together, the absence of death benefit and PCLS reduction disclosures from the report body, and the wholesale delegation of remaining risks to product literature, constitute disclosure shortfalls of moderate-to-high severity. The vulnerability overlay: the client is recently divorced, newly self-employed, and managing a pension sharing order — a complex set of circumstances. There is no indication in the pack of a specific vulnerability driver (e.g. cognitive, emotional) that would elevate this further beyond what is already a concern, so the overlay does not change the severity finding here but reinforces the importance of clear disclosure given the complexity of her situation.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   All three existing arrangements (Quilter PSO, True Potential, Nest) are stated to contain no safeguarded benefits. The report records the following features for each existing plan: (1) Quilter — wide fund range, flexi-access drawdown and full/partial UFPLS available, adviser servicing allowed; (2) True Potential — flexi-access drawdown and full/partial UFPLS available, 35 funds available, adviser servicing allowed; (3) Nest — full and partial UFPLS only (flexi-access drawdown explicitly stated as NOT available), 6 funds available, adviser servicing NOT permitted. The report notes that Nest does not allow a servicing adviser, and that its retirement options are more limited than the proposed Aviva plan. No explicit 'features or guarantees lost' section is present in the extracted model (path /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent, confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by extraction).
    File:     The switch analysis documents (P1, P2, P4) confirm transfer values and projected fund comparisons for True Potential (£46,672) and Nest (£21,272) but do not enumerate features or guarantees being lost. The Nest fund factsheet (P5, P13, P18) describes the Nest Higher Risk fund, the Guided Retirement Fund, and responsible investment approach but does not identify specific contractual protections or guarantees attaching to the client's Nest policy. The True Potential fund literature (P11, P14, P15) describes fund objectives and suitability but does not list policy-level features. The Aviva T&C (P20, P22) describe Aviva product features including Smooth Managed Funds, Investment Pathways and commercial property — none of these are identified as features present in the existing plans. The Aviva pension illustration (P21) notes there is no spouse/civil partner income included and annuity assumptions but does not compare features to existing plans. No policy schedule or plan-level document for Quilter, True Potential or Nest is present in the pack that would confirm or deny specific contractual protections beyond what the report itself states. The Nest fund literature specifically notes flexi-access drawdown is not part of standard Nest offerings (consistent with the report's statement that flexi-access drawdown is not available under Nest).
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records no 'features or guarantees lost' analysis (path absent, confirmed NotPresentInDocument). There is no explicit section in the report articulating what the client is losing by leaving each existing plan, only a description of existing features — the report does not draw the comparison to show what is absent in the new plan or gained/lost in the transfer.
      - Nest currently does not allow a servicing adviser. The report records this fact but does not expressly frame it as a feature the client is giving up by leaving Nest (i.e. after transfer the client will gain adviser servicing on Aviva — this is a benefit gained, not lost, but the framing is absent).
      - The True Potential plan offers 35 funds and the Nest plan offers 6 funds. The report records these figures but does not compare them to the Aviva fund range (over 6,900 funds per the report elsewhere) in the context of what is being given up or gained. No feature of True Potential or Nest is identified as superior to Aviva and being sacrificed.
      - The Quilter PSO features are described (flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, wide fund range, adviser servicing) but no comparison is drawn to what Aviva offers in their place or what, if anything, is being foregone.
      - Death benefit: the switch analysis (P2) shows the Aviva plan will provide 97% of the existing plan's death benefit on day of transfer (£65,907 vs £67,945). This represents a material reduction in death benefit value on transfer. The report does not appear to disclose this reduction to the client as a feature or protection being given up.
      - No projected retirement value for NP1 is present (confirmed absent by extraction), so the report does not illustrate the value of what is being given up in terms of projected outcomes at retirement relative to the existing plans.
    The report records the features of each existing arrangement and states that none contains safeguarded benefits. That is the baseline disclosure. However, the critical issue under G8.6 is not merely listing existing features but identifying what the client is giving up by transferring. The report does not contain a dedicated 'features or guarantees lost' section — this is confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction metadata, so it is a report silence rather than an extraction failure.
    
    The most material finding is the death benefit reduction. The switch analysis document [P2] explicitly states that 'After transfer - the proposed Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) will provide 97% of the existing plan's death benefit', reflecting a day-one reduction from £67,945 to £65,907 — a shortfall of £2,038. This is a quantified reduction in a protection the client currently holds. It is present in the supporting evidence but absent from the report's disclosure to the client as a feature being given up. Under the check's decision logic, a material disadvantage present in research or product literature and omitted entirely from the report is the highest-severity finding.
    
    On the Nest plan, the report correctly notes that flexi-access drawdown is unavailable and that adviser servicing is not permitted. These are limitations of the existing plan rather than features being lost on transfer — the client is moving to a plan with more flexibility. This does not constitute a 'feature being given up' and is not a concern under G8.6.
    
    On True Potential, the report notes 35 funds and flexi-access drawdown availability. The Aviva plan offers over 6,900 funds. No feature of True Potential appears superior to Aviva and being sacrificed, so the fund-range comparison does not generate a 'giving up' concern.
    
    The absence of a projected retirement value for the Aviva plan (NP1) means the report cannot illustrate any loss of projected outcome. However, the switch analysis documents [P1, P4] do contain projected fund value comparisons at different growth rates, so this information exists in the file — the concern is whether the report conveys it to the client.
    
    No policy schedules are in the pack for any of the three existing plans, so it is not possible to verify whether there are any other contractual protections (e.g. guaranteed annuity rates, waiver of premium, loyalty bonuses) beyond what the report states. The guard instructs that a guarantee in provider literature not mentioned in the report is the finding — but no such guarantee is identified in the available literature passages. The absence of policy schedules is noted but does not alone generate a finding beyond what is already established.
    
    Vulnerability overlay: the extraction records that /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail is absent (NotPresentInDocument). No specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the available passages that would heighten the concern about the adequacy of risk and loss disclosure for this client.
    
    The primary finding is: the death benefit reduction identified in [P2] (client loses 3% of death benefit value on transfer, approximately £2,038) is present in the supporting evidence but not disclosed in the report as a protection being given up. This is a Disclosure shortfall of High severity under the guidance.

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report provides four cost-justification statements: (1) Like-for-like (excluding adviser charges), Aviva is 0.59% less expensive than True Potential, so the proposed plan can underperform by £275.37 p.a. (2) Like-for-like, Aviva is 0.09% more expensive than Nest, requiring outperformance of £19.15 p.a. (3) Including adviser charges (critical yield), Aviva can underperform True Potential by 0.01% p.a. to age 89 at 2.94% growth. (4) Including adviser charges, Aviva must outperform Nest by 1.23% p.a. to age 89 at 2.94% growth. The report also states the switching cost effect on the fund is -12.42%. Three projected values are given for the Aviva plan: £42,300 (0%), £106,000 (2.94%), £261,000 (5.88%) in real terms. The report discloses the higher Nest charge as a disadvantage (0.39% vs 0.30%, £19.15 p.a. increase). The critical yield appendix shows existing schemes combined at £48,408 / £121,807 / £298,632 and Aviva at £42,300 / £106,000 / £261,000 across the three growth rates. The report uses 2.94% as the mid growth rate throughout. No projected retirement value for the new plan (NP1) is separately stated in the solution section; projections appear only in the Critical Yield appendix.
    File:     The switch analysis projections (SAP) files corroborate three growth-rate projections for the Nest-only comparison: existing scheme £19,334 / £48,651 / £119,277 and Aviva £18,800 / £47,300 / £116,000 at 0% / 2.94% / 5.88%, with a critical yield of 0.09% (+0.09%) and fund effect of -2.68% (Nest alone, no adviser charge). A separate SAP (all charges including existing OAC) confirms death benefit: True Potential £46,672, Nest £21,272, total £67,945, Aviva £65,907 — so the proposed plan provides 97% of existing death benefit. The no-charges SAP confirms the Aviva plan projects higher than existing (£60,100 vs £53,497 at 0%, £151,000 vs £134,613 at 2.94%) without charges. The IAC-only SAP lump sum comparison shows existing schemes £33,600 vs Aviva £36,600. Notes in all SAP files warn that investment choice available with Aviva may differ from current providers and that actual transfer value may differ due to investment fluctuations. The report's critical yield appendix (passage [P18]) shows the combined existing schemes value at mid growth as £121,807 vs Aviva £106,000, with the fund effect of -12.42% — the switching cost is therefore driven primarily by initial adviser charges (3%) on the combined fund. The SAP (no charges, Nest only) shows fund effect of -2.68% for Nest alone, consistent with the 0.09% additional charge. The report's own narrative states the critical yield for Nest is 1.23% p.a. (including adviser fees), but the SAP no-charges file shows only 0.09% for Nest without adviser fees — the difference is attributable to the inclusion of the 3% initial charge and 1% ongoing charge. No passage contradicts the 0.59% True Potential like-for-like saving. The death benefit reduction (to 97%) is disclosed in the SAP files but not mentioned in the suitability report's disadvantages section.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report's critical yield appendix shows a fund effect of -12.42% on switching (combined plans including adviser charges). The Nest-only SAP (no charges) shows a fund effect of -2.68% for Nest alone. These figures are on different bases (combined with adviser charges vs Nest-only without adviser charges) and are not directly contradictory, but the report does not explain that the -12.42% is dominated by the 3% initial adviser charge deducted from the combined ~£328k fund rather than underlying plan charges — the basis is not stated in the report body.
      - The SAP files (passages P10, P11) disclose that the proposed Aviva plan will provide only 97% of the existing death benefit (£65,907 vs £67,945 total). This reduction in death benefit is not mentioned anywhere in the report's disadvantages section or elsewhere in the suitability report narrative — it is a benefit being given up that is absent from the report.
      - The report states projected values of £42,300 / £106,000 / £261,000 for Aviva (real terms, all plans combined including PSO). The Nest-only SAP (no charges) projects Aviva at £18,800 / £47,300 / £116,000 for Nest alone. These are on different bases (all plans vs Nest only) and are not contradictory, but the report does not clarify that the projections in the Critical Yield appendix are for the combined fund (True Potential + Nest + PSO), not just the switched plans.
      - The report's Like-for-Like section states the Aviva plan is 0.59% less expensive than True Potential, implying a saving of £275.37 p.a. The switch charges table shows True Potential current annual recurring charge as 0.98% and Aviva at 0.39%, a difference of 0.59% — consistent. However the 0.98% includes the 0.50% ongoing adviser charge. The like-for-like comparison excludes the adviser charge from both sides, leaving True Potential at 0.48% and Aviva at 0.39% — a saving of 0.09%, not 0.59%. The report states 0.59% saving without clearly stating that it is net of the existing adviser charge on one side only, creating a potential disclosure ambiguity about the basis of the comparison (though this is flagged as an internal inconsistency in the extraction).
      - The report states the critical yield for True Potential (including adviser charges) means Aviva can underperform by only 0.01% p.a., yet the like-for-like section says Aviva is 0.59% cheaper than True Potential. These two statements are not arithmetically reconcilable without explanation of the adviser charge impact — the report does not bridge the two figures for the client.
      - The extraction notes that /solution/NP1/projectedRetirementValue is absent from the document — projections appear only in the Critical Yield appendix, not in the solution section of the report where a client would first read it.
    The report provides cost justification at two levels (like-for-like charges and including adviser fees / critical yield) for both the True Potential and Nest transfers, and discloses the -12.42% fund effect of switching. Projections are given at three growth rates in the appendix. The comparison between the report and the file evidence is largely consistent: the 0.09% Nest additional charge and £19.15 figure match the SAP file; the death benefit figures in the SAP (97% of existing) are consistent with the SAP files. However, two concerns arise. First, the death benefit reduction is disclosed in the SAP files (P10, P11) — the proposed Aviva plan provides only 97% of the existing death benefit (£65,907 vs £67,945), and the SAP files themselves note 'consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall.' This is a material reduction in a benefit being given up that is entirely absent from the suitability report's disadvantages section and the main narrative — it is not mentioned at all in the report. This is a disclosure shortfall. Second, the basis of the -12.42% switching cost is not explained to the client: the report presents it as the 'effect of switching' without making clear it is primarily driven by the 3% initial adviser charge deducted from the ~£328k combined fund at outset, not by ongoing charge differences. Without that explanation, the client cannot assess whether the switching cost is justified. The critical yield figures for True Potential (can underperform by 0.01%) vs the like-for-like saving (0.59% cheaper) are not reconciled in the report — the difference arises from inclusion of adviser charges, but this is not bridged for the client. The 0.59% True Potential saving figure also requires explanation of its basis (adviser charge excluded from both sides) which is not prominently stated. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability detail was available in the extraction (the detail field is noted as absent), so no additional concern arises here beyond the general disclosure quality. On balance, the absence of the death benefit reduction from the report's disadvantages section — a material benefit being given up that is identified in the supporting evidence — is a Potential Concern. The basis of the switching cost and the reconciliation between like-for-like and critical yield figures represent weak justification. The death benefit issue is the primary finding.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) Higher charges vs Nest: +£19.15 p.a. / 0.09% more than Nest. (2) Critical yield for Nest: proposed plan must outperform Nest by 1.23% p.a. at 2.94% growth to age 89. (3) Effect of switching on fund value for Nest: -12.42%. (4) Critical yield for True Potential: proposed plan can underperform TP by 0.01% p.a. (5) Performance not guaranteed. (6) Transaction costs excluded from quoted charges. (7) Charge illustration may differ from illustration. (8) Switch between providers does not guarantee greater fund at retirement. (9) Cancellation risk — previous provider not obliged to take plan back. (10) Cancellation loss risk — fall in value before receipt deducted. (11) Minimum access age 55 rising to 57 from April 2028. (12) IHT legislative change from 6 April 2027 on unused DC pensions. No projected retirement value is stated in the report. No loss scenario expressed as a monetary amount is stated. No features or guarantees lost on transfer are documented.
    File:     [P3] (SAP All charges ex existing OAC) gives the critical yield table for the Nest comparison: at 2.94% growth the rate of return required from Aviva is 4.17% (+1.23%), and the effect on the fund if moved to Aviva is -31.35% (not -12.42% as stated in the report). The same passage shows the Nest existing scheme projected value at 2.94% as £48,651 and the Aviva projected value as £33,400. [P6] (SAP IAC only ex existing OAC) gives an alternative Nest critical yield scenario: rate of return required +0.19%, effect on fund -5.6%. [P8] (SAP no charges) shows death benefit on day of switch: TP £46,672, Nest £21,272, Aviva £67,945 — equal, no shortfall. [P9] notes that the investment choice with Aviva may differ from current providers and could impact future performance. [P14] (Aviva KFD) warns: value of pension can go down as well as up; you may get back less than paid in; transferring from older scheme could mean losing valuable rights or benefits; if transferring from a defined benefit scheme you may lose guaranteed income. [P1] (True Potential factsheet) states: 'With investing, your capital is at risk. Investments can fluctuate in value and you may get back less than you invest.' The extraction metadata confirms /solution/NP1/projectedRetirementValue is absent from the report. The metadata also confirms /replacementAnalysis/0,1,2/featuresOrGuaranteesLost are absent. Searches for loss scenario content (Q8.8.4) returned nothing.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report states the effect of switching to Aviva on the Nest fund is -12.42%. The SAP All Charges ex existing OAC document (P3) states the effect is -31.35% at all growth rates. These are materially different figures for the same metric.
      - The report states the critical yield for Nest (all charges including adviser fee) requires Aviva to outperform by 1.23% p.a. at 2.94% growth. P3 confirms +1.23% at 2.94%, so the critical yield percentage is consistent, but the fund reduction figure (-12.42% vs -31.35%) that accompanies it in the report is inconsistent with P3.
      - The report states no projected retirement value for the recommended plan (NP1); this is confirmed absent by the extraction metadata. No monetary loss scenario is illustrated.
      - The report does not disclose any features or guarantees lost on transfer for any of the three existing arrangements (EA1, EA2, EA3); the extraction metadata confirms these fields are absent from the report.
      - The SAP no-charges scenario (P6) shows a materially different effect on fund (-5.6%) and required outperformance (+0.19%), reflecting a different charges basis. The report does not state which scenario underpins its -12.42% figure, and none of the scenarios in the file produces -12.42%.
      - P3 provides a monetary loss scenario: Nest existing projected value £48,651 vs Aviva £33,400 at 2.94% growth — a £15,251 shortfall. This monetary illustration is not reproduced in the report.
      - The report does not mention the risk that Aviva's investment choice may differ from current providers and could impact future performance, despite this being stated in P9.
    The most significant finding is the fund-effect figure. The report quotes -12.42% as the effect of switching the Nest pension to Aviva. Every scenario in the supporting switch analysis produced by the research tool gives -31.35% (P3, all charges including adviser fee scenario) or -5.6% (P6, IAC only scenario). Neither scenario produces -12.42%. The -12.42% figure appears in the report without any corresponding scenario in the file. This is a data inconsistency: a material disadvantage figure disclosed to the client does not match the research on file. The -12.42% figure could conceivably arise from a different permutation of charges (e.g. excluding both initial and ongoing adviser charges), but no such scenario is in the pack, so it cannot be verified. This is a direct contradiction between the report and the supporting analysis and cannot be explained by any of the listed guards (it is not a generic risk warning, not a different date or basis that has been stated, not a ranking issue). The critical yield percentage of 1.23% at 2.94% is corroborated by P3, so that element is consistent. However the companion fund-effect figure that client sees alongside it is wrong. Second, the report contains no projected retirement value and no monetary loss scenario expressed in money terms. P3 does contain a monetary comparison: existing Nest £48,651 vs Aviva £33,400 at 2.94%, a £15,251 shortfall. This is precisely the kind of loss illustration the suitability report should carry or at least reference; its absence means the client sees a percentage but not a pound figure. The 'How to decide' guidance identifies absence of a loss scenario as a Potential Concern. Third, no features or guarantees lost are documented anywhere in the report for any of the three existing arrangements. The Aviva KFD (P14) explicitly warns that transferring from older schemes can mean losing valuable rights or benefits (e.g. defined benefit guarantees). While the existing plans appear to be DC and the risk may be low, the report does not address the point at all; it is required to do so. Fourth, the risk that Aviva's investment choice may differ from current providers (stated in P9) is absent from the report. This is a product-specific risk relevant to this client. On vulnerability: no vulnerability detail is present in the extraction (/clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail absent), so there is no overlay to apply. Severity: the -12.42% vs -31.35% discrepancy is quantitatively material — the client is shown a figure that is less than half the loss actually projected by the research. This is the highest-severity element. The absence of a monetary loss scenario and features-lost disclosure are further Moderate concerns.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). The Quilter PSO (£260,000 — the largest arrangement by value, representing ~79% of total funds) has no plan-specific switch charges analysis on file. The switch analysis documents cover only True Potential and Nest. This is the largest ceding plan with no cost/benefit analysis. The 'All charges inc existing OAC' scenario (P17/P18) shows the existing schemes projected at £121,807 vs Aviva at £106,000 at 2.94% growth — i.e. the existing arrangements produce a better projected outcome on this basis. The report does not disclose or address the scenario in which the switch produces an inferior projected fund value compared to the existing arrangements. The SAP analysis fund ongoing cost for the recommended funds shows a weighted average of 0.2154% (P5), whereas the report states investment management charge of 0.20%. These differ by 0.0154 percentage points (minor but present). 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report compares three existing arrangements (Quilter PSO £260,000; True Potential £46,672.80; Nest £21,272.64) against the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) across multiple dimensions: (1) Platform charges: Quilter 0.27% vs Aviva 0.19%, saving £208 p.a. (conditional on retaining with Quilter). (2) True Potential total annual charge 0.98% vs Aviva 0.39% — like-for-like saving of 0.59%. (3) Nest charges 0.30% (plan paid up, contribution charge 1.8% excluded) vs Aviva 0.39% — higher by £19.15 p.a. (4) Fund range: Nest 6 funds vs Aviva 6,900+. (5) Risk alignment: Nest Higher Risk Fund risk level 8 vs agreed client risk level 7; Aviva funds aligned to 7. (6) Retirement options: Nest offers UFPLS only, no flexi-access drawdown; Aviva offers flexi-access drawdown and UFPLS. (7) Adviser servicing: Nest does not allow a servicing adviser; Quilter allows it conditionally; Aviva allows active monitoring. (8) Safeguarded benefits: all existing plans stated to contain none. (9) Investment pathways considered and discounted. Switch charges analysis produced for True Potential and Nest only (Quilter PSO excluded from switch charges table).
    File:     Four switch analysis documents are on file (Selectapension): (a) 'All charges inc existing OAC' — Aviva RIY 1.5%; existing schemes projected £121,807 at 2.94% growth; Aviva projected £106,000 — existing schemes outperform Aviva on this basis. (b) 'All charges ex existing OAC' — Aviva RIY 0.4%; existing projected £134,612 at 2.94%; Aviva projected £151,000 — Aviva outperforms. (c) 'IAC only ex existing OAC' — Aviva RIY 0.5%; existing £134,612; Aviva £146,000 — Aviva outperforms. (d) 'No charges ex existing OAC' — Aviva RIY 0.4%; existing £134,612; Aviva £151,000 — Aviva outperforms. True Potential transfer value confirmed at £46,672.80; Nest transfer value also confirmed in these documents. Aviva KFD confirms flexi-access drawdown available. True Potential Defaqto data confirms existing holdings performance. The switch analysis covers True Potential and Nest only; no equivalent switch analysis document is on file for the Quilter PSO (£260,000 — the largest single arrangement). The charge arithmetic derived figures confirm: Aviva platform charge 0.19% and investment management 0.20% applied to the total of all three arrangements (£327,947). The SAP documents show the recommended funds as HSBC Global Strategy Dynamic Portfolio Accumulation C (50%) and Vanguard LifeStrategy 80% Equity Fund A Acc (50%) with weighted average ongoing cost of 0.2154%.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Quilter PSO (£260,000 — the largest arrangement by value, representing ~79% of total funds) has no plan-specific switch charges analysis on file. The switch analysis documents cover only True Potential and Nest. This is the largest ceding plan with no cost/benefit analysis.
      - The 'All charges inc existing OAC' scenario (P17/P18) shows the existing schemes projected at £121,807 vs Aviva at £106,000 at 2.94% growth — i.e. the existing arrangements produce a better projected outcome on this basis. The report does not disclose or address the scenario in which the switch produces an inferior projected fund value compared to the existing arrangements.
      - The SAP analysis fund ongoing cost for the recommended funds shows a weighted average of 0.2154% (P5), whereas the report states investment management charge of 0.20%. These differ by 0.0154 percentage points (minor but present).
      - The switch analysis documents use a retirement age of 87 as the 'proposed age', but the report's canonical model does not explicitly state 87 as the client's target retirement age in the comparison section, creating a potential basis difference.
      - The Quilter PSO is included in the recommendation table and in the Aviva charge calculation (charge arithmetic confirms 0.19% applied to £327,947 — the total of all three arrangements) but is absent from the Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment table in the report and absent from all switch analysis documents.
      - The report asserts the Quilter platform charge is 0.27% conditional on retaining with Quilter, but no independent provider document corroborates this Quilter charge rate in the file.
      - The report states the True Potential like-for-like existing total is 0.98% and the saving is 0.59% (implying new = 0.39%). The existing arrangements table states True Potential platform charge as 0.40%, not 0.98%. The 0.98% includes ongoing advice charge of 0.50% and is used inconsistently (noted as an internal inconsistency in the extraction metadata).
    The check covers whether each dimension the report compares is supported by provider or research documentation. Several material concerns arise.
    
    1. QUILTER PSO — NO SWITCH ANALYSIS: The largest arrangement (£260,000, ~79% of total funds) has no switch charges analysis on file and no plan-specific cost/benefit comparison. The SAP documents cover only True Potential and Nest. The severity guidance flags this as the highest severity situation: the largest ceding plan by value has no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis. No false-positive guard explains the absence of analysis for this plan; the PSO nature means it is being received rather than transferred out, but the charge calculation applied to it (Aviva 0.19% applied to the full £327,947 per the derived figures) means it is within scope of the ongoing cost comparison. Whether the switch of the Quilter plan is a voluntary transfer or a PSO receipt is itself ambiguous (noted in extraction ambiguities), but regardless the report justifies the platform charge saving of £208 p.a. against a Quilter 0.27% charge, and no Quilter provider document is on file to corroborate that charge rate.
    
    2. ADVERSE PROJECTION SCENARIO NOT DISCLOSED: The 'All charges inc existing OAC' SAP document (P17/P18) shows that when all charges including the existing ongoing advice charge are included, Aviva projects £106,000 vs existing £121,807 at 2.94% growth — the existing arrangements outperform Aviva. The report does not address or disclose this scenario. COBS 9.2.2R requires the report to present information that is fair, clear and not misleading. A scenario in which the existing arrangement produces a better projected outcome is material and its omission is a finding. The guard about different bases applies here: the 'all charges inc OAC' scenario incorporates the existing ongoing advice charge that the client would pay regardless, so the like-for-like comparison properly excludes it. However, the existence of the scenario where Aviva is worse should at minimum be acknowledged and explained, and it is not.
    
    3. CHARGES INCONSISTENCY — TRUE POTENTIAL: The 0.98% figure used in the like-for-like comparison includes the ongoing adviser charge of 0.50%, which inflates the apparent saving. The existing arrangements table states True Potential platform charge as 0.40%. The extraction metadata flags this as an internal inconsistency. The SAP 'ex existing OAC' documents appropriately exclude the existing OAC, but the report's narrative does not clearly explain what is included and excluded in the 0.98% vs 0.39% comparison.
    
    4. SUPPORTED DIMENSIONS: Flexi-access drawdown availability on Aviva is corroborated by the Aviva KFD (P10). The SAP 'ex existing OAC' and 'IAC only ex existing OAC' documents (P4/P15) show Aviva outperforming the existing arrangements on a like-for-like basis. Transfer values for True Potential and Nest are confirmed in the SAP documents. Investment pathways were considered and discounted (P1/P11). The fund range, risk alignment and adviser servicing claims are from the report itself ([I] passages) and have no independent corroboration in the file beyond the Aviva KFD confirming drawdown options.
    
    5. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: No vulnerability-specific factors appear in the file that would materially alter this assessment.
    
    Summary: The primary concerns are (a) no switch analysis for the largest ceding plan (£260,000 Quilter PSO), (b) the adverse projection scenario (existing outperforms Aviva when all charges included) not being addressed in the report, and (c) the Quilter 0.27% charge claim having no independent documentary support. These are material gaps in the comparison.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report makes the following assertions for each ceding plan: (1) Quilter (EA1, PSO): lower platform charges on Aviva (0.19%) vs Quilter (0.27%), saving £208 pa; no exit penalty; no client preference to retain; adviser monitoring and wider fund range (6,900+ vs 6) benefits; more flexible retirement options including flexi-access drawdown; online platform access; client wishes managed pension. (2) True Potential (EA2): no exit penalty; new plan is cheaper by 0.59% / £275.37 pa on a like-for-like basis (excluding existing OAC of 0.50%); existing total 0.98% (platform 0.40% + fund 0.58% + OAC 0.50%), new plan 0.39%. (3) Nest (EA3): no exit penalty; new plan is 0.09% / £19.15 pa more expensive on like-for-like basis; outperformance required of 1.23% pa to age 89 at 2.94% growth; Nest plan is paid up; contribution charge 1.8% (not currently applied). Features/guarantees lost: not stated in report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument). Transfer value for Quilter: not stated (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument). Alternatives considered: not evidenced in the passages.
    File:     The suitability analysis packs (SAP documents, category G) confirm: (1) Switch charges analysis for True Potential and Nest is present in the file with critical yield, projected fund values and death benefit comparisons. For True Potential (no-charges scenario): Aviva projects £151,000 vs existing £134,612 at 2.94% growth (age 87); critical yield -0.38% below assumed rate, RIY 0.4%. For Nest (no-charges scenario): Aviva projects £47,300 vs existing £48,651 at 2.94%; critical yield +0.09% above assumed rate; Nest fund is 0.09% cheaper. (2) Death benefit comparison for True Potential + Nest combined shows existing £67,945 vs Aviva £65,907 at day of switch (97% of existing). (3) Aviva platform charge 0.19% and Quilter platform charge 0.27% are not matched in any passage; £208 pa saving figure is not matched. (4) The fund range claim (6,900+) is not corroborated by any passage. (5) Flexible retirement / flexi-access drawdown claim for Aviva is not corroborated by any evidence passage. (6) No SAP or comparison document for the Quilter PSO plan (EA1, ~£260,000) is present in the retrieved passages. (7) The internal inconsistency identified in extraction: True Potential platform charge stated as 0.40% in existing arrangements table but 0.98% used as total (including OAC) in switch charges table; like-for-like comparison removes OAC to give 0.98% vs 0.39% saving of 0.59%. (8) The report states outperformance required to age 89 for Nest but SAP documents use age 87 throughout. (9) The Aviva KFD/T&C (category H) confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio exists and describes charge/disinvestment mechanics but does not corroborate specific charge rates or comparisons.
    Discrepancies:
      - Quilter platform charge 0.27% and Aviva negotiated rate 0.19% producing £208 pa saving: neither figure matched in any evidence passage — claim is unsupported by category E or G evidence.
      - Quilter PSO (EA1, ~£260,000): no SAP, comparison analysis or provider documentation in the file; this is the largest ceding plan by value and has no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis in the evidence.
      - Fund range claim (6,900+ vs 6): no corroborating passage.
      - Flexi-access drawdown availability on Aviva vs existing plans: no corroborating passage confirms this feature or that it is unavailable on the ceding plans.
      - Features or guarantees lost: report is silent (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument for all three plans); no provider documentation confirms none exist, particularly for the Quilter PSO.
      - Report states outperformance required to age 89 for Nest; SAP analysis documents use age 87 — discrepancy of 2 years in term used.
      - True Potential charge inconsistency within the report: existing arrangements table states platform charge 0.40%; switch charges table uses 0.98% total (including OAC 0.50%); like-for-like comparison subtracts OAC and quotes saving of 0.59% — figures used inconsistently across sections (internal inconsistency recorded in extraction).
      - Death benefit after switch (Aviva £65,907 vs existing £67,945) is only 97% of existing — report does not address the £2,038 death benefit shortfall or whether replacement of the shortfall was considered.
      - Quilter PSO transfer value not stated in report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument); transfer value basis for the £208 pa saving and initial fee calculation of £9,838.36 is unexplained.
      - Initial advice charge £9,838.36 stated as 3% of amount invested but 3% × stated transfer value of £67,945.44 = £2,038.36 only; reconcilable only if PSO funds (~£260,000) are included — basis never explicitly stated in report.
      - No alternatives to switching considered or evidenced in the passages.
      - Nest contribution charge 1.8% listed in existing arrangements but switch charges table uses only 0.30%; ambiguity over whether contribution charge is truly inapplicable not resolved in report.
    The report makes multiple switch-justification claims across three ceding plans, but the evidential position varies materially by plan and by claim type.
    
    **Quilter PSO (EA1, ~£260,000 — largest plan by value):** No SAP, comparison illustration or provider documentation for this plan appears in the evidence. The sole comparison made is on platform charges (0.27% vs 0.19% = £208 pa saving), but neither figure is matched in any passage and neither the Quilter charge nor the Aviva negotiated rate is confirmed by a category E or G document. The transfer value is not stated. Given this plan represents approximately 79% of total funds (£260,000 of £327,945), the absence of a plan-specific cost/benefit analysis is a high-severity gap.
    
    **True Potential (EA2):** The SAP documents (P8, P16) do support a comparison for True Potential: Aviva projects better outcomes at 2.94% and 5.88% growth, critical yield is below assumed rate (saving of 0.39pp at 2.94%), and the no-charges scenario shows Aviva outperforms. However, an internal inconsistency in the report's charge presentation is recorded: the existing arrangements table states platform charge 0.40%, but the switch table uses 0.98% total (0.40% + 0.50% OAC + 0.58% fund — noting 0.40+0.50+0.58=1.48%, not 0.98%; the 0.98% appears to exclude OAC, being 0.40%+0.58%). The like-for-like narrative then removes the OAC and derives a saving of 0.59% (0.98% − 0.39%). While the logic is followable, the presentation is inconsistent across sections, which is a disclosure quality concern.
    
    **Nest (EA3):** P14 confirms that the new plan is 0.09% more expensive on a like-for-like basis and the critical yield exceeds the assumed growth rate by 0.09pp. The report acknowledges this and states outperformance of £19.15 pa is required. This is consistent with the SAP evidence. However, the report states the outperformance target applies to age 89, while the SAP documents use age 87 throughout — a 2-year discrepancy in term that cannot be explained by a rounding or basis difference and remains unresolved.
    
    **Death benefit:** P13 shows the combined existing death benefit (True Potential + Nest only) is £67,945 and the Aviva plan provides £65,907 at day of switch — 97% of existing. The report does not address this shortfall or whether the difference should be replaced. The Quilter PSO death benefit is not included in this comparison at all, which is a further gap.
    
    **Features and guarantees:** The extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all three plans. No provider documentation confirming absence of safeguarded benefits or guarantees appears in the file for any plan, and in particular not for the Quilter PSO. This is a high-severity omission under the severity guidance.
    
    **Vulnerability overlay:** No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages as materially affecting the switching analysis. The vulnerability assessment detail is also NotPresentInDocument per the extraction, but that is assessed under CHK-010 and does not independently worsen this finding.
    
    **Alternatives:** No evidence of alternatives considered appears in any passage.
    
    In summary: the True Potential switch has SAP-backed analysis that broadly supports the recommendation, with a charge presentation inconsistency. The Nest switch has SAP support but a term discrepancy (age 87 vs 89). The Quilter PSO — the largest plan — has no plan-specific analysis, no features/guarantees assessment, and unconfirmed charge comparisons. The death benefit shortfall post-switch is unaddressed. Taken together, these constitute material gaps requiring the outcome to be Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   All three existing arrangements (Quilter PSO, True Potential Personal Pension, Nest Personal Pension) are stated to contain no safeguarded benefits and no safeguarded benefits are being given up. Employer contributions are stated as not being lost for any of the three plans. The extraction confirms that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the report for all three plans — the report does not address features or guarantees beyond the safeguarded benefits statement.
    File:     The SelectaPension switch analysis documents (categories G) contain fund value comparisons, critical yield tables, death benefit comparisons and charge illustrations for True Potential and Nest, but none of these documents contain a provider-level confirmation or scheme booklet statement that the ceding plans hold no safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed growth rates or other protected features. The Nest fund factsheet [P24] covers asset allocation but says nothing about plan-level guarantees or benefits. No provider correspondence, scheme booklet, or plan terms document is present in the retrieved passages that positively confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits or other protected features for any of the three ceding plans. The SAP illustrations note that 'These policies operate on the Money Purchase basis' [P17] and reference standard DC projections, but this is an illustration assumption, not a provider confirmation of the absence of guarantees.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no safeguarded benefits exist in any of the three ceding plans, but no provider documentation (scheme booklet, plan terms, or provider confirmation letter) is present in the case file to corroborate this assertion for any of the three plans.
      - The report is silent on featuresOrGuaranteesLost (confirmed absent by extraction) — there is no disclosure of whether any plan-level features such as guaranteed annuity rates, guaranteed growth rates, waiver of premium, loyalty bonuses or similar valuable features exist or are being given up.
      - The Quilter PSO plan (EA1, value £260,000 — the largest arrangement by value) is excluded from the SelectaPension switch charges assessment table; it has no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis at all in the file, and no provider documentation confirming the absence of guarantees for it is present.
      - The death benefit comparison in [P8] covers only True Potential and Nest (total £67,945) against Aviva (£65,907), showing a 3% shortfall. The Quilter PSO is excluded. No explanation is given for why the Quilter plan's death benefit is not included in this comparison.
    The central issue is that the report's assertion of 'no safeguarded benefits' for all three ceding plans is not corroborated by any provider documentation in the file. Under the check's decision logic and guard, an explicit 'no safeguarded benefits' statement in the report must still be corroborated by policy documentation — the report asserting it is not evidence of it. No scheme booklet, provider confirmation letter, or plan terms document appears in any retrieved passage for Quilter, True Potential or Nest. The SelectaPension illustrations [P1, P7, P12, P17, P18] are switching analysis tools, not provider confirmations of the absence of guarantees. The note in [P17] that the policies operate on a 'Money Purchase' basis is an illustration assumption, not independent provider confirmation. The absence of featuresOrGuaranteesLost from the report (confirmed by extraction as NotPresentInDocument, not extraction failure) means the report also does not address whether any non-safeguarded but valuable features — such as guaranteed annuity rates at maturity, waiver of premium, loyalty bonuses or guaranteed growth rates — are present or being given up. This is a disclosure shortfall and a missing component. The Quilter PSO is the most significant gap: at £260,000 it is the largest single arrangement by value (79% of total), it is absent from the switch charges assessment table, absent from the death benefit comparison, and has no provider documentation whatsoever in the file confirming the nature of its benefits. The severity is highest by the check's own guidance: the largest ceding plan by value has no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis, and the switch proceeds with no provider documentation confirming the absence of guarantees for any plan. No vulnerability information in the file bears specifically on this requirement beyond what CHK-010 would assess.

        "These policies operate on the 'Money Purchase' basis (also known as 'Defined Contribution') & provide a fund of money, at retirement, which is then used to purchase a pension and normally a Pension Commencement Lump Sum (PCLS)."

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report considers five alternatives: Option 1 (leave existing scheme untouched), Option 2 (leave in place and switch funds in situ), Option 3 (switch into another existing arrangement), Option 4 (switch into employer's pension), Option 5 (set up a new plan — the recommended option). It also addresses a Stakeholder Pension and Investment Pathways. For Option 1 (Quilter), the stated rejection reason is that Quilter platform charges are higher at 0.27% vs Aviva's 0.19%, saving £208 per annum, and the client has no preference to remain with Quilter. For Option 2, higher Quilter platform charges are inferred as the reason even where funds are switched in situ. Options 3 and 4 are listed as considered but carry no explicit rejection rationale. The stakeholder pension is rejected on cost and flexibility grounds. Investment pathways are rejected as insufficient for the client's tailored needs. The analysis is framed around the Quilter PSO. True Potential and Nest are addressed in separate recommendation sections citing charges and performance but the alternatives table is not replicated for them individually.
    File:     [P2] (report, category I) reproduces the five-option menu and the recommendation table showing Quilter PSO → Aviva and Nest → Aviva. [P4]/[P6]/[P8] are Selectapension switch-charge analyses covering True Potential and Nest (not Quilter PSO), providing projected fund values at retirement under different charge scenarios (all charges including existing OAC; IAC only excluding existing OAC; no charges excluding existing OAC). These confirm a formal charges comparison exists for True Potential and Nest. [P5] shows at the 2.94% growth rate the existing schemes produce a higher PCLS (£30,400) than Aviva (£26,600) in the 'all charges including existing OAC' scenario. [P7] shows in the 'IAC only excluding existing OAC' scenario Aviva produces a higher PCLS (£36,600 vs £33,600). No Selectapension or equivalent analysis is on file for the Quilter PSO. [P21] (Aviva KFD) notes the general risk of losing valuable benefits on transfer and recommends considering all options. No case-file document (category E or G) provides a charges comparison for the Quilter PSO specifically, nor is there provider documentation confirming or denying safeguarded benefits for that plan. The file contains no evidence supporting or contradicting the £208 per annum saving figure quoted for Quilter. No document addresses Options 3 or 4 with any reasoning.
    Discrepancies:
      - Options 3 and 4 are listed as considered in the report but carry no rejection rationale in the report itself, and no supporting evidence in the file explains why they were discounted.
      - The alternatives analysis is framed primarily around the Quilter PSO; no separate alternatives table or equivalent analysis is presented for True Potential or Nest, even though they are materially different plans (different providers, charges, fund structures).
      - The report's rejection of Option 1 for Quilter cites a £208 per annum saving based on 0.27% vs 0.19% platform charges, but no independent document in the file corroborates the Quilter platform charge of 0.27% or the £208 figure — all supporting passages on this point are [I] category only.
      - No Selectapension or equivalent switch-charges analysis exists on file for the Quilter PSO, unlike True Potential and Nest which each have one.
      - Under the 'all charges including existing OAC' Selectapension scenario [P5], the existing schemes produce a higher projected PCLS (£30,400) than Aviva (£26,600) at the 2.94% growth rate, which is not addressed in the alternatives discussion.
      - No provider documentation confirming the absence of safeguarded or guaranteed benefits is on file for the Quilter PSO (the largest arrangement at £260,000).
    The report covers the required option menu (Options 1–5 plus stakeholder and investment pathways) and provides explicit rejection reasoning for Options 1 and 2 in relation to the Quilter PSO, and for the stakeholder and investment pathway alternatives. The Selectapension analyses on file ([P4], [P6], [P8]) provide a documented charges and projections comparison for True Potential and Nest, corroborating the decision to switch those plans. However, several weaknesses remain after applying the guards.
    
    First, the Quilter PSO is the largest arrangement (£260,000 — nearly 80% of total assets) yet is the only ceding plan for which no independent switch-charges analysis exists on file. The £208 per annum saving and the 0.27% Quilter charge are asserted only in the report ([P2], category I) and are not corroborated by any category E or G document. The report cannot corroborate itself.
    
    Second, under the SetCoverage guard, the alternatives menu is stated once for the case as a whole. This is permissible where the rejection reasoning is plan-specific and the plans are not materially different. Here the three plans are materially different: the PSO plan (£260,000, Quilter) is separated by an order of magnitude from True Potential (£46,673) and Nest (£21,273), arises from a pension-sharing order rather than personal contributions, and may carry different terms or guarantees. The guard therefore does not excuse the absence of plan-level reasoning for Options 3 and 4 in relation to True Potential and Nest, nor for the Quilter PSO.
    
    Third, the Selectapension 'all charges including existing OAC' scenario ([P5]) shows the existing schemes projecting a higher PCLS than Aviva at the central growth rate (£30,400 vs £26,600). The report does not address this scenario or explain why the 'IAC only' scenario ([P7], which favours Aviva at £36,600 vs £33,600) is the appropriate comparator. The basis for selecting which scenario is relevant to the client is not disclosed.
    
    Fourth, no provider documentation confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits for the Quilter PSO is on file. The Aviva KFD ([P21]) flags the general risk of losing guaranteed benefits on transfer but is generic. Given the PSO's size and origin, the absence of plan-specific confirmation is a high-severity gap.
    
    On vulnerability: the report notes the client is recently divorced and has moved to self-employment, both of which are potential vulnerability indicators. No specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the file that would materially alter this assessment beyond what the fact-find already captures ([P23]).
    
    The weakest points are: (a) no independent corroboration of the Quilter cost comparison, (b) no safeguarded-benefits confirmation for the largest plan, and (c) the unfavourable projection scenario in the file is not addressed. These are material gaps for the highest-value plan.
