  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   Three plans are being switched/consolidated. (1) Quilter PSO: exit penalty £0; platform charge saving of 0.08% (0.19% vs 0.27%), stated as £208 p.a. saving. (2) Nest: exit penalty £0; new plan costs 0.09% / £19.15 p.a. more on a like-for-like basis, requiring the new plan to outperform by 1.23% p.a. to age 89 at 2.94% growth. (3) True Potential: exit penalty £0; new plan 0.59% / £275.37 p.a. cheaper on a like-for-like basis (excluding existing 0.50% ongoing advice charge). Benefits claimed: lower platform costs on Quilter transfer (£208 p.a.), wider fund range (6,900+ vs 6), flexible drawdown, adviser servicing and monitoring, online platform access. Critical yield analysis provided at three growth assumptions: 0.41% at 0%, 3.37% at 2.94%, 6.32% at 5.88%.
    File:     Three SelectaPension switch analysis documents are on file (categories G): 'All charges ex existing OAC', 'IAC only ex existing OAC', and 'No charges ex existing OAC'. All three cover True Potential and Nest only; the Quilter PSO does not appear as an analysed ceding plan in any switch analysis document. The True Potential transfer value is confirmed at £46,672.80 and the Nest fund value at £21,272.64 (paid up, proposed age 87). The SAP 'all charges' document (P1) shows a Reduction in Yield for Aviva of 0.4% and a Rate of Return Required from Aviva at 1% growth of -0.6% (-0.6%), at 2.94% of 2.33% (-0.61%), and at 5.88% of 5.25% (-0.63%). The SAP 'no charges' document (P8) shows Aviva projected fund at 0%, 2.94% and 5.88% of £60,100, £151,000 and £370,000 vs existing £53,497.38, £134,612.81 and £330,027.33. The SAP documents do not show the Quilter PSO as a ceding plan; no separate critical yield or charges comparison for the Quilter PSO exists in the file. The report's critical yield figures (0.41% at 0%, 3.37% at 2.94%, 6.32% at 5.88%) do not match those in the SAP documents (−0.6% at 1%, 2.33% at 2.94%, 5.25% at 5.88%). The growth rate assumptions in the SAP (1%, 2.94%, 5.88%) differ from those used in the report's critical yield table (0%, 2.94%, 5.88%). The £208 p.a. Quilter platform saving, the £19.15 Nest charge difference and the £275.37 True Potential saving are not corroborated by any passage. The PCLS comparison in P3 shows existing schemes £33,600 vs Aviva £26,600 (all charges scenario), while P7 shows existing £33,600 vs Aviva £36,600 (IAC only scenario).
    Discrepancies:
      - Critical yield at 0% growth: report states 0.41% (+0.41%); SAP documents show rate of return required at 1% growth of -0.60% (-0.60%) — different growth assumption and opposite sign (report's figure is not corroborated).
      - Critical yield at 2.94% growth: report states 3.37% (+0.43%); SAP 'all charges' shows 2.33% (-0.61%) at the same 2.94% growth rate — numerically inconsistent.
      - Critical yield at 5.88% growth: report states 6.32% (+0.44%); SAP 'all charges' shows 5.25% (-0.63%) at the same 5.88% growth rate — numerically inconsistent.
      - The sign convention differs: the report's critical yield figures are all positive (Aviva must outperform), whereas the SAP figures are all negative (Aviva needs to earn less to match existing), indicating a fundamentally different result or a different basis of comparison.
      - The SAP documents model True Potential and Nest only; the Quilter PSO is absent from every switch analysis document on file — no charges comparison, critical yield or cost/benefit analysis is evidenced for the largest single ceding plan by value (£260,000).
      - The report's stated £208 p.a. Quilter platform charge saving (0.08% difference) is not corroborated by any passage in the file.
      - The report's stated Nest charge difference of +0.09% / +£19.15 p.a. is not corroborated by any passage.
      - The report's stated True Potential like-for-like saving of -0.59% / -£275.37 p.a. is not corroborated by any passage.
      - The report states outperformance required vs Nest of 1.23% to age 89 at 2.94% growth; the SAP documents reference age 87 and show a negative rate of return required — inconsistent age and direction.
      - The PCLS comparison differs between SAP scenarios: P3 ('all charges') shows Aviva £26,600 vs existing £33,600 (Aviva lower); P7 ('IAC only') shows Aviva £36,600 vs existing £33,600 (Aviva higher) — these are scenarios not directly contradicting the report but show materially different outcomes depending on charge basis, and the report does not address the all-charges PCLS shortfall.
      - The SAP 'no charges' document (P8) uses plan charges of 0.4% monthly and fund AMC of 0.58%, not the negotiated 0.19% platform charge plus 0.20% fund charge stated in the report for Aviva — indicating the switch analysis documents may not reflect the actual negotiated rates.
    The report discloses switching costs and benefits for all three ceding plans and provides a critical yield table. However, there are material discrepancies between the report's critical yield figures and those in the SelectaPension analysis documents on file.
    
    First, and most significantly, the Quilter PSO — the largest ceding plan at £260,000, representing approximately 79% of the total fund being transferred — is absent from all three SelectaPension switch analysis documents. No plan-specific charges comparison, critical yield or cost/benefit analysis has been evidenced for this plan. The report relies solely on an assertion of a £208 p.a. saving from a 0.08% platform charge reduction, but this figure is uncorroborated by any file document. Under the severity guidance, the largest ceding plan by value lacking plan-specific cost/benefit analysis is a High concern.
    
    Second, the critical yield figures in the report (0.41% at 0%, 3.37% at 2.94%, 6.32% at 5.88%) do not match those in the SAP 'all charges' documents (−0.60% at 1%, 2.33% at 2.94%, 5.25% at 5.88%). At the same 2.94% growth rate, the report states 3.37% required return while the SAP shows 2.33%. At 5.88%, the report states 6.32% while the SAP shows 5.25%. The sign reversal is also significant: the report shows positive figures (Aviva must outperform to match existing), while the SAP shows negative figures (Aviva needs lower returns to match existing), suggesting either a different basis of calculation or that the critical yield table in the report relates to a combined scenario (including PSO) not modelled in the SAP documents. The SAP 'no charges' document (P8) shows the plan charges used as 0.4% monthly and fund AMC of 0.58%, not the negotiated 0.17% Aviva platform plus 0.20% fund charge stated in the recommendation — this may explain part of the discrepancy but is not reconciled in the report.
    
    Third, the Nest charge difference (£19.15 p.a.) and the True Potential saving (£275.37 p.a.) stated in the switch charges table are not independently corroborated in any passage, though the SAP does cover these two plans.
    
    The guard on non-financial benefits applies: the report argues adviser servicing, wider fund range and drawdown flexibility explicitly, which can justify the small Nest cost increase (£19.15 p.a.). However, this guard does not remedy the absence of any switch analysis for the Quilter PSO or the contradiction between the report's critical yield figures and those in the SAP.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes absence of vulnerability detail, so no additional finding arises here.
    
    Overall: the Quilter PSO lacks any evidenced switch analysis (High); the critical yield figures in the report contradict those in the SAP documents (High); the monetary charge savings for all three plans are unconfirmed by independent evidence.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   All three existing arrangements (Quilter/PSO, True Potential Personal Pension, Nest Personal Pension) have safeguardedBenefits.isBeingGivenUp = false. All three have no exit penalties, described as 'No charges for transfer and/or switching'. The report does not discuss any featuresOrGuaranteesLost for any of the three arrangements (extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument).
    File:     The switch analysis documents (SAP illustrations) confirm that True Potential and Nest are both Money Purchase / Defined Contribution arrangements. The SAP documents note that on the day of switch the Aviva Pension Portfolio will provide 97% of the existing plans' death benefit (£65,907 vs £67,945 combined). The True Potential SAP shows transfer value equals fund value (£46,672.80), and Nest SAP shows transfer value equals fund value (£21,272), consistent with no exit penalties on those two plans. The Aviva KFD warns generally that transferring from an older company pension scheme could mean losing valuable rights such as guaranteed income based on final salary, and that the transfer may not result in higher benefits. The Quilter PSO arrangement (£260,000) does not appear in any switch charges assessment table and has no SAP illustration in the retrieved passages; no provider documentation confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits or exit penalties for the Quilter PSO arrangement is present in the evidence pack. The Fact Find notes a Quilter pension with transfer cont. of £351,493.34 and value of £294,867.94 as at 20/07/2026, but no guarantees analysis for it is present.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts no safeguarded benefits are being given up for the Quilter PSO arrangement, but no provider documentation or SAP illustration for the Quilter PSO is present in the evidence pack to corroborate this — the largest arrangement by value (£260,000) has no plan-specific safeguarded-benefits confirmation.
      - The report asserts no exit penalties apply to the Quilter PSO arrangement, but no provider confirmation of this is present in the evidence; the assertion is self-standing.
      - The SAP death benefit comparison shows the proposed Aviva plan will provide only 97% of the existing plans' combined death benefit on the day of switch (£65,907 vs £67,945), representing a day-one reduction of £2,038. The report does not address whether this shortfall has been considered or will be replaced, notwithstanding the SAP's own note that 'consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall'.
      - The extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all three arrangements — the report contains no analysis of features or guarantees lost, beyond the bare assertion that none are being given up.
      - The Quilter PSO is excluded from the switch charges assessment table — the SAP covers only True Potential and Nest — so no like-for-like charges or benefits comparison exists for the largest ceding arrangement.
    The check requires that for every ceding plan the report identifies and addresses safeguarded benefits, guarantees and exit penalties, corroborated by external evidence. For True Potential and Nest the SAP illustrations confirm DC/money-purchase status, matching transfer values to fund values (consistent with no exit penalties), and the report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits are being given up is at least consistent with the plan-type evidence, though no explicit guarantee-check document is cited. However, for the Quilter PSO — the largest arrangement at £260,000, comprising approximately 79% of the total fund — there is no SAP illustration, no provider letter and no guarantee-check document in the pack. The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits are being given up and no exit penalty applies to this arrangement is entirely unsupported by external evidence; it is self-corroborating only, and the report does not corroborate itself. This is the most material gap: the severity guidance flags 'safeguarded benefits given up with no analysis, or no provider documentation confirming none exist' as the highest severity, and 'the largest ceding plan by value has no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis' as High. Both apply here. Additionally, the SAP documents for True Potential and Nest identify a day-one death benefit shortfall of £2,038 (3% reduction) and explicitly state that consideration should be given to replacing the shortfall. The suitability report does not address this. The Aviva KFD's generic warning about potential loss of valuable benefits does not substitute for plan-specific analysis. The guard states the requirement 'only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up'; however, where no provider documentation confirms that no benefit is being given up for the Quilter PSO, the absence of that documentation is itself the concern — the guard cannot be used to excuse the missing evidence for the largest plan. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to raise additional considerations specific to this requirement beyond what CHK-010 will address separately.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report lists five options for each ceding plan (leave untouched, in-situ fund switch, switch into another existing arrangement, switch into employer scheme, new plan) and recommends Option 5. For the Quilter PSO: Option 1 rejected on cost (Quilter platform 0.27% vs Aviva 0.19%, saving £208 p.a.) and client preference (no ties to Quilter). Option 2 rejected because Quilter platform costs remain higher even if funds switched in situ (inferred). Options 3 and 4 listed as considered but no explicit rejection rationale stated. For Nest and True Potential: Option 2 rejected because the new scheme offers a wider fund range better suited to risk profile. Options 3 and 4 again listed without explicit rejection rationale. Stakeholder pension discounted as less cost-effective and lacking flexible access / fund range. Investment pathways discounted as too generic. The recommended Aviva platform is said to offer lower charges, wider fund range, active monitoring and better alignment with client's risk profile and retirement goals.
    File:     The switch charge analysis documents (Annes M SAP series) are on file and compare the existing True Potential and Nest plans against the Aviva Pension Portfolio (Choice) on an all-charges, initial-advice-charge-only, and no-charges basis [P4, P7, P10]. These confirm transfer values of £46,672.80 (True Potential) and £21,272.64 (Nest) and show projected values at retirement age 87 under three growth scenarios. The performance comparison in the FE scatter chart [P17] shows the proposed blended portfolio (A: 59.29% over 5 years) marginally ahead of NEST Higher Risk (B: 59.28%) and ahead of True Potential (C: 52.25%). The SAP documents confirm that Aviva charges are lower than the existing plans on a like-for-like basis. No SAP or comparable charge analysis for the Quilter PSO is present in the file. No explicit evidence corroborates rejection reasons for Options 3 (switch into another existing arrangement) or Option 4 (switch into employer scheme) — these are listed in the report without any reasoning. The Nest fund information [P15, P16] is on file. No documentary evidence corroborates the Quilter platform charge of 0.27% or the £208 annual saving figure other than the report itself.
    Discrepancies:
      - Options 3 and 4 (switch into another existing arrangement; switch into employer scheme) are listed as considered for all ceding plans but no rejection rationale is provided in the report and no supporting evidence for their rejection appears in the case file.
      - The Quilter PSO (the largest single arrangement at £260,000) is absent from the switch charge analysis documents — the SAP analyses cover only True Potential and Nest. No independent charge comparison for Quilter versus Aviva is in the file.
      - The report asserts Quilter platform charge is 0.27% and the saving is £208 p.a. These figures appear only in the report itself; no Quilter provider documentation or third-party analysis corroborates them.
      - Option 2 rejection for the Quilter PSO is marked as inferred by the extraction — no explicit rejection reasoning appears in the report for that plan specifically, only an overall cost comparison.
      - The report states the recommended plan is more cost-effective than a stakeholder pension but provides no stakeholder charge comparison or evidence to support this.
    The check focuses on whether alternatives to the recommended switch were properly considered and evidenced. For True Potential and Nest, the SAP documents [P4, P7, P10] provide a charges-based comparison corroborating that Aviva is lower cost on a like-for-like basis, and the performance analysis [P17] shows the proposed funds are broadly comparable or superior over five years. The rejection of Option 2 for these plans (wider fund range argument) is stated in the report and is client-specific. These two ceding plans are adequately covered from an alternatives perspective, with the caveat that Options 3 and 4 lack explicit rejection reasoning. However, the Quilter PSO is the largest arrangement by value (£260,000 — 79% of total funds) and is absent from every SAP document. The report asserts a Quilter platform charge of 0.27% versus Aviva's 0.19% and a saving of £208 p.a., but these figures appear only in the report itself — no Quilter documentation, no provider illustration and no third-party switch analysis is in the file to corroborate them. The £208 saving on £260,000 implies a 0.08% differential (0.27% minus 0.19%), which is arithmetically consistent with the figures stated, but 0.08% of £260,000 is £208, meaning the claim is internally coherent; the problem is the absence of any external evidence confirming the Quilter charge itself. For the largest ceding plan, this means the cost-benefit case for switching rests solely on the report's own assertion. Options 3 and 4 lack any rejection reasoning across all plans — this is a disclosure gap common to all three ceding plans but most material for the Quilter PSO given its size. The vulnerability overlay has no independent bearing here: no vulnerability characteristics identified in the file alter the adequacy of the alternatives analysis. The guard on consolidation as a valid client objective applies: the client stated no preference to remain with Quilter, which partially supports the switch, but this does not substitute for an evidenced cost comparison on the largest plan. Overall: the Quilter PSO analysis is unsupported by independent evidence, Options 3 and 4 have no rejection reasoning, and the stakeholder comparison is unsubstantiated. These are material gaps, the most serious being the absence of any plan-specific charge/benefit analysis for the largest ceding plan.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits
  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified
    Report:   The report asserts the following benefits justify the switch costs: (1) Quilter: lower platform charge 0.19% vs 0.27%, saving £208 pa; no exit penalty; no client preference to remain; adviser monitoring capability; 6,900+ funds vs 6; flexi-access drawdown; online platform. (2) Nest: new plan is 0.09% / £19.15 pa MORE expensive on like-for-like basis; must outperform Nest by 1.23% pa (including adviser charges at 2.94% growth) to age 89; justified by improved risk alignment, broader fund range, flexi-access drawdown, adviser servicing. (3) True Potential: new plan saves 0.59% / £275.37 pa like-for-like (excluding existing ongoing advice charge of 0.50%); including adviser charges the Aviva plan can underperform True Potential by only 0.01% pa to age 89, described as effectively cost-neutral. Benefits include improved risk alignment, broader fund range, lower charges, professional oversight.
    File:     The SAP documents (P1/P2 including existing OAC; P3/P5/P6 excluding existing OAC; P7/P8 no charges) confirm the switch charges analysis is present in the file. P3 (ex existing OAC, True Potential at 2.94%): existing paid-up at £85,961, indicating a benchmark for the True Potential switch. P1 (inc existing OAC): True Potential paid-up at £73,156 at 2.94%. P6 (IAC only, ex existing OAC) shows Aviva PCLS at £36,600 vs existing schemes £33,600. P2 (all charges inc OAC) shows Aviva PCLS at £26,600 vs existing £30,400 — i.e. Aviva delivers LESS at retirement when all charges including existing OAC are included. P7 (no charges): Aviva £151,000 vs existing £134,612 at 2.94%, suggesting Aviva outperforms on a no-charges basis, meaning the fund selection is more growth-oriented. P9 (Aviva KFD) confirms flexi-access drawdown / SIPP structure and warns about transfer risks and potential loss of valuable benefits. P4 confirms Aviva AMC 0.25% pa with negotiated reduction of 0.08%, giving 0.17%, plus fund charges (HSBC 0.21%, Vanguard 0.2207%, weighted avg 0.2154%). The Quilter charge comparison (0.19% vs 0.27%) and the fund-range claim (6,900+ vs 6) are asserted in the report only — no independent provider documentation confirms the Quilter platform charge of 0.27% or the Quilter fund count of 6. The SAP tools show the Aviva plan is cheaper on a like-for-like basis excluding existing adviser charges (True Potential), but more expensive when all charges including the new adviser charge are included (Nest). No passage in categories E or G independently evidences the Quilter charge of 0.27%, the fund count comparison, or that no safeguarded benefits exist in the Quilter PSO.
    Discrepancies:
      - 1. The report claims the Aviva platform charge is 0.19%; P4 states the standard AMC is 0.25% reduced by 0.08%, giving 0.17% — a 0.02% discrepancy vs the 0.19% stated in the report's own charges table (P13). These may be reconcilable: 0.25% − 0.08% = 0.17% for investment management plus a separate platform fee, but the arithmetic does not straightforwardly produce 0.19%.
      - 2. The Quilter platform charge of 0.27% appears only in the suitability report (P16, category I). No independent provider document or category E/G source confirms this figure.
      - 3. The fund range comparison of 6,900+ (Aviva) vs 6 (Quilter) appears only in the report (P15, category I). No external source confirms the Quilter fund count of 6.
      - 4. P2 (SAP all charges including existing OAC) shows Aviva PCLS of £26,600 vs existing schemes £30,400 at retirement, meaning on a total-charges-included basis the switch leaves the client with less at retirement. The report does not address this scenario from the SAP.
      - 5. No passage in categories E or G confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits or guarantees in the Quilter PSO, despite P9 (Aviva KFD) explicitly warning that transferring from an older company pension could involve losing guaranteed income or valuable rights. The report asserts no safeguarded benefits are lost but this claim is unsupported by any evidence document.
      - 6. The 'no exit penalty' assertion for all three ceding plans is stated only in the report (category I passages); no provider exit confirmation letter or category E document is on file to corroborate it for the Quilter PSO in particular.
      - 7. The report states the Nest switch requires 1.23% pa outperformance to match projected values. The SAP (P1, all charges inc OAC) confirms this scenario is modelled, but the Nest switch remains loss-making on a total-charge basis with no benefit other than qualitative ones (adviser servicing, fund range, flexi-access). The file contains no evidence that Nest does not already offer flexi-access drawdown or that its fund range is limited to the extent claimed.
      - 8. The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction — True Potential platform charge stated as 0.40% in existing arrangements table but switch charges table uses 0.98% including existing ongoing advice charge of 0.50% — means the like-for-like saving of 0.59% (which excludes the 0.50% existing OAC) is presented as a cost benefit of switching, when in practice the client will be paying 1% ongoing advice going forward vs 0.50% previously. The net position on adviser charges is a cost increase of 0.50% pa, which the report does not highlight as a cost of switching.
    The requirement is to assess whether evidence supports claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits. This requires looking at each limb.
    
    For the True Potential switch: The SAP documents (P3, P5) corroborate a genuine like-for-like charge saving of 0.59% pa excluding the existing ongoing advice charge. However, the existing OAC of 0.50% is being replaced by a new OAC of 1.00% — a net increase of 0.50% pa in adviser charges. The SAP (P1, including existing OAC) shows Aviva projected fund of £73,156 vs True Potential £73,156 at 2.94% (paid up) — the report's own 'can underperform by 0.01%' figure is very thin. The file contains evidence supporting the charges comparison for True Potential, though the net adviser charge increase is not disclosed as a cost.
    
    For the Nest switch: The report openly acknowledges the new plan is 0.09% / £19.15 pa more expensive on a like-for-like basis and requires 1.23% pa outperformance including adviser charges. The SAP documents corroborate the charge modelling. However, the benefits claimed to justify this cost increase — wider fund range, flexi-access drawdown, adviser servicing — are stated only in the report (category I) with no independent evidence. There is no evidence that Nest lacks flexi-access drawdown or has a fund range so limited that this constitutes a genuine additional benefit. P9 (Aviva KFD) confirms Aviva offers these features but does not evidence that Nest lacks them.
    
    For the Quilter PSO switch: The report claims a cost saving (0.19% vs 0.27%) but neither the Quilter charge of 0.27% nor the fund count of 6 is evidenced by any category E or G document. More seriously, the Quilter PSO arises from a pension sharing order against an ex-husband's pension. P9 (Aviva KFD) explicitly warns that defined benefit or older company pension schemes may carry guaranteed income or valuable rights that would be lost. No document in the file confirms that the Quilter PSO contains no safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates or other valuable features. The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits are lost is entirely unsupported by evidence. This is the highest severity concern per the severity guidance.
    
    On the fund range comparison guard: the report claims 6,900+ vs 6 funds. P15 confirms Aviva has 6,900+ but this is a report passage (category I). No independent source confirms the Quilter fund count. This does not count as corroboration.
    
    The internal inconsistency regarding the True Potential charge basis (0.40% platform in existing table vs 0.98% in switch table including 0.50% OAC) means the like-for-like saving of 0.59% is presented in a way that does not make clear to the reader that the ongoing adviser charge is doubling from 0.50% to 1.00%, which is a disclosure shortfall.
    
    Overall: the Quilter safeguarded benefits gap and absence of corroboration for the key cost comparison figures are material concerns. The Nest cost increase is acknowledged but justified only by benefits that are not evidenced from external sources. No vulnerability-related adjustment is required: the file notes the client is recently divorced and self-employed but no specific vulnerability drivers are recorded that would alter the weight of this analysis.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   Three plans are being switched to Aviva: (1) Quilter PSO — lower platform charge saving £208 pa (0.19% vs 0.27%), no safeguarded benefits, client has no preference to remain with Quilter, adviser active management; (2) True Potential — platform charge saving (0.40% to 0.21% = 0.19% saving, £88.68 pa), fund charge saving (0.58% to 0.20%), like-for-like saving £275.37 pa / 0.59%; including adviser charges the new plan can underperform by only 0.01% pa to age 89 to match the same value; improved risk alignment, broader fund range; (3) Nest — annual charges slightly higher on like-for-like basis (+0.09%, £19.15 pa), justified by improved risk alignment (risk 8 fund vs agreed risk 7), broader fund range, flexi-access drawdown, adviser management; including adviser charges, new plan must outperform by 1.23% pa at 2.94% growth to age 89. Five alternatives were considered and discounted. No safeguarded benefits identified as lost for any plan. featuresOrGuaranteesLost not stated in report for any plan.
    File:     SAP documents (P9, P11, P13, P15) provide switch analysis for True Potential and Nest only — the Quilter PSO does not appear in any SAP charges table. P9 (all charges ex existing OAC) confirms True Potential fund value £46,672.80, Nest fund value £21,272.64 and provides projections. P13 (no charges ex existing OAC) shows Aviva projected £151,000 vs existing £134,612.81 at 2.94%, RIY 0.4% for the combined True Potential + Nest transfer. Meeting notes (P2, P4, P8) confirm client risk profile level 7, investment horizon 5–10 years, no specific concerns about Nest or True Potential, no mention of safeguarded benefits. Aviva KFD (P20) warns generically about transfer risks including possible loss of guaranteed income from DB schemes. No provider documentation from Quilter, True Potential or Nest confirming absence of safeguarded benefits is present in the pack. No plan-specific cost/benefit SAP analysis for the Quilter PSO is present.
    Discrepancies:
      - The Quilter PSO is included in the report's switch recommendation and the platform charge saving of £208 pa is stated, but no SAP charges analysis for the Quilter PSO appears in any file passage — the SAP documents cover only True Potential and Nest.
      - The report states featuresOrGuaranteesLost is not addressed for any of the three plans. No provider documentation from Quilter, True Potential or Nest confirming absence of safeguarded benefits is present in the evidence pack.
      - The report states the Nest switch results in higher charges (+0.09%, £19.15 pa) yet asserts net benefit; the SAP (P9) covers this plan but the report's own extraction notes ambiguity about whether Nest's 1.8% contribution charge is excluded correctly from the 0.30% figure used — this is not resolved in any passage.
      - The report states five alternatives were considered case-wide, but no plan-specific alternatives analysis is evidenced for the Quilter PSO in the file.
      - The Aviva KFD (P20) warns that transferring from an older company pension scheme could mean losing guaranteed income (DB scheme risks); no passage confirms or denies whether the Quilter PSO originated from a DB scheme, and no provider documentation addresses this.
      - The report states the True Potential platform charge saving is 0.19% / £88.68; 0.19% × £46,672.80 = £88.68 — this arithmetic is consistent. However the internal inconsistency noted in the extraction (0.40% platform charge in existing arrangements table vs 0.98% total in switch charges table including 0.50% ongoing advice) is unresolved by any evidence passage.
      - The report does not state what features or guarantees (if any) exist on the Quilter PSO — given this is a Pension Sharing Order receipt held in cash, no passage confirms whether any safeguarded or guaranteed annuity rates attach to it.
    The report makes three distinct switch recommendations. For True Potential and Nest, the file contains SAP analysis documents (P9, P11, P13, P15) that provide charges projections and confirm fund values consistent with those used in the report. The like-for-like charge arithmetic is broadly supported: the derived figures confirm 0.98% existing vs 0.39% proposed for True Potential (saving 0.59%) and 0.30% existing vs 0.39% proposed for Nest (costing 0.09% more). The report's critical yield figures (0.01% underperformance tolerance for True Potential; 1.23% outperformance requirement for Nest) are stated in the report (P24 [I]) and the SAP documents exist on file, but since the SAP passages retrieved do not reproduce the critical yield figures verbatim in these passages, the corroboration is the existence of the SAP documents rather than a direct passage match.
    
    The most significant concern is the Quilter PSO: this is the largest single arrangement by value (£260,000 of the £327,945 total), yet no SAP charges analysis for it appears in the evidence pack. The report asserts a platform charge saving of £208 pa and no safeguarded benefits, but neither assertion is supported by any evidence category E or G passage. The SAP documents retrieved cover only True Potential and Nest. Per the severity guidance, the largest ceding plan by value having no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis is a High concern. Additionally, the report and extraction both note that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is not stated for any plan, and no provider documentation from any of the three providers confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits is present in the pack. The Aviva KFD (P20) explicitly warns about potential loss of guaranteed income from DB schemes on transfer, which heightens the concern about the absence of documentation for the Quilter PSO specifically.
    
    The alternatives analysis (five options considered) is documented in the report (P23 [I]) and covers the case generally, but P23 is an [I] passage and cannot corroborate itself. No external evidence addresses whether alternatives were genuinely considered for the Quilter PSO specifically.
    
    The vulnerability overlay: the client is navigating post-divorce financial reorganisation (P2, P7). Meeting notes (P2, P4) confirm risk level 7 and investment horizon, and no vulnerability factors are recorded that would materially alter this specific finding.
    
    On balance: the True Potential and Nest switch analysis is supported by SAP documentation in the file, even if not perfectly cited in individual passages. The Quilter PSO — the largest plan — has no supporting charges analysis, no safeguarded benefits confirmation, and no plan-specific alternatives discussion evidenced outside the report itself. This is a material gap.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report asserts that no employer contributions are lost on any of the three transfers (employerContributionsLost: false for all three). It also states that no exit penalties apply to any of the three plans ('No charges for transfer and/or switching' repeated for all three). The report does not contain any statement on features or guarantees lost — the extraction confirms this path is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument for all three plans). The Quilter arrangement is a Pension Sharing Order receipt (£260,000 current value); True Potential and Nest are personal pensions with transfer values of £46,672.80 and £21,272.64 respectively. The report's switch charges assessment covers True Potential and Nest but not the Quilter PSO.
    File:     P21 (Aviva KFD) warns that transferring from an older company pension scheme could mean losing valuable defined benefit rights or benefits. No provider documentation from True Potential, Nest or the Quilter PSO scheme confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features is present in the retrieved passages. The SAP documents (P16, P22-P24) cover the receiving scheme (Aviva Pension Portfolio) and include performance/early transfer analysis, but do not address features or guarantees on the ceding plans. The Fact Find (P2) notes regarding Nest: 'Nest steps - Tax Free cash withdrawal of £40,000 to cover fees' and 'No change to underlying portfolio', but does not address guarantees. No document in the file confirms whether any of the three ceding plans hold safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, protected tax-free cash or other valuable features. The Quilter PSO is explicitly excluded from the switch charges assessment table per the extraction ambiguity, and no plan-specific cost/benefit analysis for the Quilter PSO appears in the evidence. The SAP documents appear sized on the combined fund (starting value approximately £65,000–£67,684 at 0% growth in year 1 across versions), which is broadly consistent with the True Potential + Nest transfer values of £67,945.44, not the full £327,945.44 including the PSO — suggesting the Quilter PSO may not be fully incorporated in the switch analysis documents on file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report is silent on features or guarantees lost for all three ceding plans; the extraction confirms this is absent from the document (not an extraction failure). No provider documentation corroborating the absence of safeguarded benefits is present in the file.
      - The Quilter PSO (£260,000 current value — the largest single arrangement) has no plan-specific cost/benefit comparison or switch charges analysis in the file. The switch charges assessment in the report covers only True Potential and Nest.
      - The SAP documents on file appear to be sized on the True Potential + Nest transfer values (~£67,945) rather than the full combined fund including the PSO (~£327,945), suggesting the Quilter PSO is not included in the suitability analysis projections, yet it forms the majority of funds under management.
      - The report asserts no employer contributions are lost for all three plans, but no evidence from any of the three providers (particularly Quilter/the PSO source scheme) confirms whether the client was an active member with live employer contributions at the time of transfer.
      - The report's assertion of no exit penalties for all three plans is stated in the report itself; no independent provider confirmation of nil exit penalties appears in the retrieved passages for any of the three plans.
    The primary concerns are: (1) Features and guarantees — The report is silent on what features or guarantees (if any) are lost from the three ceding plans. The extraction confirms this is absent from the document itself, not an extraction failure. No provider documentation from True Potential, Nest or the Quilter PSO source scheme is in the file to confirm that no safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates or protected tax-free cash exist. P21 (Aviva KFD) explicitly warns about this risk for older scheme transfers. The absence of this disclosure is a shortfall on the face of the report and is unmitigated by any file evidence. (2) Quilter PSO — This is the largest arrangement by value (£260,000, approximately 79% of total funds). It does not appear in the switch charges assessment table. The SAP documents are sized on approximately £65,000–£67,945 starting values, consistent with True Potential + Nest only, not the full combined fund. The Quilter arrangement is a PSO receipt rather than a voluntary switch, which could explain its exclusion from the switch charges table (one candidate reading in the extraction ambiguity). However, the PSO funds are apparently being received into the Aviva platform as part of this advice, and the absence of any plan-specific analysis — charges comparison, features lost, guarantees — for the arrangement comprising 79% of the total value is a material gap regardless of the PSO mechanism. (3) Employer contributions — The report asserts none are lost. The employer contributions guard applies: a contribution that stops on leaving service is not lost by the transfer, and it is necessary to check whether the client is still an active member. No evidence in the file addresses active membership status for any of the three schemes. The Fact Find note on Nest ('No change to underlying portfolio') and the paid-up treatment in the charges table suggest Nest is paid up, which is consistent with no active employer contributions. No equivalent confirmation exists for the Quilter source scheme or True Potential. The assertion is self-stated and unCorroborated. (4) Exit penalties — All three plans are stated as having no exit penalties. This is asserted in the report itself; no independent provider documentation confirms nil penalties for any plan. Under the guard, a nil penalty confirmed by the provider and left out of the report is not a finding, but here the nil penalty is only in the report — there is no provider confirmation in the file. This is a weaker concern than the features/guarantees gap but contributes to the overall disclosure shortfall. The most material issues are: the complete absence of any features/guarantees analysis for all three plans (highest severity per guidance), and the absence of any plan-specific analysis for the Quilter PSO which is the largest arrangement. Vulnerability: no vulnerability-specific adjustment to this finding is required — the vulnerability assessment detail was not extracted (NotPresentInDocument) and CHK-010 covers that in its own right.

CHK-010 — Vulnerability, Support Needs and Foreseeable Harm Overlay
Outcome: Potential Concern

  Potential Concern across 5 requirement(s). 5 raised a concern (G10.1, G10.2, G10.3, G10.4, G10.5). The report states the client intends to appoint her daughters as Power of Attorney but no current POA is granted — the file is consistent with this but neither the report nor the file contains any vulnerability assessment or structured FG21/1 consideration despite the presence of FG21/1 drivers (recent divorce as a life event, change in employment status, pending inheritance from deceased mother). No vulnerability assessment detail appears anywhere in the report (confirmed by extraction as NotPresentInDocument) and no vulnerability assessment document is present in the case file, yet the case carries identifiable FG21/1 drivers. The report records no support needs, communication adjustments, or adjustments to the advice process — the file neither contradicts this nor provides evidence that such an assessment was conducted and found none to be needed.

  [G10.1] Details of additional support and needs, including third-party support
  Potential Concern — severity High
    Report:   The report records that the client is aged 55, recently divorced, self-employed, lives with partner Tony (separate finances), has two adult daughters integral to estate planning, and intends to appoint them as Power of Attorney. No Power of Attorney is currently granted (isPowerOfAttorneyGranted: false). No formal vulnerability assessment detail is present in the extracted model.       - No vulnerability assessment detail appears anywhere in the report (confirmed by extraction as NotPresentInDocument) and no vulnerability assessment document is present in the case file, yet the case carries identifiable FG21/1 drivers.
      - The report records no support needs, communication adjustments, or adjustments to the advice process — the file neither contradicts this nor provides evidence that such an assessment was conducted and found none to be needed.
    The trigger is satisfied (hasVulnerabilityIndicators: true). The case carries identifiable FG21/1 drivers: recent divorce (life event), bereavement (late mother, pending inheritance from estate), change in employment status from employed to self-employed, and the client is aged 55 approaching retirement. Under FG21/1 these are recognised drivers that require at least a considered vulnerability check and a record of the outcome. Neither the suitability report nor any supporting document in the pack contains a vulnerability assessment, a record that one was conducted and found no concerns, or any note of communication adjustments or support needs being considered. The absence of a POA is consistent across report and file. The future intention to appoint daughters as POA is a guard-captured point: this is future planning, not current third-party support, and is not treated as a finding. The client's financial literacy (accounting/currency trading background) and proactive engagement are recorded in the meeting summaries and are relevant context, but they do not substitute for a recorded FG21/1 vulnerability check: capability is one dimension and life-event-driven vulnerability is another. The file is silent — not contradictory — on support needs, but the absence of any vulnerability assessment in a case with clear FG21/1 drivers is a process risk and a disclosure shortfall. Severity is High: there are multiple concurrent FG21/1 drivers and no assessment anywhere.

  [G10.2] Communication preferences and formats
  Potential Concern — severity Low
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the communication preferences path (/vulnerability/perClient[]/communicationPreferences).   [G10.3] Adjustments made to support client comprehension
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The suitability report records two vulnerability-adjacent characteristics: (1) recently divorced with a Pension Sharing Order (significant life event) and (2) recently bereaved (late mother, pending inheritance). The report notes the client has a background in accounting and currency trading and describes her as having a fair degree of knowledge and understanding. The canonical model extraction flags /vulnerability/perClient[]/adjustmentsMade[] as absent from the extracted model, with the extraction's own log recording the path /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail as NotPresentInDocument. The report therefore asserts two life-event vulnerability drivers but records no explicit vulnerability assessment and no adjustments made to support client comprehension.
    File:     The case file (meeting summaries [P8], [P15], [P6]) corroborates the divorce and bereavement circumstances. [P8] notes the client is 'a seasoned professional with a background in accounting and currency trading' and that she 'recognise[s] the value of professional advice'. [P15] records the divorce and pension-sharing order process. [P6] records bereavement context (inheritance from late mother's estate, complicated by legal proceedings). The fact find [B] passages are largely blank on vulnerability, communication preferences and support needs. No passage in any category records a formal vulnerability assessment, any adjustment to communication format, language, pace or medium, any accessible format provision, any third-party or carer involvement, or any specific measure taken to support comprehension in light of the two recorded life events. The meeting summaries record practical administrative steps (letter of authority, paper-based Quilter process) but nothing framed as a vulnerability-driven adjustment.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report records two FG21/1 life-event vulnerability drivers (divorce and bereavement) but no vulnerability assessment is present anywhere in the file.
      - The report is silent on any adjustments made to support client comprehension; no adjustment record exists in the file either.
      - No communication preference, accessible format or language requirement is recorded in either the report or any supporting document.
      - No third-party involvement, trusted contact, Power of Attorney or family support arrangement is recorded or ruled out in either the report or the file despite the client being recently bereaved and divorced.
      - The fact find vulnerability/support-needs fields are blank; no alternative record compensates for this.
    Two clear FG21/1 life-event drivers are present: divorce with a Pension Sharing Order and bereavement. Under FCA FG21/1 these are recognised triggers requiring the adviser to consider whether the client may be temporarily or permanently vulnerable and to record the outcome of that consideration, together with any adjustments made. The report itself acknowledges the circumstances but the extraction confirms that /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail is NotPresentInDocument — meaning the report contains no formal vulnerability assessment section, not merely that extraction failed to read one. No passage in categories B, C or H records a vulnerability screening outcome, a decision that no adjustment was needed, or any positive step taken to adapt the advice process. The client's accounting background and stated financial literacy are noted and may inform a judgment that no additional support was needed, but that judgment is never made explicit and is not a substitute for a recorded assessment. The absence is therefore a process gap: the drivers were captured, the assessment was not conducted or not recorded, and no adjustments (or reasoned decision that none were needed) appear anywhere in the file. This is a Moderate finding: support needs associated with two recorded life events are not reflected in any communication or adjustment record, which falls short of the Consumer Duty obligations under PRIN 2A.2.1R, PRIN 2A.5 and FCA FG21/1.

  [G10.4] Vulnerability has a material impact on the recommendation (overlay)
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] or /vulnerability/foreseeableHarms[]. The extraction log confirms these paths are absent because they are NotPresentInDocument. The suitability report does record: (a) the client is aged 55 and recently divorced; (b) she intends to appoint her two adult daughters as Power of Attorney (not yet in place); (c) capacity for loss is assessed as moderate; (d) attitude to risk is 7/10 (Growth); (e) knowledge and experience described as medium/fair. No section of the report explicitly addresses how any of these characteristics — individually or in combination — materially affects the recommendation, nor does it document any foreseeable harms arising from the client's circumstances.
    File:     The case file contains no standalone vulnerability assessment document. The risk profile report [P1, P9, P14] records the ATR process and capacity for loss questionnaire but all the free-text discussion fields ('No additional notes were recorded') are blank. The meeting summaries [P12, P21] describe the client as a financially capable professional with an accounting and currency trading background. No FG21/1 vulnerability indicators — health concerns, bereavement distress, cognitive impairment, financial abuse risk — are flagged in any document. The client is noted as recently divorced [P6, P12], expecting a significant inheritance from her late mother's estate [P6], and transitioning from employed to self-employed [P6]. The Power of Attorney intention is recorded in the suitability report [P6] but no third-party authority is in place and no file document records it as a vulnerability-related measure. The capacity for loss section of the suitability report [P16] notes moderate capacity but no document in the file links this to a foreseeable harm analysis or a vulnerability overlay. The extraction log confirms /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail is NotPresentInDocument.
    Discrepancies:
      - The suitability report records an intention to appoint daughters as Power of Attorney [P6] but no impact-on-recommendation analysis is present anywhere in the report or file in connection with this.
      - The report notes the client is recently divorced and has experienced a bereavement (late mother's estate pending) — two life-event drivers under FG21/1 — but no vulnerability overlay or foreseeable-harm section appears in either the report or supporting documents.
      - The risk profile discussion fields that would capture vulnerability-related notes are all blank [P9], meaning the assessment process did not generate any recorded consideration of vulnerability.
      - The canonical model paths /vulnerability/perClient[]/impactOnRecommendation[] and /vulnerability/foreseeableHarms[] are absent from the report (confirmed NotPresentInDocument by extraction log), yet the trigger is satisfied and the case carries FG21/1 life-event indicators.
    The check trigger is satisfied: hasVulnerabilityIndicators = true, and the file contains clear FG21/1 life-event drivers — recent divorce, bereavement (late mother's estate), and a change of employment status all occurring in close proximity. Under FG21/1 these are recognised drivers that require a vulnerability assessment to be conducted and documented, and where characteristics are found (or even ruled out), the adviser must record how they were considered in the suitability determination.
    
    The suitability report does contain some relevant detail: it notes the client's age (55), the Power of Attorney intention, moderate capacity for loss, and a fair level of financial knowledge. However none of these are framed as a vulnerability assessment, and none of the material feeds into an explicit impact-on-recommendation or foreseeable-harm analysis. The report's capacity-for-loss section [P16] is the closest the document comes to this, but it is presented purely as a risk-tolerance calibration rather than a Consumer Duty vulnerability overlay.
    
    The risk profile report's free-text discussion fields [P9] — Attitude to Risk, Investor Experience, Capacity for Loss, Agreed Risk Level — are all blank ('No additional notes were recorded'). This means the tool that would ordinarily capture vulnerability-related observations produced no output on this case.
    
    The Power of Attorney point deserves separate attention. The report states the client 'intends to appoint' her daughters [P6], meaning no authority is currently in place. This is recorded as a factual circumstance, not as a support measure or vulnerability consideration. No document in the file records any discussion of what this means for the client's current ability to act independently, or what steps have been taken in the interim.
    
    The guards applied: age alone is not vulnerability, and the client's financial background (accountancy, currency trading) supports capability. These factors reduce the severity of the absence. However, the combination of divorce, bereavement and employment change — all active at the time of advice — constitutes a multi-driver FG21/1 profile that required at least a documented assessment of whether vulnerability characteristics were present, even if the conclusion was that none applied. That assessment is absent from every document in the file. The claim that vulnerability was considered is therefore unsupported.

  [G10.5] Vulnerabilities recorded in the file and in the report
  Potential Concern — severity Moderate
    Report:   The report records hasVulnerability as 'Possible' under category 'LifeEvents', with two inferred characteristics: (1) the client is recently divorced with a Pension Sharing Order — quoted as 'You are currently aged 55 and recently divorced, hence the Pension Sharing Order'; and (2) the client has recently lost her mother and is expecting an inheritance from the sale of her late mother's property — quoted as 'You are also expecting to receive an inheritance of £350,000 from the sale of your late mothers property which is currently pending legal proceedings.' Both are noted as inferred rather than explicitly assessed, and no formal vulnerability assessment date or detail is recorded. No support needs, communication adjustments, third-party authority, or specific measures taken to support informed decision-making are referenced in the report.
    File:     Multiple meeting summaries corroborate both life-event drivers. P7 (Meeting Summary with Jack Davidson, 16/01/2026) records that the client is 'currently in the process of managing your financial affairs following your recent divorce' and that the pension transfer is a 'crucial step in ensuring your financial stability and independence post-divorce.' P2 (Meeting Summary with Jack, 20/02/2026) describes the client's background and confirms the post-divorce context. P9/P10 (Meeting Summary, 21/07/2026) records that the client has recently left employment, is in a 'transitional phase', is anticipating an inheritance of approximately £150,000 from her mother's estate 'which is currently complicated by legal challenges involving her mother's ex-partner', and references her role as a grandmother. P11 (Meeting Summary with Jack Davidson) notes that the divorce may have caused a 'shift in financial priorities and risk tolerance' and advises a comprehensive risk profile review. P4 (Meeting Summary (2)) records that the risk profile was assessed at level 7 and discusses investment horizon. The Client Agreement at P19 states that reasonable adjustments are available and that communication preferences can be changed at any time, but records no specific adjustments made for this client. The fact find passages (P1, P5, P12, P14) are largely blank on vulnerability-specific fields. No formal vulnerability assessment document, screening record, or assessment date appears in the file. The search Q10.5.2 returned nothing. Notably, P10 records the inheritance expectation as approximately £150,000 (with a property valued at £1.5–1.6 million subject to legal challenge), whereas the report states £350,000 — a discrepancy in the inheritance figure, though this is a separate matter. No Power of Attorney, trusted contact, or third-party authority record is in the file.
    Discrepancies:
      - The report asserts vulnerability as 'Possible' with two life-event characteristics (divorce, bereavement), but records no formal vulnerability assessment, screening date, or assessment detail — the extraction confirms /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail is NotPresentInDocument and no such document appears in the file.
      - The report records no support needs, communication adjustments, or measures taken to support informed decision-making despite two concurrent life-event drivers (divorce and bereavement) that FCA FG21/1 identifies as vulnerability drivers requiring active consideration.
      - The file (P10) records the anticipated inheritance as approximately £150,000, whereas the report states £350,000 — the figures differ materially, though the inheritance amount is peripheral to the vulnerability assessment itself.
      - No third-party authority, Power of Attorney, or trusted contact record appears anywhere in the file or report, and the report does not address whether these were considered given the client's circumstances.
      - The client's situation at the July 2026 meeting (P9/P10) — recently left employment, financially stressed, legal challenges around the estate, role as grandmother — represents additional vulnerability context that the report does not reflect beyond the two originally recorded characteristics.
    The report correctly identifies divorce and bereavement as life-event vulnerability drivers and records hasVulnerability as 'Possible', which is consistent with what the file shows. Both characteristics are corroborated by the meeting summaries (P7, P2, P9/P10). However, the requirement is not fully met for the following reasons. First, neither the report nor the file contains any formal vulnerability assessment record, screening date, or documented outcome — the extraction flags /clientAuthority/vulnerabilityAssessment/detail as NotPresentInDocument and no equivalent document appears in the retrieved passages. The characteristics are inferred from circumstantial disclosure, not from a structured assessment. Under FCA FG21/1, identifying a driver is the beginning of the assessment process, not the end. Second, the report records no support needs, communication adjustments, accessible format requirements or measures taken to support informed decision-making, despite two concurrent significant life events. The Client Agreement (P19) references the availability of reasonable adjustments generically but records none as having been made for this client. Third, the July 2026 meeting (P9/P10) records additional context — the client had recently left employment, was financially stressed with debts of £20,000, was dealing with an ongoing legal dispute over the estate, and was a grandmother — none of which is reflected in the report's vulnerability section. This is not merely a historic or resolved characteristic; the meeting post-dates the suitability report's fact-find meetings and adds contemporaneous vulnerability context. The guard regarding resolved or historic vulnerabilities does not apply here because the July 2026 meeting shows the stressors were ongoing. The inheritance figure discrepancy (£150,000 in the file vs £350,000 in the report) is noted but is not the primary vulnerability finding. The core finding is that the report identifies the drivers but goes no further: it does not document what assessment was conducted, what conclusion was reached, what adjustments (if any) were made, or how the vulnerability characteristics were factored into the recommendation — all of which FCA FG21/1 requires. This is a disclosure shortfall and a missing evidence concern at moderate severity: the drivers are present and partially acknowledged, but the assessment process and its outputs are absent from both the report and the file.
